## D86 RPC settle tail and command admission: a handler of a runtime that cannot report its wait is taken to wait once it outlives a quantum

What: in RPC mode Pi emits `agent_settled` in the microtasks that follow a published `agent_end`, so its stdin reader, which runs between macrotasks, reads no line between them (`agent-session.ts:1044-1058,1752-1777`, `rpc-mode.ts:355-360,801-808,833`). When an `agent_before_settle` or `agent_settled` handler, or an extension command, awaits a timer, I/O or another real wait, Pi reads stdin while it waits (`rpc-mode.ts:801-808,833`: `activeCommands++`, the `finally` that decrements it, and `void handleInputLine(line)` start every line without waiting for the one before). PiG reads stdin on its own goroutine, so `rpcSettleGate` holds each stdin batch, and stdin's end, from a published `agent_end` until `agent_settled` is written, and `rpcAwaitHandler` admits the next line only once a command, an input handler or a before_agent_start handler suspended or finished. The gate is open during a compaction, an auto-retry delay, a new run, an extension dialog and a settle-tail handler that waits. A Node runtime reports the end of its event-loop window for a command, an input handler, a before_agent_start handler and the settle tail: a window that closes with the handler unanswered is the handler awaiting a timer, I/O or another real wait, and is reported as blocked, which releases `rpcAwaitHandler`; a handler that only runs microtasks never closes its window unanswered, so it holds stdin as in Pi. The host sees a Node handler's waits exactly. A Go, Rust, Python or Lisp handler blocks in the language's own call (`net/http`, a socket read, a sleep), which nothing but the language tells from computing, so the host takes such a handler to be waiting once it has run for `subprocess.HandlerWaitQuantum` (250 ms) without answering: it acknowledges the invocation as suspended, which admits the next stdin line, and counts a settle-tail handler as waiting, which opens the gate. A handler that answers within the quantum is Pi's microtask-only continuation and holds stdin as before. After stdin's end, a running handler of such a runtime counts as waiting at once, so shutdown does not wait for it.

Why: the host learns of a wait only from a runtime's report, and those runtimes have no equivalent of the Node window that closes unresponded. Without a bound, a Go handler that awaits the network (the LangSmith extension's `agent_settled` PATCH with `LANGSMITH_TRACING_BACKGROUND=false`) left every later stdin line unanswered for as long as the wait lasted, where the same handler in Node answers `get_state` during it. Without the gate, a command sent on seeing `agent_end` would be answered before `agent_settled`, which Pi never does for a handler that does not wait.

Rejected options (each surveyed against the failing case, a plain `net/http` or socket wait in an `agent_settled` handler or command):
- Waits reported only where an SDK mediates them (the `blocked` report of a host call, as the Node runtime reports `WINDOW_CLOSING_CALLS`): exact for `ctx.exec`, dialogs and model calls, blind to the stdlib I/O that caused the report; the ported LangSmith client dials with a plain `*http.Transport`.
- Instrumenting the language's I/O (`httptrace` on the handler's context, wrapping `http.DefaultTransport`, patching `socket`): covers one client of one language; a custom transport, a raw socket, a sleep or a file read escapes it, and Rust and Lisp have no equivalent hook.
- Reading the language scheduler (Go `runtime/metrics` goroutine states, an `asyncio` idle loop): the Go counters are documented as approximate and process-wide (a fused extension shares the host's goroutines), Rust and Lisp expose nothing, and every reading needs a sampling interval anyway.
- Polling the extension process's thread states (`/proc/<pid>/task`, `libproc`): platform-specific, still needs an interval, and impossible for a fused extension, which shares the host process.
- Dropping the gate for these runtimes: removes the deterministic order for a handler that does not wait, because the host handles a command as soon as it is read while the handler runs.
- A longer bound (the 2 s heartbeat interval): the same behavior with a client stall of seconds.

Value of the quantum: a no-op `agent_settled` handler's request to response took 0.2 to 0.8 ms at the median, at most 3.1 ms at the 99th percentile and at most 8.7 ms at the maximum in 150 runs per SDK (Go, Rust, Python, Lisp, Node; xd670, idle and with twice as many busy loops as CPUs). 250 ms is about 29 times the slowest run, and bounds the delay a waiting handler adds to the first line it holds back. `TestRPCSettleHandlerLatencyProbe` in `coding/cli/rpc_settle_latency_probe_test.go` repeats the measurement (`PIG_PROBE_SETTLE_LATENCY=1`).

Value of the quantum for the two events Pi emits with `void emit` (`session_info_changed`, `thinking_level_select`; RPC `set_session_name`, `set_thinking_level`): the reply of the command waits for the first handler's first suspension (`Session.dispatchUnawaitedEvent`), so the quantum is the longest a reply is held by a Go, Rust, Python or Lisp handler that waits or never returns (Pi replies in under 1 ms); the same 250 ms is kept, one constant for every use of the record. Measured at 25, 50, 100, 250 and 1000 ms (50 calls per cell, xd6702 container): a waiting handler holds the reply for the quantum plus 1 ms at the median and at the 95th percentile in every SDK; a handler that computes for 50 ms keeps Pi's order (its notification first) only at a quantum above 50 ms; a handler that computes for 700 ms writes its notification after the reply at every quantum below 700 ms. `TestRPCUnawaitedEventLatencyProbe` in `coding/cli/rpc_unawaited_event_test.go` repeats the measurement (`PIG_PROBE_NAME_LATENCY=1`).

Observable effect for those two events: in the Go, Rust, Python or Lisp SDK, `set_session_name` and `set_thinking_level` reply up to 250 ms after the request while an extension handler of the event waits or never returns, where Pi replies at once. A handler whose wait ends at once writes its notification before the reply, where Pi writes it after; a handler that computes for more than 250 ms without awaiting writes its notification after the reply, where Pi, whose caller the computation blocks, writes it first. The handler's effect is neither lost nor dropped: it arrives, late or reordered. Node handlers report the end of their synchronous part and are as in Pi.

Observable effect: in the Go, Rust, Python or Lisp SDK, a command sent during the first 250 ms of an `agent_before_settle` or `agent_settled` handler that waits on a timer or I/O is answered after `agent_settled` instead of before it, and a stdin line sent during the first 250 ms of a command, input or before_agent_start handler that waits is answered after that handler instead of before it; later in the wait both match Pi. A handler that computes for more than 250 ms without waiting is taken to wait, so a command sent meanwhile is answered during it, where Pi, whose loop the computation blocks, answers it afterwards. Output order for every other case, including every Node handler (command, input, before_agent_start and settle tail: a line sent during its wait is answered during it, at once), is as in Pi. The quantum does not apply to Node runtimes, which report their waits.

Scope: only a settle-tail handler, command, input or before_agent_start handler of a runtime that reports no window, and only a command or stdin end sent while it runs; and the first handler of a `session_info_changed` or `thinking_level_select` event of such a runtime, and only for the reply of the command that emitted it. Outside that case the gate matches Pi and is not waived.

Owner decision: 2026-10-01, owner Michael Kinsy approves the gate holding stdin through such a handler as a temporary divergence ("Yes go with recommendations", in the lead session). The 250 ms quantum that bounds the hold (ticket `fix/071-settle-block`; the owner's standing instruction was to fix a Go handler that blocks `get_state` at the root and to record the rejected options) is approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-11-07.

Owner decision: 2026-11-17, owner Michael Kinsy approves the extension of this record's scope to the first handler of `session_info_changed` and `thinking_level_select` (the two events Pi emits with `void emit`) with the 250 ms quantum, on the measured proposal `ROW30-QUANTUM-MEASURED.md` ("go with you recommended", relayed by the coordinator; recorded in `handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md` item 4 of the 2026-11-17T00:53Z decisions). The decision above of 2026-10-01 and 2026-11-07 covers the settle tail, commands and input handlers.

Owner decision: 2026-11-17 (decision 28, 15:08Z), owner Michael Kinsy: the known gap this record carried, a Node input handler that awaits I/O holding the next stdin line, is cured in the runtime, not waived (see What: the same report now covers a Node before_agent_start handler and a Node command).

Scope extension: 2026-11-17T15:39Z, Claude under the owner's delegation (decision 28; row 30 precedent) extends this record's scope and the 250 ms quantum to the before_agent_start handler of the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp runtimes.

Call-site markers: `coding/cli/rpc_settle_gate.go`: `rpcSettleGate`; `coding/extension/host/subprocess/conn.go`: `HandlerWaitQuantum`, `withRequestQuantum`; `coding/extension/host/subprocess/host.go`: `makeEventHandler`, `makeCommandHandler`, `unawaitedFirstSuspension`; `coding/session.go`: `dispatchUnawaitedEvent`; `coding/cli/rpc_admission.go`: `rpcAwaitHandler`; `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/runtime.mjs`: `armHandlerWaitWindow`, `armRequestWindow`.

Evidence: `TestRPCCommandDuringSettleHandlerNetworkWaitAnswersAcrossSDKs` and `TestRPCCommandDuringCommandNetworkWaitAnswersAcrossSDKs` in `coding/cli/rpc_settle_wait_test.go` (Pi, PiG with Node, Go, Rust, Python and Lisp handlers that wait on a socket), `TestRPCCommandDuringInputHandlerNetworkWaitAnswersAcrossSDKs` (an input handler that waits on a socket; Pi, Node isolated and packed, Go, Rust, Python and Lisp), `TestRPCCommandDuringBeforeAgentStartHandlerNetworkWaitAnswersAcrossSDKs` (the same seven rows for a before_agent_start handler), `TestRPCNodeHandlerThatOnlyRunsMicrotasksHoldsStdin` (input and command, Pi and Node: a handler that only runs microtasks holds stdin), `TestRPCNodeInputHandlerWaitSemanticsComparedWithPi` (two lines keep their order, a throw after the wait, an abort during the wait) and `TestRPCNodeInputEndDuringInputHandlerWaitComparedWithPi`, `TestRPCCommandAfterAgentEndAnswersAfterFastSettleHandlerAcrossSDKs` (a handler that does not wait holds stdin in every SDK, as in Pi), `TestConformance_WaitingHandlerIsSuspended` in `test/extension-conformance/wait_quantum_conformance_test.go` (isolated, packed and fused realizations), `TestHandlerOutlivingTheQuantumIsSuspended`, `TestHandlerAnsweringWithinTheQuantumIsNotSuspended` and `TestRequestWithoutTheQuantumSetsNoTimer` in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/handler_quantum_test.go`, `TestInteractiveEditorStaysLiveWhileSettleHandlerOrCommandWaits` in `test/integration/settle_wait_interactive_test.go`; `TestRPCCommandAfterAgentEndAnswersAfterAgentSettledComparedWithPi`, `TestRPCCommandDuringSuspendedSettleTailAnswersComparedWithPi` and `TestRPCInputEndDuringSuspendedSettleTailShutsDownComparedWithPi` in `coding/cli/rpc_input_end_order_test.go` (Pi and PiG for Node handlers), `TestRPCInputEndDuringGoSDKSettleTailHandlerShutsDown` in `coding/cli/rpc_shutdown_drain_go_test.go`, `TestRPCSettleGate*` in `coding/cli/rpc_settle_gate_test.go` and `TestSettleTailCounts*` in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/command_window_test.go`.

Evidence for the two events: `TestRPCUnawaitedEventAcrossSDKs` in `coding/cli/rpc_unawaited_event_test.go` (`ReplyDoesNotWaitForAHandler`: Pi and PiG with Node, Go, Rust, Python and Lisp handlers that wait on a socket, both commands; `ShortWaitOrder` and `ComputeOrder` pin the two orders declared above) and `TestConformance_UnawaitedEventDoesNotWaitForAWaitingHandler` in `test/extension-conformance/unawaited_event_wait_conformance_test.go` (isolated, packed and fused realizations).

Parity allowance: the Pi comparison rows run the TypeScript fixture, whose handlers wait and answer exactly as Pi's do; the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp rows assert Pi's observable order for the same wait.

Remove when: the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp runtimes report the end of a handler's own wait exactly, as the Node runtime does with its window, or PiG runs extensions on one shared event loop as Pi does.

## D87 PiG's versions of Pi's easter eggs

What: Pi has two easter eggs that show Pi's art. PiG plays them with its own art. Pi 1.4.0's `easter-egg-3d.ts` plays both the logo and, in fullscreen mode, a 3D Armin for `/arminsayshi`; PiG ports it with PiG's models. Its logo path draws exactly what the `pi-logo-animation.ts` of Pi's 1.0.0 release drew (lift-off cells, start scale, dust center and timeline are unchanged), so the header click keeps its pig behavior.
1. Clicking the header logo in fullscreen mode (`interactive-mode.ts:1111`, `easter-egg-3d.lazy.ts` `playPiLogo3d`, `easter-egg-3d.ts`; Pi 1.0.0 `pi-logo-animation.lazy.ts` and `pi-logo-animation.ts`). The clickable logo is PiG's pig head (D2): cells 1 to 16 of the header's first seven lines, where Pi's logo takes cells 1 to 4 of two, or the 4 cells of the one-line `PiG.` text mark where it stands in for Pi's logo; Apple Terminal has no clickable logo, as Pi's text wordmark has none. The object that flies, grows and spins is that head, built from the active sprite's head pixels and colors (`/sprite`), not Pi's three-color logo. Where Pi's logo shuffles its blocks as a sliding puzzle (from 4.4 s, one 5.2 s cycle at a time), the spinning head gives way for the 3.6 s of Pi's shuffle to a side-view running pig, 38 pixels by 22, ray cast in braille dots with the head's shading and blocks the size of the head's, so it is more than twice the head's width: rounded body, ears, eye, snout with nostrils, cheek, curly tail and dark outline, four legs in a four-frame run cycle with a one-pixel bob. It runs in place where the head spins. In the first 0.6 s of the run the head's braille dots dissolve one by one into the pig's while the pig turns from the head's spin to face the camera, and in the last 0.6 s the pig dissolves back into the head the same way while it turns back to the head's spin, so no frame is empty or a hard swap; Esc during the run dissolves the pig back into the head over 0.6 s while Pi's exit plays. The head is back, whole and spinning, for Pi's 1 s return and 0.6 s hold. The ray caster holds up to 65535 faces where Pi's holds 255 (`Uint8Array`), because the running pig has more, and renders the head and the pig as two layers during a dissolve. The running pig takes the active sprite's colors: a color the head draws keeps its color, a body the head does not draw as `P` is the head's most common color (Kratos's skin, Spider-Ham's suit), a body too dark for a dark outline is outlined in its highlight (Vader), and characters keep accessories (the Sheriff's hat and star, Vader's red panel, Kratos's tattoo, Piglet's shirt, Spider-Ham's web, PiGrogu's robe). The dissolve, its timing and dust, the 3D ray caster and shading, the light-background halo, the starfield, the `escape to return` hint, the spin locked to the cycle, the reverse exit, the keys (`tui.select.cancel` and `app.clear`, a second press skips the exit) and the mouse handling are Pi's.
2. `/arminsayshi` (`interactive-mode.ts:handleArminSaysHi`, `armin.ts`) draws a 31 by 34 pig head labeled `pigsayhi` instead of Armin's 31 by 36 image labeled `ARMIN SAYS HI`. The seven effects, their random selection and order of random calls, and their timing are Pi's. `/pigsayhi` is a second name for the same easter egg; Pi sends `/pigsayhi` to the model as an ordinary prompt.

3. In fullscreen mode, `/arminsayshi` and `/pigsayhi` play the 3D pig (`interactive-mode.ts:handleArminSaysHi`, `easter-egg-3d.lazy.ts` `playArmin3d`, `easter-egg-3d.ts` `arminModel`). The only change is the model: Pi's bitmap is Armin's 31 by 36 portrait in the theme's accent color; PiG's is the active sprite's 16 by 14 head in its colors, one block per pixel extruded to Pi's depth. Pi's camera distance (80), width share (0.45), growth from a speck at the center, dust from the center, sliding puzzle (moves per step, candidate order, preference for blocks that did not just move, the flight home and the hold), spin, starfield, hint, halo, exit and keys are Pi's. Outside fullscreen mode both commands draw the inline pig head, as Pi draws the inline Armin, and with another overlay open they do nothing, as Pi does.

`/dementedelves` keeps Pi's announcement, which still says "pi has joined Earendil" (`earendil-announcement.ts`). Pi 1.4.0 has no OpenCode Kimi easter egg (`daxnuts.ts`); PiG never ported it.

Why: PiG never presents itself as Pi (D2). Pi's logo animation and Armin's portrait are Pi's art; the owner wants PiG's own versions of them.

Owner decision: 2026-10-01, owner Michael Kinsy ("PiG versions of Pi's eggs").
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 4 re-approval as recommended, checked by Claude and Tern

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/pig_egg3d.go` (`pigLogoModel`, `pig3dModel`), `internal/codingagent/pig_logo_animation.go` (the object, `pigLogoBlocks`), `internal/codingagent/pig_logo_run.go` (the running pig), `internal/codingagent/startup_header.go` (`handleBuiltInHeaderMouse`), `internal/codingagent/armin.go` (the image and label) and `internal/codingagent/startup_input.go` (`/pigsayhi`).

## D88 PiG runs its own first-time setup with a sprite step

What: Pi 1.4.0 shows its first-time setup dialog (`first-time-setup.ts`: theme with live preview, then analytics opt-in) only for its official distribution with `PI_EXPERIMENTAL=1`, the default agent directory and no `settings.json` (`startup-ui.ts:131-148`, `main.ts:723-727`). PiG shows it on an interactive start in the default agent directory that holds no `settings.json` yet, which includes every fresh `PIG_HOME`. As in Pi, a custom agent directory (`PIG_CODING_AGENT_DIR`) skips it (`startup-ui.ts:144-146`). Print, JSON and RPC modes never show it. Esc skips setup and saves nothing.

The dialog is a port of Pi's component with three steps: theme (System, Dark, Light, with live preview), then sprite, then analytics. The sprite step lists the fifteen built-in sprites in a scrolling window of eight rows with the position below it, then a last item, `Create your own...`. The dialog's logo is the pig head of the highlighted sprite on the sprite step and of the chosen sprite after it, never Pi's `SETUP_LOGO_LINES`. The welcome line says `Welcome to PiG` where Pi's says `Welcome to pi`, and the analytics text names PiG instead of Pi (D2). Finishing saves the theme and analytics choice in `settings.json` as Pi does, and saves the sprite exactly as `/sprite set` does (`$PIG_HOME/state/pig-standard/login.json`). `Create your own...` shows how to create a sprite (`/login`, `/model`, then `/sprite create`), and finishing with it keeps the active sprite. `/sprite create` sends a guided turn that writes a TypeScript extension registering the sprite with `ctx.ui.registerSprite` when a model with credentials exists, and explains `/login` otherwise.

PiG shows the dialog as Pi does: on its own startup screen before runtime services start, after migrations and before session selection (`main.ts:716-727`). Extensions are not loaded yet, so the sprite step offers only the built-in sprites; extension sprites are chosen later with `/sprite`. The gate is observed before startup writes any file.

Why: PiG never presents itself as Pi (D2), so Pi's official-only setup never runs for PiG; the owner wants a first-run setup that also chooses the sprite.

Owner decision: 2026-10-02, owner Michael Kinsy (first-run-sprite, release 0.4.0).
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

Call-site markers: `coding/cli/main.go` (gate observation), `coding/cli/extensions.go` (`shouldRunFirstTimeSetup`), `internal/codingagent/first_time_setup.go` (`FirstTimeSetupComponent`, the welcome line, `ShowFirstTimeSetup`).

Evidence: `TestShouldRunFirstTimeSetupGate` (interactive with and without `settings.json`, print, piped stdin, JSON, RPC), `TestFirstTimeSetupOriginalCasesAsFork` and `TestForkedDistributionDoesNotBlockOnOfficialFirstTimeSetup` (Pi's original gate inputs against PiG's real CLI), `TestFirstTimeSetupEscSkipsOnInteractiveStart` and `TestFirstTimeSetupSavesThemeSpriteAndAnalytics` (real CLI in a PTY), `TestFirstTimeSetupSpriteStepListsExtensionSpritesAndSubmits`, `TestFirstTimeSetupKeepsTheHighlightedSpriteThroughAnalyticsAndSubmit`, `TestShowFirstTimeSetupSavesTheChoiceBeforeTheTUI`, `TestShowFirstTimeSetupSkipSavesNothing`, `TestFirstTimeSetupHintUsesLowercaseKeyText`, `TestSpriteCreate`, `TestSpritePickerCreateYourOwnShowsTheHint`, `TestFirstTimeSetupCreateYourOwnShowsTheHintAndKeepsTheSprite`, `TestFirstTimeSetupThemePreviewAndEscSkip` and `TestFirstTimeSetupLogoIsThePigHeadAndPreviewsTheSprite`. Pi's analytics cases in `first-time-setup.test.ts` stay ported unchanged (`TestAnalyticsSettings`).

Parity allowance: Pi never shows the dialog for a fork, and parity fixtures carry `settings.json`, so no paired scenario compares it.

Protects: Pi's first-time-setup cases check when the setup dialog appears (its gate: the official distribution, `PI_EXPERIMENTAL=1`, the default agent directory and no `settings.json`) and what Esc and each step save.

PiG-shaped test considered: Pi's gate inputs run against PiG's real CLI (`TestFirstTimeSetupOriginalCasesAsFork`, `TestForkedDistributionDoesNotBlockOnOfficialFirstTimeSetup`), PiG's own gate `TestShouldRunFirstTimeSetupGate`, and PTY runs of the real CLI for Esc and for saving the theme, sprite and analytics choice. Running Pi's component test unchanged was considered.

Why not: Pi's cases expect the dialog only for Pi's official distribution; PiG shows it in every default agent directory without `settings.json` and adds a sprite step, so the same inputs cannot give the same results. The PiG-shaped tests assert the approved gate and steps.

Remove when: never; the first-run setup is PiG's.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:4be01b23b1aafffb

## D89 Tool renderer resolvers of subprocess extensions answer once per tool, off the UI loop, and cannot wrap next()

What: Pi 1.4.0's `pi.registerToolRenderer()` resolvers run synchronously whenever a tool card is created (`interactive-mode.ts:getRegisteredToolDefinition`, `runner.ts:resolveToolRenderers`), and `next()` lazily runs the remaining resolvers and returns the registered tool's renderer functions, which a resolver may wrap. A PiG extension in another process (Node, Go, Python and Rust SDKs, isolated or packed) cannot be called on the UI loop. PiG therefore:
1. Evaluates `next()` before asking the extension and sends what it draws (`renderShell`, whether `renderCall` and `renderResult` exist). The SDK's `next()` returns a marker for those renderers. A resolver that returns the marker keeps them; the marker's render functions are absent, so a resolver cannot call or wrap them. A marker or a copy of it that the resolver gives a call or result renderer is the resolver's own renderers: `next()`'s `renderShell` with only the render functions the resolver set.
2. Asks each extension once per tool, on a host goroutine. Until it answers, the card draws with `next()`'s renderers; an answer other than `next()`'s renderers then draws the tool's cards again, starting their renderer state over. The answer is kept until the extension reloads, restarts, recovers or registers another resolver.
In-process Go extensions, including the built-in MCP extension, resolve exactly as Pi does.

Why: AGENTS.md forbids blocking extension IPC on the TUI loop, and a renderer function cannot cross the process boundary. Owner decision: 2026-10-03, owner Michael Kinsy (port-101 Option A: full SDK support with this design).

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/tool_renderer_resolvers.go` (`makeToolRendererResolver`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/runtime.mjs` (`resolveToolRenderers`), `extensions/sdk/tool_renderer_resolvers.go` (`ToolRendererResolver`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` (`ToolRendererResolver`), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/tool_render.rs` (`ToolRendererSet`).

Evidence: `TestToolRendererResolversAcrossSDKs` (test/extension-conformance: every SDK, isolated and packed, against the in-process reference: own renderers drawn by the extension, none, `next() ?? own`, a `next()` copy given a call renderer in each SDK's idiom, unrelated tools deferred), `TestToolRendererRegisteredAfterLoadingAcrossSDKs` (a resolver registered by a command), `TestReapplyToolPresentationDrawsCardsWithALaterResolution` and `TestReapplyToolPresentationReturnsACardWithoutRenderersToThePlainCard` (a later answer draws the card again), `TestToolRendererAnswerOfNextDoesNotRedrawCards`, `TestToolRendererResolverSurvivesARuntimeToolRegistration`, and the in-process `TestUpstreamRunnerResolvesToolRenderersInExtensionLoadOrderEachAbleToDeferToTheNext` and `TestRegression10285*` tests.

Parity allowance: Pi's own resolvers run in its single process; no paired scenario loads a third-party resolver extension.

Remove when: PiG can run an extension's resolver synchronously without IPC on the UI loop and pass renderer functions across processes.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:92890ede7b8bfe77

## D90 llama.cpp reasoning models offer every budgeted thinking level

What: Pi's built-in llama.cpp provider (`extensions/llama/provider.ts` `toPiModel`, Pi 1.4.0) marks a model as reasoning when its chat template reads `enable_thinking`, and maps only the `off` and `medium` thinking levels. Its `qwen-chat-template` request sends `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking` and no budget, so every level other than `off` asks for the same unbounded thinking. PiG maps `off`, `minimal`, `low`, `medium` and `high` and sets `compat.thinkingTokenBudgetField` to `thinking_budget_tokens`, so each level sends Pi's budget for that level (1024, 2048, 8192 and 16384 tokens, or `thinkingBudgets`, clamped to leave answer room) with `enable_thinking`. `xhigh` stays unsupported because Pi's budgets give it the `high` budget. `off` sends `enable_thinking: false` and no budget, as Pi does.

Why: GitHub issue #129. Pi has the same bug and Pi 1.4.0 does not fix it. llama-server applies a per-request `thinking_budget_tokens` when its command line sets no reasoning budget; an older server ignores the field and thinks as Pi's request asks. Owner task decision: 2026-10-04 (port-102: implement the fix ahead of upstream and record it).

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/llama/provider.go` (`toPiModel`).

Evidence: `TestLlamaReasoningModelOffersBudgetedThinkingLevels` (cmd/pig: the built-in host's model offers off through high and each level's request carries its `thinking_budget_tokens`; red before the change with `[off medium]`), `TestRefreshModelsDiscoversChatTemplateThinkingSupportForLoadedModels` (the ported Pi test, with Pi's expected map recorded at the assertion).

Parity allowance: no paired scenario runs a llama.cpp server; Pi's own test `packages/coding-agent/test/llama-extension.test.ts` asserts the two-level map this entry replaces.

Protects: the upstream llama-extension case checks the thinking levels a llama.cpp reasoning model offers and the request each level sends.

PiG-shaped test considered: `TestLlamaReasoningModelOffersBudgetedThinkingLevels` asserts the five-level map and each level's `thinking_budget_tokens`; `TestRefreshModelsDiscoversChatTemplateThinkingSupportForLoadedModels` runs Pi's case with Pi's expected map recorded at the assertion. Running Pi's case against the vendored TypeScript was considered.

Why not: Pi's case asserts the two-level (`off`, `medium`) map this entry replaces on purpose, so Pi's inputs cannot give Pi's expected output in PiG; the PiG-shaped tests assert the approved map.

Remove when: Pi offers more than off and medium for a llama.cpp reasoning model, then follow Pi.

Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:86dd8a0a5d9ce840

## D93 /reload evaluates an edited ES module extension again

What: Pi 1.5.2 imports each extension through jiti with `moduleCache: false` (`packages/coding-agent/src/core/extensions/loader.ts:571-602`, `loadExtensionModule`; the jiti instance and its `import` are `:588-593`), so `/reload` evaluates a TypeScript extension and its local TypeScript imports again. jiti 2.7.0 hands a file it treats as an ES module, an `.mjs` file or a `.js` file under `"type": "module"`, to Node's own `import()` when it imports asynchronously (`eval_evalModule`), and Node keeps an ES module for the life of the process by its URL. Pi therefore keeps running an edited ES module, an extension's entry or a local module it imports, until it restarts, even when the edit is a syntax error. This also holds for an `.mjs` module that a TypeScript extension imports. A TypeScript extension's own source runs its edit. This was probed with the pinned Pi in RPC mode. Pi main is the same: at `d36266e06` (read 2026-11-13) `loader.ts` does not differ from 1.5.2 and jiti is still 2.7.0.

What an edit followed by `/reload` runs, per source form (Pi 1.5.2 and PiG on the same extension, `TestReloadOfAnEditedNodeExtensionAgainstPi`):

| Source form | Pi | PiG |
|---|---|---|
| Node `.ts`: the entry and its local `.ts` imports | the edit | the edit |
| Node `.mjs` | the old module | the edit (this record) |
| Node `.js` under `"type": "module"` | the old module | the edit (this record) |
| Node `.cjs`, and `.js` read as CommonJS: the entry or a module it requires | the old module, which Node's `require` cache keeps | the old module |
| Go, Python and Rust source extensions | no Pi side: Pi loads no such extension | the edit: PiG builds or starts the extension again (`TestReloadRunsTheCurrentSourceOfACommandLineExtension/go`, `/python`, `/rust`) |

PiG's runtime hooks record, for each local extension module that Node loads, the source it loaded and the extension modules each evaluation imports. Local means outside the runtime and outside `node_modules`. When a reload pass starts, PiG gives a new URL, with a `pig-reload=<pass>` query, to each ES module whose source changed since Node loaded it and to each ES module that imports one, directly or through others. The resolve hook resolves every import of such a module to that URL, so the reload evaluates exactly those modules again:
- Every other module keeps its instance and its state, as in Pi.
- Extensions, TypeScript ones included, that import one edited module share its one new evaluation.
- An edit that breaks a module fails each extension that imports it on `/reload`, as an edited TypeScript extension's does in Pi, and the next valid edit loads it again.
- A module that an edit no longer imports stops counting.
- An installed package under `node_modules` keeps its module, and so does a CommonJS module, which Node keeps in its `require` cache: an edit of either takes effect after a restart, and does not evaluate its importers again.
- A later unedited reload keeps the latest evaluation, and a Session replacement never evaluates a module again.
- An old generation that still serves until the swap and imports such a module dynamically gets the new evaluation.
- A module evaluated again has the query in its `import.meta.url`, in what `import.meta.resolve` returns for it, and in the file names of its stack traces.
- An import that adds its own query, such as `import("./x.mjs?t=1")`, counts as an import of `x.mjs`, so an edit of `x.mjs` evaluates the importer again.
- The hooks need Node's synchronous module hooks (`module.registerHooks`, Node 22.15 or later); with an older Node release PiG keeps Pi's behavior.
- On Bun (`PIG_TS_RUNTIME=bun`), which runs neither Node hook API, a Bun runtime plugin records the same facts when Bun resolves a module it is about to load, before Bun reads the source. A reload removes each such module from Bun's module registry, and the next import evaluates it again under its own URL: on Bun, `import.meta.url` and stack traces carry no query.
- Node keeps every evaluation of a module until the process ends.

Why: fixed ahead of upstream. The owner fixes a non-destructive inherited Pi bug in PiG when the fix only stops a failure and changes nothing for a user who does not hit it, and reports it upstream.

Owner decision: 2026-10-04, owner Michael Kinsy (FIXLANE 2: fix the `.mjs` reload in 0.4.1 instead of deferring it; ID assigned by the lead).

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/loader.mjs` (`track`, `reevaluateEdited`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/register-hooks.mjs` (`trackExtensionModulesOnBun`).

Evidence: these tests are in `coding/extension/host/subprocess`.
- `TestHostReloadEvaluatesOnlyAnEditedESModuleExtensionAgain` (`.mjs` and `"type": "module"` `.js`, packed and isolated):
  - an unedited reload keeps the module;
  - an edit of a static import, a dynamic import, or a module first imported since the last reload evaluates it again;
  - a later unedited reload keeps the new evaluation;
  - an edited installed package keeps its module.
- `TestHostReloadKeepsAnUneditedSharedModuleOfPackedESModuleExtensions`: shared instance kept, then one new shared instance.
- `TestHostReloadKeepsOneSharedEvaluationAcrossAFailedImporter`.
- `TestHostReloadForgetsAnImportTheEditRemoved`.
- `TestHostReloadGivesTypeScriptAndESModuleImportersOneNewEvaluation`.
- `TestHostReloadKeepsAnESModuleWhoseCommonJSDependencyChanged`.
- `TestNodeReloadEvaluatesAnEditedTypeModuleJsEntryAgain`: an edit runs, a broken edit fails the load, and the fix loads it again. It replaces the test that pinned Pi's behavior.
- `TestSessionReplacementKeepsAnEditedESModuleUntilAReload`.
- `TestHostReloadKeepsMjsModuleStateAndReevaluatesTsModules`: Pi's rule for an unedited `.mjs` module.
- `TestReloadRunsTheCurrentSourceOfACommandLineExtension/node-mjs` (`coding/cli`): the real CLI over RPC.
- `TestReloadOfAnEditedNodeExtensionAgainstPi` (`coding/cli/reload_edited_source_pi_test.go`): the pinned Pi and PiG over RPC on the same extension, for each Node source form of the table above. Its Pi rows fail when Pi's side changes.
- The Bun row (`PIG_TS_RUNTIME=bun`, Bun in the runtime slot) is run by no gate: no gate image has Bun.

Each test fails with an earlier runtime: the CLI test with the runtime before D93, and the last four regression tests with the first D93 runtime. Each of ten mutations of the rules fails at least one test. Loading 19 more ES module extensions in one process takes the same 30 ms as before D93.

Parity allowance: after an edit and `/reload`, the factory of an ES module extension runs from the new module in PiG and from the old one in Pi. `TestReloadOfAnEditedNodeExtensionAgainstPi` compares both on the same extension and fails when Pi's side changes; the other tests above pin PiG's behavior.

Remove when: Pi's loader evaluates an edited ES module extension again on `/reload` (upstream issue drafted 2026-10-04).

SCRUTINIZED:approved

## D94 Subprocess editor working status runs from host snapshots

What: When a subprocess editor opts into embedded working status, Pi runs the StatusIndicator's own animation timer inside the editor and re-checks `isWorkingStatusEditor` each time a status is shown. PiG advances the animation on the host and sends the editor snapshots (`RemoteEditorStatus`), so the JavaScript side builds the indicator with empty initial frames and takes the frames and frame index from the snapshot. PiG also reads the opt-in once, at editor install, and the Go host places the status from it; an editor that changes `embedWorkingStatus` afterwards is not re-checked. The working and compaction colors match Pi 1.4.0: the editor's own `borderColor`, read at render time, or the thinking level's border color when the editor has none.

Why: One animation timer on the host keeps the spinner in step with the rest of the UI across the process boundary, and the host decides placement before the editor can answer.

Owner decision: 2026-10-05, owner Michael Kinsy (pr137).
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/editor-component.mjs` (install opt-in and indicator construction), `coding/extension/remote_editor.go` (`RemoteEditorStatus`).

Evidence: the Node editor test in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime_node_editor_component_test.go` compares border rows with Pi's own CustomEditor and StatusIndicator.

Parity allowance: observable rows match Pi; only timer ownership and opt-in re-check timing differ.

Remove when: the editor protocol can return a live opt-in and snapshots are replaced by an editor-side timer.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:117f0fccd57dbe53

## D96 The remote execution daemon is a Go program, not Pi's Rust binary

What: Pi 1.4.0's `@earendil-works/pi-env` runs a Rust daemon (`packages/env/daemon`, one `pi-env` binary per target) on the remote machine. PiG's daemon is a Go program (`env/cmd/pi-env`, `env/daemon`) with the same command line (`pi-env serve --token <hex>`) and the same wire protocol, framing and `hello` fields. It runs `exec` and `watch` through PiG's `durable/env/node`, which is the reference for the remote environment: `docs/semantics.md` of the package defines the remote as `NodeExecutionEnv` on the remote machine. Two effects are observable:

- Pi and PiG deploy to the same `~/.pi/mobile/tools` directory, and each deploy removes the other's daemon (`ssh.ts` upload scripts). A host used by both tools uploads again whenever the tool changes.
- The Go daemons are 4.1 MB to 4.7 MB, and the Windows upload is base64, about 6 MB. On a link near 128 kbit/s the upload approaches `UPLOAD_TIMEOUT_MS` (300 s).

Frame bodies are semantically equal to Pi's, not byte for byte: Go's JSON encoder sorts keys and escapes U+2028 and U+2029. Both daemons parse either form.

Why: PiG is a Go port with Go release builds. One static binary per target (`make build-env-daemons`) avoids a second toolchain, and the daemon reuses the code that defines the local environment.

Owner decision: reviewer recommendation (rev-port-104-env), approved by owner Michael Kinsy 2026-10-06.

Call-site markers: `env/cmd/pi-env/main.go`, `env/daemon/server.go` (`hello`), `env/daemon/fs.go` (target system names).

Evidence: `env/daemon/server_test.go` and `env/conformance_test.go` run the shared environment conformance suite against the Go daemon; `env/differential_test.go` compares it with `NodeExecutionEnv`.

Parity allowance: the protocol, `hello` fields (`os`, `arch` spelled as Rust's `std::env::consts`), error mapping and path rules match Pi; only the daemon implementation and the tool directory sharing differ.

Remove when: never, unless the owner decides to ship Pi's Rust binaries.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:b7617e4f895eb6f3

## D98 The eval harness hands the agent process an OAuth credential file

What: Pi's eval harness keeps the model credential in the model runtime's memory and deletes the runner's `auth.json` before the unprivileged sandbox drop (`packages/evals/src/harness.ts:312-335`), so the agent's file tools cannot read it. PiG's Session is a pig process. For an API key, PiG matches Pi: the key stays in the harness, and the isolated run's `auth.json` holds only a `!` command that fetches it from the harness's Unix socket, which the agent's file tools cannot open. An OAuth credential is written as stored to the isolated run's `auth.json`, owned by the sandbox user, and the agent's `read` tool can open it. The harness still deletes the runner's `auth.json` in the sandbox, removes the run's root afterwards, and strips the `PI_EVAL_*` variables and, in the sandbox, the credential's environment variable from the process environment.

Why: pig reads an OAuth credential only from `auth.json` and writes a refreshed token back to it, so the process that sends the provider request needs the file. A `!` command resolves only an API key value (`ai/auth.go` `AuthStorage.Get`, `internal/configvalue`).

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy 2026-10-06. Proposed by the lead under the owner's instruction of 2026-10-05 to port and account for packages/telemetry and packages/evals; narrowed to OAuth credentials by the rev-gap-telemetry-evals review.

Call-site markers: `internal/evals/harness_run.go` (credential hand-off in `RunPiCodingAgent`).

Remove when: pig accepts an OAuth credential from a source the sandboxed agent cannot read, such as an inherited file descriptor or a socket the harness serves.

Evidence: `TestRunPiCodingAgentKeepsTheAPIKeyOutOfTheAgentsFiles` and `TestAPIKeyHandOff` in `internal/evals/harness_run_test.go` prove that an API key reaches the provider and not the agent's files and that an OAuth entry is handed over as stored; `docs/parity/gap-closure/gap-telemetry-evals.md` lists the difference.

Parity allowance: the eval tooling is a developer tool outside Pi's user-visible surface; documentation evals run only in the container sandbox with the six documentation tools.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:87ba5801cd11bdb6

## D99 The Windows npm self-update quarantine waits out a held image

What: Before an npm self-update on Windows, Pi and PiG move each loaded image out of the package directory and copy it back (windows-self-update.ts:75-83). Pi moves it with one `renameSync`, which fails while another process holds the image open without delete sharing. PiG retries that rename while it fails with an access, sharing or lock error, with delays that double from 25 ms to 500 ms and add up to 10 seconds, and it stops early when the update's context ends. Any other error, and the error that remains after 10 seconds, fails the update as in Pi.

Pi bug: on Windows, Pi's npm self-update fails when another process, such as an anti-virus scanner or a search indexer, holds a loaded image open without delete sharing at the moment Pi renames it (`windows-self-update.ts:81` in Pi 1.4.0, one `renameSync` with no retry).

Why: Anti-virus scanners and search indexers open a newly written or newly started executable without delete sharing for a short time. In PiG the quarantined image is `pig.exe` itself, which the user just started, so an update could fail in that window. npm's graceful-fs retries its own Windows renames in the same way.

Owner decision: lead task decision of 2026-10-05 for PR #161 (rev-test-flakes-041), approved by owner Michael Kinsy 2026-10-06.
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/windows_self_update.go` (`quarantineNativeDependencies`). The retry is `internal/fsretry.Rename`.

Evidence: `TestQuarantineWaitsOutAHandleHeldOnTheRunningImage` (internal/codingagent) and the `internal/fsretry` Windows tests hold the image open as a scanner does. `TestWindowsNpmSelfUpdateReplacesTheRunningInstallation` (cmd/pig) runs the update end to end.

Parity allowance: when no other process holds the image, PiG does exactly what Pi does. It differs only while another process holds the image.

Remove when: Pi retries the quarantine rename, or Windows stops refusing to rename a file that another process holds open.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:b14dbcf6fcc4ba00

## D103 JS Error.stack is not modelled

What: Pi copies `error.stack` into a diagnostic's error info (`packages/ai/src/utils/diagnostics.ts:29`) and into any error snapshot that spreads an `Error`, so a persisted diagnostic or session entry can carry a JavaScript stack trace. PiG's error info (`ai.DiagnosticErrorInfo`) has a `Stack` field that stays empty, and PiG error types have no `stack` member. The inherited `name` is the error type's `Name()` method, which `ExtractDiagnosticError` reads, and `cause` is `Unwrap()`.

Why: A Go program has no JavaScript stack. A Go stack names source files and functions of the build machine, so storing one in a session file would leak paths, and it would not match Pi's text in any case.

Owner decision: lead answer of 2026-10-06 20:05 to question 2 of lg-ai-types-a, approved by owner Michael Kinsy.

Call-site markers: `ai/diagnostics.go` (`ExtractDiagnosticError`).

Remove when: Pi stops persisting `error.stack`, or an owner-approved sanitized Go stack format is defined.

Evidence: `TestExtractDiagnosticErrorOmitsTheStack` in `ai/diagnostics_upstream_test.go` shows that a diagnostic built from an error has no stack. `docs/parity/gap-closure/ledger-autobind.md` lists the inherited `stack` rows this record closes.

Parity allowance: a diagnostic differs from Pi's only in the missing `stack` text, which no PiG or Pi code reads back.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:08f623ed4fbc39b1

## D105 The interactive driver owns terminal input; extension input listeners run asynchronously

What: Pi's `TUI.start()` owns the terminal. It reads stdin and runs `handleTerminalInput` (color reply, light/dark report, input-listener chain, cell-size reply, overlay focus fix-up, focused component), and `TUI.stop()` runs the protected `beforeTerminalStop` and `afterTerminalStop` hooks. In PiG the renderers (`tui.TUI`, `TuiAltScreen`) own frames, overlays, the listener chain (`RunInputListeners`), and the color and cell-size consumers (`ConsumeTerminalColorResponse`, `ConsumeTerminalColorSchemeReport`, `ConsumeCellSizeResponse`) with Pi's names and order. The interactive driver owns the raw-mode reader and calls those in that order for each chunk. An extension's terminal-input listener runs after them, asynchronously, with an ordering ticket per chunk. The `before` and `after` terminal start and stop hooks are inline in each renderer's `Start` and `Stop`.

Upstream state: Pi runs extensions in-process, so an extension's `addInputListener` callback is a synchronous member of the listener chain, and TypeScript's protected virtuals let a subclass add the start and stop hooks.

Why: an extension is a subprocess that answers over a socket, and no blocking extension IPC may run on the input loop. The driver must therefore keep the extension pass off the loop and resume the chunk with the verdict. Go has no virtual dispatch from an embedded base, so the two renderers carry the hook bodies directly.

Owner decision: lead ruling of 2026-10-07 15:20 (LEAD-RULINGS-1520, gap-agent-tui) under the owner's standing parity-scope approval; flag it in the 0.4.2 release notes for the owner.

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/interactive_input.go` (`dispatchInputChunk`), `internal/codingagent/terminal_input_queue.go` (`passTerminalInput`), `tui/tui_input.go` (`RunInputListeners`).

Remove when: never (subprocess-only extensions), or when Pi's extension input listeners become asynchronous and the driver can adopt Pi's `handleTerminalInput` whole.

Evidence: `tui/tui_input_pipeline_test.go`, `tui/main_screen_oracle_test.go`, the `tui/tui-base/terminal-input-pipeline` contract in `test/parity/behavior-contracts.toml`, and the `extensions-runtime` terminal-input scenarios.

Parity allowance: the observable key order and the listener verdicts match Pi; only the thread that runs an extension's listener differs.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:330b5519acbdbb09

## D110 Git sources: `#subdirectory=` selects a subdirectory; `git://` and upper-case schemes yield usable repositories

What: PiG parses Git package sources with a port of Pi's `parseGitUrl` (`packages/coding-agent/src/utils/git.ts`) and `hosted-git-info` (`coding/source/gitparse.go`, `coding/source/hostedgit.go`), and differs from Pi in four spellings. (1) A fragment that starts with `subdirectory=` (`git:https://host/org/repo@v1#subdirectory=plugins/review`) selects a subdirectory of the repository for a Piglet source (`docs/additive-features.md`); Pi has no such spelling and reads every `#<text>` as a ref. Any other fragment is a ref in PiG too, as in Pi. (2) A source that starts with `git://` is a protocol URL: its repository is `git://host/org/repo`. Pi reads the leading `git:` as the shorthand prefix and returns the repository `https:////host/org/repo`. (3) The scheme of a protocol URL is matched without regard to case when PiG decides whether to prefix `https://`: `HTTPS://host/org/repo` keeps its repository. Pi returns `https://HTTPS://host/org/repo`. (4) A `git:file://host/path` source (a local bare repository, used by Piglet installs) is a Git source whose host is the URL host, such as `localhost`; Pi's `parseGitUrl` returns null for it.

Pi bug: for (2) and (3) Pi's `parseGitUrl` returns a repository no Git client can clone (`https:////host/org/repo`, `https://HTTPS://host/org/repo`), so `pi install` of such a source fails at clone time.

Why: (1) is additive: `@<commit>#subdirectory=<path>` is how PiG installs a Piglet that lives in a repository subdirectory, and no Pi input is rejected for it. (2) and (3) are Pi results that no Git client can clone; PiG returns the repository the user wrote.

Owner decision: lead ruling of 2026-10-09 on the Git source oracle findings (owner priorities: parity, then maintainability): port Pi's accepted spellings, keep `#subdirectory=` as an additive spelling, and record it as a numbered divergence. The number D110 is the next free ID after D109.

Observable effect: `git:github.com/o/r#subdirectory=sub` has the subdirectory `sub` and no ref in PiG; Pi reads the ref `subdirectory=sub`. `git://github.com/o/r` and `HTTPS://github.com/o/r` resolve to a cloneable repository in PiG and to an unusable one in Pi; `git:file://localhost/tmp/r.git` is a source in PiG and null in Pi. Every other of the oracle's 170 spellings, including all hosted-git-info shorthand forms (`git:user/repo`, `github:`, `gitlab:`, `bitbucket:`, `gist:`, `sourcehut:`), `#<ref>`, `%2F` and backslashes, gives Pi's repository, host, path and ref.

Call-site markers: `coding/source/gitparse.go` (`parseGitURL`), `coding/source/ref.go` (`parseGit`, `splitGitSubdirectory`).

Locked by: `TestParseGitMatchesPi` (`coding/source/parse_git_oracle_test.go`) runs Pi 1.4.0's `parseGitUrl` over the spellings and pins the two differences in `knownGitDifferences`; `TestParseGitFragmentIsARefUnlessItIsSubdirectory` and `TestParseGitSubdirectory` (`coding/source/ref_test.go`) pin the subdirectory spelling; `TestParseURLMatchesNode` (`internal/nodeurl`) checks the URL reader against Node.

Parity allowance: none beyond the four spellings above.

Remove when: Pi accepts a `#subdirectory=` spelling or PiG stops installing Piglets from repository subdirectories (1); Pi fixes the `git://` and upper-case scheme repositories (2, 3) or parses `git:file://` sources (4).

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:3ef7266621580d74

## D111 Abort abandons a non-cooperative extension tool after a grace period

What: Abort abandons a non-cooperative extension tool after a grace period: Pi awaits it indefinitely; a subprocess call must not block the agent loop forever. When a run aborts while an extension tool runs, PiG cancels the `tool_call` request, so the tool's AbortSignal fires (Node `AbortSignal`, Go `ctx.Done()`, Rust and Python `is_cancelled`), and waits `ToolAbortGrace` (3 s) for the tool to settle. A tool that settles within the grace period behaves exactly as in Pi: Pi's `executePreparedToolCall` awaits `tool.execute` and makes its result, or its thrown message, the tool result (`packages/agent/src/agent-loop.ts:831-853`). A tool that has not settled when the grace period ends fails with Pi's abort text `createErrorToolResult("Operation aborted")` (`agent-loop.ts:633,751,770`), the session is free, and the tool's late answer is discarded. Pi awaits such a tool indefinitely and stays "Working" through every further Escape.

Pi bug: Pi awaits an aborted extension tool call without a limit, so a tool that never settles holds the agent loop and the Session until the process ends.

Why: an extension tool runs in another process. A tool that never settles would hold the agent loop, and with it the Session, until the process ends, and the user has no way to recover the Session.

Scope: only an extension tool call (`tool_call`) of the subprocess host, which covers every SDK in its own process, packed, and fused. A tool that settles within the grace period, built-in tools, in-process extensions, provider streams and `user_bash` operations keep Pi's behavior and are not waived. For a provider stream that means no bound: the abort of a turn reaches the author of an extension's `streamSimple` (a `provider_stream_simple` request), like the author of a native Provider's stream, as a signal-only cancel, which aborts the author's signal and ends nothing. The host forwards what the author's stream gives afterwards and waits for the author's own end (its terminal event, its result, or its error), which decides the turn's final message, because Pi's consumer has no check of the signal (`packages/agent/src/agent-loop.ts:424-478`). An author that never reacts holds the turn until the process ends, as in Pi. Every SDK does the same, in its own process, packed, and fused (`TestAbortedProviderTurnKeepsWhatTheAuthorsStreamGives` in `test/extension-conformance/provider_stream_cancel_test.go`). A call whose context has already ended when it reaches the host is sent to the extension, as Pi runs `tool.execute` with an aborted signal (subagent-extension.test.ts "returns aborted task details when the signal is already aborted": five aborted task results): the request frame carries `signal_aborted`, the tool starts with its signal aborted and its host calls are answered, the signal-only cancel follows the request, and the grace period below runs from the call's start. Only a direct call of the tool and an abort that lands between the agent's gate and the host's write reach the host this way: Pi's agent loop and a nested `executeTool` answer `Operation aborted` without entering the tool when the signal is already aborted (`agent-loop.ts:633,751,770`), and PiG's gates do the same, so they never send it. The state the tool runs against is written first; a call whose frames cannot be queued within the grace period (the writer's queue stays full) ends as `Operation aborted`; a frame already queued waits for its write as every request does, and the call ends with the connection's failure when the peer stops reading (the heartbeat, D56): the tool call returns the connection's failure, an `ExtensionUnresponsiveError` or the `TransportError` of the write the failed connection ended, wrapped as `sync extension state for tool <name>` or `tool <name>`. A connection that is closing or closed (a shutdown, a reload, a replacement) takes no call: it is not sent and waits no grace period. The abort cancels nothing but the tool's signal, as Pi's does (`agent-loop.ts:817-853`): the host sends a signal-only cancel (`CancelPayload.signal_only`), so the tool's pending and later host calls (dialogs, notifications, session reads, `exec`) are answered, and only its nested tool calls (`executeTool`, built-in tool `execute` and `localExec` called under the request) take the abort, as the signal they run with (`runner.ts:998`, `options.signal ?? signal`). What the tool still awaits when the grace period ends is cancelled then: that is the whole of this divergence for a tool that awaits a host call, which Pi would await indefinitely.

Owner decision: 2026-10-09, owner Michael Kinsy, ruling on robust-ext F6 (option A, refined: grace period, then `Operation aborted`, the same rule for every SDK).

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/conn.go` (`ToolAbortGrace`, the `tool_call` branch of `Conn.request`).

Evidence: `TestAbortedToolCallSettlesCooperativelyOrFailsAfterTheGrace` in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/tool_abort_grace_node_test.go` (a Node tool that rejects on abort keeps its message, one that resolves keeps its result, one that hangs fails with `Operation aborted` no sooner than the grace period, isolated and packed) and `TestConformance_AbortedToolSettlesWithinTheGrace` in `test/extension-conformance/tool_abort_grace_test.go` (Go, Node, Rust and Python in their own processes and packed, and fused Go). Both fail when the host stops waiting at the cancel, with `context canceled` in place of the tool's own answer. `TestRPCAbortedExtensionToolSettlesAndFreesTheSession` in `coding/cli/rpc_tool_abort_grace_test.go` drives the Session path in RPC mode: the hanging tool's `tool_execution_end` carries `Operation aborted` no sooner than the grace period, the abort answers after the run settles, and the next prompt runs a cooperative tool whose own thrown message is its result before the grace period ends. `TestToolAbortKeepsTheToolsHostCalls` in `test/extension-conformance/tool_signal_only_test.go` (every SDK, isolated, packed and fused: a dialog pending at the abort and one opened after it are answered and the tool's signal reads aborted, a nested `abort_tool` sees the abort, and the dialog a tool still awaits at the grace's end is cancelled then) and `TestToolCallAbortCancelsSignalOnlyAndSparesItsHostCalls` and `TestToolCallInactivityCancelStaysOrdinary` in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/tool_abort_signal_only_test.go` (the cancel frame is signal-only, nested calls end, other calls are spared, the ordinary cancel follows at the grace's end, the inactivity cancel stays ordinary). A call whose context had already ended is covered by the "returns aborted task details when the signal is already aborted" subtest of `TestSubagentExampleChildLifecycle` in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/subagent_extension_upstream_test.go` (Pi's test with its inputs: five aborted task results), `TestConformance_ToolCalledAbortedEntersWithItsSignalAborted` in `test/extension-conformance/tool_aborted_entry_test.go` (every SDK, isolated, packed and fused: the tool echoes a nonce, reports that its signal was aborted on its first line and gets its host call answered, and one that does not settle ends as `Operation aborted`) and, in `coding/extension/host/subprocess/tool_call_ended_context_test.go`, `TestEndedToolCallReachesTheExtensionWithItsSignalAborted`, `TestEndedToolCallKeepsItsHostCallsUntilTheGraceEnds`, `TestAbortBetweenTheAgentGateAndTheHostWriteEntersTheTool`, `TestEndedToolCallGivesTheToolTheWholeGraceFromTheCallsStart` (a context that ended ten grace periods before the call: the tool answers late in the grace and its own result is the result), `TestEndedToolCallThatCannotBeWrittenFailsAsAborted`, `TestEndedToolCallOnAClosingConnectionIsNotSent` and `TestEndedToolCallDoesNotLengthenShutdownOrReplacement` (a call sent after its context ended, then `Close` or `Retire` with the tool not settled: it ends at once with the error a call that was in flight when its connection went gets, well under the grace period).

Parity allowance: no paired scenario runs a tool that ignores its signal, because Pi never finishes that run. The cooperative case is compared through the conformance row above.

Remove when: Pi stops awaiting aborted tool calls.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:b1bdc034cdf01952

## D113 A Session has no `extensionRunnerRef` cell

What: Pi's `AgentSessionConfig.extensionRunnerRef` is a mutable `{ current?: ExtensionRunner }` that the caller creates before the Agent and that `AgentSession` assigns on every runtime build (`agent-session.ts:296,498,3644-3645`). `sdk.ts:333-476` creates the cell so the closures it gives the Agent and the cache warmer (cache-warming decision `:338`, request headers `:347`, `before_provider_request` `:383`, `after_provider_response` `:388`, `provider_stream_event` `:400` and `context` `:437`) read the current runner. PiG's `SessionOptions` has no such field. A Session reads its own current runner, and `Session.ExtensionRunner()` returns it after every replacement. A caller that builds an Agent or a CacheWarmer for `SessionOptions.Agent` or `SessionOptions.CacheWarmer` and needs the runner inside their closures reads it from the Session after construction instead of from a cell the Session fills in.

Why: Go closes over the Session in the hooks the Session installs, so no PiG code needs the cell. A cell shared with the caller would be a second source of truth for the runner of a Session that replaces its runner on reload and on session replacement.

Scope: only a caller that supplies an Agent or a CacheWarmer. The Session's own Agent and warmer, the CLI and the SDK entry points behave as in Pi.

Owner decision: 2026-10-10, owner Michael Kinsy, Q1 of the design-out audit (option B: `SessionOptions.Agent` and `SessionOptions.CacheWarmer` are ported; `extensionRunnerRef` stays designed out with a numbered divergence).
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

Call-site markers: `coding/session.go` (`SessionOptions.Agent`).

Evidence: `TestNewAgentSessionDrivesTheSuppliedAgentAndChainsItsHooks` and `TestNewAgentSessionDrivesTheSuppliedCacheWarmer` (`coding/session_supplied_agent_test.go`) drive a supplied Agent and warmer through the Session; `Session.ExtensionRunner()` is the accessor.

Parity allowance: no paired scenario builds an Agent through the public Pi constructor, because Pi's only caller is `sdk.ts`.

Protects: the property Pi's `extensionRunnerRef` serves: the hooks of a caller-supplied Agent and cache warmer read the Session's current extension runner after every replacement. No Pi test covers the cell itself; Pi's suite helper (`test/suite/harness.ts`) builds one.

PiG-shaped test considered: `TestNewAgentSessionDrivesTheSuppliedAgentAndChainsItsHooks` and `TestNewAgentSessionDrivesTheSuppliedCacheWarmer` drive a supplied Agent and warmer through the Session and its `ExtensionRunner()` accessor.

Why not: a test of the cell needs the cell, and PiG has none by design (a second source of truth for the runner); the property is held by the two tests above.

Remove when: PiG's SDK needs a caller-owned runner cell.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:7f11c40f6478a71b

## D114 `StdinBuffer` has no `getMaxListeners`, `setMaxListeners` or `MaxListenersExceededWarning`

What: Pi's `StdinBuffer` extends Node's `EventEmitter` (`packages/tui/src/stdin-buffer.ts:281`). It inherits the listener limit of 10 per event (`EventEmitter.defaultMaxListeners`), `getMaxListeners()` and `setMaxListeners(n)`, and Node prints `MaxListenersExceededWarning` to stderr once per event when a registration takes that event past the limit. PiG's `tui.StdinBuffer` ports the rest of the listener surface (`On`, `Once`, `Off`, `Emit`, `ListenerCount`, `Listeners` and the others in `tui/stdin_buffer_events.go`), and its `ProcessBytes`, `ProcessString` and `Flush` also return the sequences they emit. It has no listener limit: any number of listeners register without a warning, and it has no `GetMaxListeners` or `SetMaxListeners`.

Why: the lead ruled on 2026-10-08 against a `getMaxListeners` and `setMaxListeners` that carry a magic 10, and the owner kept that ruling. Go could match: `addRegistration` could count the registrations of the event against a per-buffer limit of 10 and print Node's warning to stderr once per event.

Scope: only a caller that adds more than ten listeners for one `StdinBuffer` event. Pi's `ProcessTerminal` adds one `data` and one `paste` listener (`terminal.ts`), so no Pi code reaches the limit.

Owner decision: 2026-10-10, owner Michael Kinsy, Q3 of the design-out audit (option A: keep the members designed out and record a numbered divergence for the missing warning).
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

Call-site markers: `tui/stdin_buffer.go` (`StdinBuffer`) and `tui/stdin_buffer_events.go` (`addRegistration`).

Evidence: none beyond the absence of any Pi caller. `TestStdinBufferEventsMatchPi` pins the events that Pi's callers read.

Parity allowance: no paired scenario adds an eleventh listener to a `StdinBuffer`.

Protects: Node's listener limit of 10 per event and its `MaxListenersExceededWarning` on the eleventh registration. No Pi test asserts it; the tui tests rely on the events Pi's callers read.

PiG-shaped test considered: `TestStdinBufferEventsMatchPi` pins those events. A test that adds eleven listeners and expects Node's warning was considered.

Why not: the owner ruled on 2026-10-10 (Q3, option A) against a magic limit of 10, so a test of the warning would assert behavior PiG does not have; no Pi code reaches the limit.

Remove when: PiG's `StdinBuffer` gets Node's listener limit and warning (design-out audit Q3 option B).

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:77e22446f4d6c770

## D115 Gemini 2.x tool-result images follow the whole run of function responses

What: for a Google (Generative AI and Vertex) model without multimodal function responses (a `gemini-*` or `gemini-live-*` id with a major version below 3; `google-shared.ts:180-186` `supportsMultimodalFunctionResponse` returns true for every other id, so Gemini 3 and non-Gemini ids nest the images in the function response), PiG sends the "Tool result image:" turns of a run of tool results after the last function response of the run, in result order, so every function response of one model turn stays in the single user turn after it. Pi 1.4.0 pushes each image turn right after its result (`packages/ai/src/api/google-shared.ts` `convertMessages`, the `toolResult` branch: "For Gemini < 3, add images in a separate user message"), so the next result starts a new turn. Pi's own test asserts that shape (`packages/ai/test/google-shared-image-tool-result-routing.test.ts:80-88`: contents[2] function responses, [3] image turn, [4] a function response). Pi main (earendil-works/pi, 2026-10-10) is unchanged.

Pi bug: with a Gemini model below version 3, a parallel tool batch whose results carry an image in any result but the last splits the function responses of one model turn across user turns, and Gemini rejects every later turn of the session with HTTP 400 (`google-shared.ts` `convertMessages`, the `toolResult` branch).

Why: Gemini answers the function calls of one model turn only when the user turn directly after it holds a function response for every call ("Please ensure that the number of function response parts is equal to the number of function call parts of the function call turn", Google function-calling documentation; the same file's comment: "Cloud Code Assist API requires all function responses to be in a single user turn"). A parallel tool batch with an image in any result but the last gets HTTP 400 on every later turn of the session. A single call, and a run whose images all belong to the last result, produce Pi's request byte for byte.

Scope: `geminiConvertMessages` in `ai/google.go`, used by `google-generative-ai` and `google-vertex`. Gemini 3 models, which nest images in the function response, are unchanged.

Owner decision: 2026-10-10, owner rule "fix every confirmed bug now, Pi's included", relayed by the lead; number assigned by the lead.

Call-site markers: `ai/google.go` (`flushImageTurns`, `geminiConvertMessages`).

Evidence: `TestGoogleImageToolResultsKeepFunctionResponsesInOneTurnD115` and `TestGoogleImageToolResultOfASingleCallMatchesPiD115` (`ai/google_image_tool_results_d115_test.go`), `TestGeminiToolResultImageOrderingMatchesUpstream`, the payload oracles `TestGoogleGenerativeAIPayloadMatchesPi` and `TestGoogleVertexPayloadMatchesPi` (Pi's payload rewritten by `piPayloadWithD115`), and the differential fuzz `TestPipelineMatchesPi` / `TestAIPipelineMatchesPi` (`test/pipeline-diff`, Pi's request rewritten by `withD115`, PiG's request checked against the Google contract without an allowance).

Parity allowance: the paired scenarios send no Gemini 2.x image tool results.

Remove when: Pi keeps the function responses of one model turn in one user turn (Pi's routing test changes), at which point delete the deferral and the two rewrites.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:8fb823b23c03c57b

## D116 Bedrock Converse messages of one role are merged

What: PiG's Amazon Bedrock Converse request merges adjacent messages of the same role into one message whose content blocks keep their order (tool results first, then the user text that follows them). Pi 1.4.0 pushes every user message (`packages/ai/src/api/bedrock-converse-stream.ts:1014`, `result.push({ role: ConversationRole.USER, content })`) every run of tool results (`:1124`) and every assistant message (`:1087`) as a message of its own and never checks the previous message's role, so a user prompt right after tool results, two user messages, or user messages around a dropped (empty, errored or aborted) assistant turn put two user messages side by side. Pi main (earendil-works/pi, 2026-10-10) is unchanged.

Pi bug: Pi's Bedrock Converse request can put two user messages side by side, for example a prompt sent after a turn aborted while tools ran, and Bedrock rejects every later turn of the session with a ValidationException (HTTP 400) (`bedrock-converse-stream.ts:1014,1087,1124`).

Why: the Converse API requires the roles of a conversation to alternate and rejects two consecutive messages of one role with a ValidationException (HTTP 400). The shape is common: aborting a turn while tools run and then sending a new prompt gives `assistant(toolUse), user(toolResult...), user(prompt)`, and every later turn of the session then fails.

Scope: `convertBedrockMessages` in `ai/providers/bedrock/bedrock.go`, for every Bedrock Converse model. An already alternating conversation produces Pi's request byte for byte. The final cache point still goes on the last user message.

Owner decision: 2026-10-10, owner rule "fix every confirmed bug now, Pi's included", relayed by the lead; number assigned by the lead.

Call-site markers: `ai/providers/bedrock/bedrock.go` (`push` in `convertBedrockMessages`).

Evidence: `TestBedrockMergesAdjacentSameRoleMessagesD116` (`ai/providers/bedrock/bedrock_alternation_d116_test.go`, red before the fix: roles [user assistant user user]), `TestBedrockConvertMessages_SkipsEmptyAssistant`, the Pi-derived `TestBedrockUpstreamConvertMessages` case, and `TestBedrockConversePayloadMatchesPi` (Pi's payload rewritten by `piBedrockPayloadWithD116`).

Parity allowance: `test/parity/scenarios/providers-faux-streaming/29-bedrock-ecmascript-whitespace.toml` runs a Bedrock conversation that starts with two user messages. Its Pi probe (`test/parity/testdata/bedrock-whitespace-pi.mjs`) merges adjacent same-role messages in the captured Pi request, content blocks in order. The scenario compares the complete captured message arrays with `json_output_equal`, so block contents, order and presence still have to match.

Remove when: Pi merges or otherwise alternates adjacent same-role messages in its Converse converter.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:947b4384ecb3d97d

## D117 `DiscoverAndLoadExtensions` takes no event bus

What: Pi's `discoverAndLoadExtensions(configuredPaths, cwd, agentDir?, eventBus?)` (`packages/coding-agent/src/core/extensions/loader.ts:828`) hands the optional `eventBus` to every extension it loads, so the extensions of that call and the caller share one `pi.events` bus. PiG's `coding.DiscoverAndLoadExtensions(ctx, configuredPaths, cwd, agentDir)` takes a context instead and no bus. The Extension Host runs every extension in its own process and owns the routing of `pi.events` between the extensions it hosts (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/event_bus.go`), so the extensions of one call share that routed bus and a caller cannot join it.

Why: the Host keeps one ordered `pi.events` registry for every realm it runs, and no Host API attaches a caller's bus to it. Accepting the parameter and ignoring it would hide the difference, so the parameter does not exist. Go could match: the Host could forward each routed emit to the caller's bus and, when an extension first listens on a channel, subscribe that channel on the caller's bus as a native (value) listener of its registry, with a marker that keeps an emission from coming back through the bridge. The compiled-in factories of a Session share a caller's bus through the Session loader, which does take one.

Scope: only a caller that loads extensions with `DiscoverAndLoadExtensions` and expects to emit or listen on `pi.events` itself. Discovery order, deduplication, entry-point rules, loading and error reporting are Pi's.

Owner decision: 2026-10-10, lead ruling on port-sdk-extension-loading (number D117 assigned by the lead).

Call-site markers: `coding/discover_extensions.go` (`DiscoverAndLoadExtensions`).

Evidence: `TestDiscoverExtensionPathsMatchesPisDiscoveryCases` runs the discovery cases of Pi's `extensions-discovery.test.ts` through the function's discovery; `TestDiscoverExtensionPathsFollowsPisLoaderOrderAndRules` and `TestDiscoverExtensionPathsReadsManifestsLinksAndConfiguredPathsAsPi` (`coding/discover_extensions_test.go`) pin order, manifest and link rules; `TestDiscoverAndLoadExtensionsLoadsWhatItDiscoversAndReportsFailures` and `TestDiscoverAndLoadExtensionsReturnsTheRuntimeTheExtensionsShare` (`coding/discover_extensions_node_test.go`) pin loading, errors and the shared runtime; `TestUpstreamExtensionsDiscovery` (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/upstream_discovery_test.go`) loads Pi's discovery fixtures in the Host.

Parity allowance: no paired scenario passes an event bus to `discoverAndLoadExtensions`; Pi's `extensions-runner.test.ts` and `extensions-input-event.test.ts` pass none.

Remove when: the Extension Host accepts a caller-owned event bus.

## D118 A durable commit with an unknown task or submission status fails with "Unknown task status"

What: a commit to PiG's MemoryStorage, or to the JSONL storage built on it, whose task or submission status is outside the closed set (`pending`, `running`, `waiting`, `completing`, `terminal`; `queued`, `placed`, `done`, `unanswered`) fails before anything is stored, with `Unknown task status "<status>"` or `Unknown submission status "<status>"`. Pi 1.4.0 has no such check. Its prepareCommit (`packages/durable/src/storage/memory.ts:268-289`) accepts the batch, and apply then indexes the status in the fixed-key `taskIdsByStatus` / `submissionIdsByStatus` objects (`:375-392`), which throws a V8 TypeError (`Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'length')`, or `ids.push is not a function` / `ids.splice is not a function` for a status such as `toString` or `__proto__`). By then the earlier writes of the batch, the record type and the ID list are applied, so `scanTasks` later returns `[undefined]`. Pi's JsonlStorage.commit (`packages/durable/src/storage/jsonl/storage.ts:278-305`) appends the sidecar and main.jsonl lines before apply throws.

Why: Pi's message is a TypeError leaking from an undefined access, not a designed message, and the status is outside Pi's static type, so Pi never meets it from typed code. A Go `TaskStatus` or `SubmissionStatus` is a string and cannot exclude it. PiG reports the status by name and stores nothing, instead of corrupting the in-memory indexes and writing a commit that the next open rejects.

Scope: `checkStatuses` in `durable/storage/memory.go`, run at the end of prepare, so every error Pi raises before apply (document actions, global IDs, sequence) takes precedence as in Pi. Recovery is unchanged: a stored commit with an unknown status fails to open with `Invalid committed state at sequence N`, exactly as in Pi (`jsonl/storage.ts:706-712`).

Owner decision: 2026-10-10, lead ruling on rev-batch-ctor-recovery finding 10 (keep PiG's message; number from the lead block).

Call-site markers: `durable/storage/memory.go` (`checkStatuses`).

Evidence: `TestMemoryStorageRejectsAnUnknownTaskOrSubmissionStatus` (`durable/storage/memory_status_test.go`), `statusPrecedencePrograms` in `TestMemoryStorageMatchesPiOnSeededPrograms` (document errors in the same batch match Pi), and `TestJsonlRecoveryMatchesPiOnDamagedDirectories` (recovery of a status replaced with `bogus` matches Pi).

Parity allowance: no paired scenario commits durable records with an out-of-type status; the Pi differentials compare only the batches where both raise the same error.

Remove when: Pi validates task and submission statuses before apply, at which point use its message.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:c81e3dd421fbacd7

## D120 A streamed tool call without an id or name no longer wedges the session (pi-mono #10299)

What: Pi stores a tool call that a provider streams without an id (and, for OpenRouter/GLM and Xiaomi MiMo, without a function name) and replays it. Its tool result then carries `tool_call_id: ""`, and every later turn fails with `400 tool messages must include a non-empty string tool_call_id` or `tool_calls[0] is missing a function name`. PiG gives a call streamed without an id a unique `<name>_<epoch ms>_<counter>` id when its block ends (the form Pi's Google adapters generate, `google-generative-ai.ts:195-200`). Every provider's replay omits a tool call whose name is blank or whose id is still empty after the provider's ID normalization, together with the tool results that name its id (an empty id included). A tool result whose call is absent is passed on as in Pi. Stored sessions that already hold such a call replay again.

Why: the session is unusable after one malformed chunk. The fault is in the stored transcript, so a retry cannot recover it. The fix shape follows the `transformMessages` patch proposed in the issue thread.

Scope: the shared stream builder (`endToolCallWith`) and the shared tool-flow pass of `transformMessages` (`prepareProviderToolFlow`), which every API path reaches (Completions, Responses, Anthropic and Mistral through `TransformMessages`; Google and Bedrock directly). A call with an id and a name replays as in Pi. Mistral's ID normalization gives an id-less call an id, so there only a nameless call is omitted.

Owner decision: 2026-10-10, owner ruling "fix every confirmed bug now, ported from Pi or not".

Call-site markers: `ai/stream_builder.go` (`endToolCallWith`), `ai/tool_flow.go` (`prepareProviderToolFlow`).
