Parity allowance: the commit sequence numbers (`entries.commit_seq`, `document_revisions.seq`, `next_seq`) of an unawaited-job comparison with Pi's store, and only in `TestUnawaitedJobCommittedAfterTheAnswerChangesOnlyOrder`; every other interop comparison runs the awaited fixture and compares them.

Remove when: the core engine is the default Durable engine and the order sweep (the interop scenarios with the job unawaited, compared with Pi's store) shows 0 mismatches in 2,000 runs at GOMAXPROCS 1, 2 and 8, which needs a production `Settle` for the core and the contract trace rows E24 and I to match Pi; delete `TestUnawaitedJobCommittedAfterTheAnswerChangesOnlyOrder` then.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:211b2ec1af358ef4

## D182 A proxy start event with an unusable content index ends the stream with an error

What: `StreamProxy` (`agent/proxy.go`) ends the stream with an error when a `text_start`, `thinking_start` or `toolcall_start` event names a content index that is past the end of the content array, or that is not a JavaScript array index (absent, null, negative, fractional, or a non-numeric string). Pi stores the block with `partial.content[contentIndex] = block`: an index past the end leaves holes in the array (`[null,null,{text:"x"}]` after JSON), and a non-index key becomes a property that JSON never shows. The stream then completes with `stopReason: "stop"` in Pi.

Why: this is a Pi bug PiG does not reproduce, not a Go limitation. Pi's holes break Pi's own consumers of the message (a `for..of` over the content, or persistence of null blocks), and the stream still reports success. Go's content slice could carry an absent block, but every reader of assistant content (about 60 sites in `ai` and `agent`, on the hottest path) assumes each slot is filled; carrying holes would make each of them tolerate one, for a malformed proxy server only. PiG instead ends the stream at the unusable index with an error that names it.

Scope: sparse or non-index `contentIndex` values on the proxy wire only. Well-formed streams are byte-identical to Pi (differential tests run Pi's `proxy.js` against the same bodies).

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-18 (proposed the same day).

Call-site markers: `agent/proxy.go` (`setProxyContent`, `proxyEventConverter.process`).

Evidence: `agent/proxy_sparse_oracle_test.go`, `agent/proxy_oracle_test.go`.

Parity allowance: none.

Remove when: `ai.AssistantMessage` content can hold an absent block, or Pi rejects an unusable content index.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:56d0a460a18df3dd

## D183 A message delivered at the terminal proxy event is not changed by later events

What: after `StreamProxy` pushes a `done` or `error` event, it drains the rest of the response body without applying it. Pi keeps reading and applies later events, parse failures and aborts to the message object it already delivered, so the consumer's message can change after the terminal event.

Why: the terminal event hands the partial message to the consumer. In Go, a later write to it is a data race with the consumer's reads. The drain keeps the connection open as long as Pi's.

Scope: a proxy server that sends events after `done` or `error`. A well-formed server does not.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-18 (proposed the same day).

Call-site markers: `agent/proxy.go` (the `terminal` branch of the read loop).

Evidence: `agent/proxy_oracle_test.go`, `agent/proxy_sparse_oracle_test.go`.

Parity allowance: none.

Remove when: the delivered message is an immutable snapshot, or Pi stops mutating the delivered message.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:932ac3ddfadf971f

## D184 A script's unawaited tool call can be aborted before its tool starts, where Pi always starts it first

What: a code mode script that calls a tool and does not await it (`tools.hold({}); await tools.echo({ text: "a" }); return 1;`) ends and aborts that call. Pi's call has always started its tool by then. PiG's call usually has, but when one goroutine of the call stalls for milliseconds while the next call's goroutine runs its whole round trip, the script ends first and the abort lands before the tool's `execute` ran. The call's nested task is `terminal` with outcome `aborted` either way. Only whether the tool code had started differs.

Why: Pi runs the launch of every call of a script in one thread. Both calls take the same chain of steps (memo, admission commit, scheduler reservation, call-phase commits, `execute`), the earlier one first, so the earlier one's `execute` precedes the later one's result by construction (`packages/durable/src/codemode/codemode.ts:765-831`, `harness/tool.ts:378-417`, `harness/scheduler.ts:1413-1417`, `1656`). That order is a consequence of one thread, not a rule: a step that waits for real time, such as a `beforeTool` hook awaiting an approval, lets a later call pass an earlier one in Pi too. PiG runs the calls on parallel goroutines. `spawnEntered` (the dispatch of a launched or replayed call) now returns only once the call's goroutine has entered, which removed the systematic overtake (the call started after the next one entered `ExecuteTool`; 36 of 12,000 runs of the Pi subtest failed at 8 CPUs, 573 of 3,000 at 3 CPUs). What is left is an operating-system stall of one goroutine between two steps of its task, while another runs on. Nothing the call waits on expresses it, so no ticket or queue orders it. Only an `entered`-style gate before `execute`, which waits while the earlier call's goroutine is running or runnable, would, and it needs a link from a nested task to its earlier sibling that the task runtime does not offer, in both `durable/harness` and `durable/core/driver`. The simpler orderings (each call waits for the previous to reach `execute`, or the script waits for earlier calls to start) change Pi's concurrency: a call stuck in an approval hook would hold up the calls after it, and a script would wait for the approval before it could end.

Scope: a call the script does not await, still running when the script ends, in `durable/codemode`: whether its tool's `execute` started before the abort. Unchanged: the call's task is `terminal` and `aborted`, an awaited call, a replayed call and every result the script sees.

Workaround: await the call before the script ends.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-18 (choice, not impossibility: the exact order needs the gate described above, or the core engine's own ordering; user impact: a call nothing awaits is aborted either way; parity cost: the gate adds a goroutine-dump poll and counts a tool blocked in a system call as working, and the simpler orderings change Pi's concurrency).

Call-site markers: `durable/codemode/call.go` (`call.launch`, the dispatch of a live call, and `call.onCall`, the dispatch of a replayed call).

Evidence: `TestALaunchedCallEntersBeforeTheNextOneIsLaunched` and `TestAReplayedCallEntersBeforeTheNextOneIsStarted` (`durable/codemode/call_test.go`) force the overtake with one processor, without a sleep: they fail in 400 of 400 runs without `spawnEntered` and pass in 300 of 300 with it. Rates of the Pi subtest `aborts a call still running when the script ends` (`TestDurableCodeMode`, GOMAXPROCS 2, 6 processes, CPUs 32-39 or 32-34 of one socket): without `spawnEntered` 36 of 12,000 (0.30%) at 8 CPUs and 573 of 3,000 (19.1%) at 3 CPUs, all `hold ran 0 times` or `no nested hold task`; with it 18 of 12,000 (0.15%) at 8 CPUs and 71 of 9,000 (0.8%) at 3 CPUs, the 26 of 3,000 first measured all `hold ran 0 times`. A trace of the remaining failures shows the earlier call admitted and started first, then its goroutine not running for 2 ms or more between two steps of its task while the later call finished.

Parity allowance: `holds.runs` of the Pi subtest `aborts a call still running when the script ends` (`durable/codemode/codemode_test.go`), and only there: 0 or 1 is accepted. The other assertions of the subtest (the script's result, the nested hold task exists, and it is `terminal` with outcome `aborted`) are unchanged.

Remove when: the core engine is the default Durable engine and orders the steps of a script's calls as Pi's single thread does, or the nested task runtime links a task to its earlier sibling so the `entered` gate before `execute` can ship; then `holds.runs` of the subtest must be 1 in 12,000 runs at 8 CPUs and 9,000 at 3 CPUs (GOMAXPROCS 2, 6 processes), and the allowance goes.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:43dd0c3e43ad9f90

## D185 A Kitty CSI-u Shift+letter with no shifted alternate decodes as the uppercase letter, where Pi keeps the base letter

What: a terminal that reports Shift+a as `ESC[97;2u`, with a base codepoint and the Shift modifier but no shifted-key field, types `A` in PiG's editor and input. Pi's `decodeKittyPrintable` types `a`, because it replaces the codepoint only when the sequence carries a shifted key. The same sequence with the shifted key (`ESC[97:65;2u`) types `A` in both, and a Shift+digit with no shifted key (`ESC[49;2u`) types `1` in both.

Why: a terminal that reports Shift+letter without the shifted alternate would otherwise lose every capital letter: the user presses Shift+a and gets `a`. PiG decodes the Shift+letter case as the user pressed it.

Scope: `DecodeKittyPrintable` (`tui/keys_decode.go`), used by `Input` and the `Editor` text path, for a Kitty CSI-u sequence whose modifier carries Shift (and no Alt or Ctrl) with a base codepoint `a` to `z` and no shifted-key field. A sequence with a shifted key, any other codepoint, the modifyOtherKeys form, `matchesKey` and `parseKey` are unchanged.

Workaround: none needed in PiG; in Pi, enable the Kitty alternate-keys report (flag 4) in the terminal so it sends the shifted key.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-20 (choice, not impossibility: PiG keeps the uppercase fallback rather than Pi's lowercase result; user impact: capital letters are typed on terminals that omit the shifted alternate; parity cost: one sequence class differs from Pi).

Call-site markers: `tui/keys_decode.go` (`DecodeKittyPrintable`, the Shift branch with no shifted key).

Evidence: `TestDecodeKittyPrintable_ShiftAndText` and `TestEditor_InsertsCapitalFromKittyCSIu` (`tui/key_csiu_text_test.go`) assert the Pi-compatible path (shifted key present) and the D185 path; `TestDecodePrintableKeyMatchesPi` (`tui/decode_printable_oracle_test.go`) runs the decoder against Pi's module over the whole sequence corpus.

Parity allowance: `TestDecodePrintableKeyMatchesPi` accepts an uppercase letter from PiG where Pi returns the lowercase letter, and only for a Kitty CSI-u sequence with the Shift modifier, no Alt or Ctrl, and no shifted-key field. `TestEditorCharacterJumpMatchesPi` leaves the CSI-u Shift+letter probe out for the same reason. Every other sequence stays byte-equal with Pi.

Remove when: Pi decodes a base-only Shift+letter as the uppercase letter, or the owner reverses the decision; then the branch, the allowance and the D185 test cases go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:dc57dfc1e8844b89

## D186 A hung-up terminal: PiG gives extensions `session_shutdown` for at most a second and exits 129; Pi's exit code and event depend on its event loop and on which event arrives first

What: when the terminal's master closes under an interactive session, the kernel sends SIGHUP and ends the session's input. Pi's SIGHUP handler (`registerSignalHandlers`, `packages/coding-agent/src/modes/interactive/interactive-mode.ts:4402-4417`) runs a graceful shutdown whose terminal writes meet EIO and take `emergencyTerminalExit` (exit 129, `:4346-4352`). Node's signal watcher does not keep the event loop alive. A session with no other referenced handle (a plain directory, no extension timers or sockets, no git watcher) has an empty loop when its input ends, and Pi exits 0 before the handler runs unless a write is still pending. Measured on Pi 1402973a, idle plain-directory hangups taken after the status render settled: 16 of 20 exited 0 and 4 exited 129. In a git repository, whose footer watches `.git/HEAD` (`footer-data-provider.ts:316`), 20 of 20 exited 129. PiG always exits 129 (`exitIfDeadTerminal`) and writes the `exit.log` line `terminal gone: <error> (exit 129)` (D102). Extensions: when Pi's SIGHUP handler runs it awaits `runtimeHost.dispose()`, which settles the interrupted run and emits `session_shutdown` with the reason `quit`, before any terminal write and without a limit (`interactive-mode.ts:4475-4496`, `agent-session-runtime.ts:415-424`, Pi 1.5.2 and main 275dde41); a write to the dead terminal during that await takes `emergencyTerminalExit` and cuts the handlers; with an empty loop Pi exits 0 with no event. PiG usually reads the end of input on the hung-up terminal before the signal (95 of 96 measured runs; in the other the SIGHUP came first) and takes one path either way: the interrupted run settles, `session_shutdown` with the reason `quit` reaches every extension once, the exit waits for the handlers for at most one second (the wait of a SIGINT, D51), then kills the extension processes and exits 129. Measured with an extension whose handler appends a line, awaits a timer and appends again, after one turn (Pi 1.5.2 and this change, 4 runs per cell; tmux kill-session, tmux kill-pane, a closed pty master as session leader and under a shell):

| Session | Pi 1.5.2 | PiG |
|---|---|---|
| idle, git repository, 200 ms handler | handler ran and its awaited line landed 16 of 16, exit 129 after 210-258 ms | handler ran and its awaited line landed 16 of 16, exit 129 after 208-244 ms |
| idle, git repository, 3 s handler | awaited in full 8 of 8, exit 129 after 3.01-3.06 s | handler started 8 of 8 and was cut at the bound 8 of 8, exit 129 after 1.01-1.04 s |
| a turn in flight, git repository, 3 s handler | handler started 8 of 8 and was cut by the next terminal write 8 of 8, awaited line never written, exit 129 after 23-28 ms | handler started 8 of 8 and ran until the bound, exit 129 after 1.00-1.02 s; with a 200 ms handler its awaited line landed 4 of 4, exit 129 after 209-213 ms |
| idle, plain directory, 200 ms handler | no event 20 of 20, exit 0 after 11-61 ms | handler ran and its awaited line landed 4 of 4, exit 129 after 210-212 ms |
| idle, plain directory, the extension holds a timer, 200 ms handler | handler ran and its awaited line landed 16 of 16, exit 129 after 211-220 ms | as the plain-directory row |
| idle, git repository, under a shell that ignores SIGHUP | no event, still running after 120 s | handler ran and its awaited line landed 4 of 4, exit 129 after 238-247 ms |

The case of filing #25 of the copiglet request (tmux kill-session; the pane's non-interactive bash runs the binary as its foreground child; a plain directory; idle after a turn) is the empty-loop row, which is why its author saw no event from Pi: in that launch Pi 1.5.2 gave no event and exit 0 in 16 of 16 (15-49 ms), and with a git repository or with an extension that holds a timer it gave the event, awaited, and exit 129 in 16 of 16 each (211-220 ms); PiG gave the event, awaited, and exit 129 in 16 of 16 in each (206-219 ms, an extension in a Node process and one fused into a Piglet Binary).

So a handler slower than one second is cut where an idle Pi waits for it; a handler of a working session finishes inside the second where Pi cuts it at once; the event is emitted where Pi's empty loop emits none; and under a shell that ignores SIGHUP, where no signal reaches Pi and it stays alive until something writes to the terminal, PiG exits 129.

Why: Pi's 0 is a timing outcome of an empty Node event loop, not a decision, and it contradicts the purpose of `emergencyTerminalExit`. PiG has no such loop whose emptiness it could reproduce. Exiting 0 on a dead terminal would leave no `exit.log` trace, which D102 forbids. This follows the owner ruling (D146) that a confirmed Pi bug is fixed whether or not Pi has it. A process whose terminal is gone cannot be seen or stopped by its user, so no handler may hold it: the one-second bound. Which of the hangup's signs PiG sees first (the end of input, a failed read, a failed write, the signal) varies from run to run, and an extension's last write must not depend on it: the event is always emitted.

Scope: a terminal that went away under an interactive session (the end of input on a hung-up output terminal, a dead-terminal error from a read or an ioctl, a failed terminal write). A live SIGHUP or SIGTERM (handlers awaited without a limit, exit 0) matches Pi and is unchanged. An idle session that holds a handle (a git repository, extension timers) matches Pi in exit code and event when a SIGHUP reaches Pi (Pi leads the session, or its shell forwards the hangup or dies of it) and its handlers finish inside the second. It differs from Pi in the empty-loop case and in the three cases above: a handler slower than a second, a working session, and a shell that ignores SIGHUP (Pi stays alive, PiG exits 129).

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-20 (the exit code of a session that no user action ended is 129 and leaves a trace, rather than a code that depends on Node's event-loop state). The `session_shutdown` emission, its one-second bound and the corrected scope were approved by the owner on 2026-11-13 ("Proceed"): the dead-terminal exit emits `session_shutdown`, waits at most the shared 1 s, and exits 129.

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/interactive_input.go` (`exitIfDeadTerminal`, `emergencyTerminalExit`).

Evidence: `36-terminal-disconnect-idle-repo` (a git repository: 129 on both sides), `10-terminal-disconnect-exit` (a working session in a plain directory: 129 on both sides) and `TestHungUpTerminalEOFExits129`. The idle plain-directory outcome is not compared, because Pi's own result varies between runs. For the event: `38-terminal-disconnect-shutdown-event` compares the idle git-repository row only (both sides: the handler's reason `quit`, its awaited line, exit 129); the other rows are held on PiG's side by `TestDeadTerminalEmitsSessionShutdownToExtensions` (tmux kill-session under a real pig, idle and with a turn in flight, 20 runs per setup, Pi's measured table in its comment) and `TestDeadTerminalExitGivesExtensionsTheirShutdownFirst` (once, bounded, an emission a SIGHUP started is joined inside the bound, a handler's failed terminal write does not start a second exit).

Parity allowance: none.

Remove when: Pi exits 129 for a hung-up terminal regardless of event-loop state.

Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 2 re-approval, the owner's dated word covers the current text; checked by Claude and Tern
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:357c1691b35168aa

## D187 A native SDK holds a batch's first host calls and responses in call and answer order, where Pi's single thread lets a call that awaits work the SDK cannot see be overtaken

What: the Go, Python and Rust extension SDKs keep, for the tool calls of one parallel batch, the orders Pi's one thread gives: the first dialog of each call opens in call order, and calls whose dialogs were answered back to back respond (and end) in the order of the answers. They hold a call to do it. A call's first host call, of any kind, waits until every earlier call of the batch has written its first host call or ended, and writing it passes the call's place on: a call that awaits an `exec` (or any other host call) before its first dialog is overtaken by the later calls' dialogs, as in Pi, where an awaited `exec` yields the thread (proved by a probe under Pi 1.3.0+1402973a: the call that awaits `pi.exec` before its dialog opens it after the later call's, 8 of 8 runs). A call whose dialog was answered holds the responses of the calls answered after it until it returns or makes its next host call. In Pi a call that awaits real work the SDK cannot see before its first host call (a `fetch`, a timer, a child process of its own) also yields the thread, so a later call's dialog opens first; and a continuation that awaits again yields at once. A native handler's blocking work (an HTTP request, a sleep, a wait on a channel) is invisible to the SDK, so the SDK holds the later calls' first host calls until the earlier handler reaches its first host call or its return, and holds the responses of the calls answered after a handler until it makes a host call or returns. The orders equal Pi's single-thread orders whenever the handlers await only host calls; a handler that awaits work the SDK cannot see makes a later call's dialog open after the handler's own, or a later response be written after the handler's, where Pi's thread would have put them first.

The same holds for the file mutation queue: the Context form of `withFileMutationQueue` (`Context.WithFileMutationQueue` in Go, `ctx.with_file_mutation_queue` in Rust and Python, `pig:ctx-with-file-mutation-queue` in Lisp, `ctx.withFileMutationQueue` in Node) names its tool call in `fileMutation.enter` (`FileMutationEnterArgs.Request`), and the host registers it in the queue only after the start windows of the extension tools before that call in its batch closed (`Conn.awaitEarlierWindows`; the windows are the ones row "file mutation queue registration order in a parallel batch" of `docs/extension-api-parity.md` already uses for the edit and write calls). A tool's window is open until its first host call that returns a Promise in Pi (an exec, a dialog, a model call, the enter itself), its answer or its end, so two tools of a batch that make only getter calls before they take the queue (Pi: no await, so both prefixes run in call order) register in call order however many round trips their getters took, and a tool that awaits an `exec` first registers after the one that did not, as in Pi. Pi's free function, which the SDKs keep unchanged, carries no tool call and registers in the order the calls reach the host, which a native SDK's scheduler and round trips decide; a Pi extension that needs Pi's order in a native SDK uses the Context form. The Node runtime is Pi's one thread, where both forms give Pi's order. A native handler that does work of its own, with no host call, before it takes the queue through its Context holds the registrations of the tools behind it for as long as the work lasts (the cost docs/extension-api-parity.md names for the edit and write calls behind a native tool).

Why: Pi runs every extension on one thread, so the order of a batch's synchronous prefixes and of its continuations is a fact of the language, and no hand-off is needed (`agent-loop.ts:586-647`, `rpc-mode.ts:91-130, 771-777`). A native SDK runs each request on a thread of its own, and the OS scheduler decides which first dialog is written first and which of two back-to-back answers a handler resumes on first, so without a gate a port opens its second dialog first and ends its calls in no order. The gate can only be placed at the points the SDK sees: its host calls and the end of the handler. It cannot know that a handler is blocked in its own work, so it cannot release the later calls at the moment Pi's thread would. The owner chose the ordering with this latency cost over leaving the orders to the scheduler.

Scope: the file mutation queue registrations made through the Context form by the tool calls of one parallel batch, in every SDK, isolated, packed and fused; and the tool calls of one parallel batch whose handlers make a host call (a dialog, `ui.select`, `ui.confirm`, `ui.input`, `ui.editor`, `ui.custom`, or any other), in the Go, Python and Rust SDKs, isolated and packed; only a dialog call opens a response slot, so a batch whose handlers open no dialog has no response gate, and the first host call of any kind takes part in the dialog place. Unchanged: the Node SDK, which one thread orders as Pi's does; a batch whose handlers make no host call (no slot, no queue wait); calls of other batches, other sessions' calls (a subagent's tool calls on the same connection) and nested calls, which take part in no order (the host names the batch on each `tool_call` request, and a request without one is ungated); the start order of the handlers, which the SDKs already kept; and the tool-result messages, which follow call order.

Workaround: start the work that blocks as a host call (`exec`, `getFlag`, any call the SDK sees): the first host call passes the call's place on, so the later calls' dialogs open while it runs, as in Pi; or open the dialog before blocking work.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-20; the file mutation queue extension approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-11-07 (choice, not impossibility: the SDK cannot observe a handler's own blocking work; user impact: only the moment a later call's dialog opens or its response is written, never their order unless the handler awaits work the SDK cannot see; parity cost: a native handler that blocks, outside a host call, before its first host call delays the later calls' dialogs of its batch).

Call-site markers: `extensions/sdk/tool_start_order.go` (`toolStart.beforeCall`, the first-host-call wait, and `toolStart.respond`, the response gate), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` (`_ToolStart.before_call` and `_ToolStart.respond`), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/tool_start_order.rs` (`ToolStart::before_call` and `ToolStart::respond`), and for the queue `coding/extension/host/subprocess/host_file_mutation.go` (`handleFileMutationEnter`).

Evidence: `TestToolBatchDialogsOpenInCallOrderAndResumeInAnswerOrderAcrossSDKs` (`test/extension-conformance`) fails for every native realization without the gates (the Go dialogs open as `[T0 T2 T3 T4 T5 T1]`, the ends follow no answer order) and passes for all nine with them; its last rounds make the first call await an `exec`, held until the other five dialogs are open, and fail for every native realization that keeps the dialog place across it (none of the later dialogs opens while the `exec` runs); the gate tests `TestToolDialogsOpenInCallOrder`, `TestToolFirstHostCallOfAnyKindPassesTheDialogPlace`, `TestToolResponsesFollowTheOrderTheAnswersWereRead` and `TestToolBatchOrdersAreIndependentAcrossBatches` (`extensions/sdk/tool_dialog_order_test.go`), `test_tool_dialog_order.py` (`extensions/sdk-py/tests`) and the `tool_start_order` and `tool_dispatch_wire` tests (`extensions/sdk-rs`) pin the held orders without a sleep and the independence of batches, including that a long call with no host call in one batch never holds another batch's dialog.

Queue evidence: `TestFileMutationOrderOfTheContextFormAcrossSDKs` (`test/extension-conformance`): two calls of one batch, one making four getter calls before it takes the queue through its Context and one none, run through a Session in every realization; it fails for every native realization with the hold removed (the second call registers first) and passes with it.

Parity allowance: none: no Pi scenario asserts the moment a later call's dialog opens while an earlier call blocks.

Remove when: a native SDK's handlers run where a blocked handler yields to the next one (an async handler model with the SDK's scheduler), or the SDK is told when a handler blocks, so a later call's dialog or response is released at the point Pi's thread would release it; then the gates in the three SDKs and this record go.

Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 2 re-approval, the owner's dated word covers the current text; checked by Claude and Tern
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:57a4f64ba0cb9cee

## D190 The built-in file mutation queue also orders mutations of one existing file reached through a hard link or a case alias Pi's realpath key keeps apart

What: Pi's `withFileMutationQueue` (`packages/coding-agent/src/core/tools/file-mutation-queue.ts`) keys each call by `realpath(resolve(path))`, and a path that does not exist keeps its resolved name. PiG's queue (`FileMutationQueue`, behind the `edit` and `write` tools and `WithFileMutationQueue`) keeps that key and also makes a call wait for every earlier call that is still queued for the same existing file under another key (`os.SameFile`): the hard links of a file, and the case aliases of a file on a case-insensitive filesystem. Pi runs those calls at once. On macOS Pi's native `realpath` returns the on-disk case, so Pi orders case aliases there (reported from the #270 author's run of the pinned Pi on macOS; not rerun here), while Go's `filepath.EvalSymlinks` keeps the name as typed; the identity check restores that order and is parity on macOS. Pi's probe on Linux (pinned `1402973a`, `fs.promises.realpath` through glibc): two calls through a hard link both start while the first is held, and so do two calls through `Target.txt` and `target.txt` on an ext4 casefold directory.

Why: both edits of two parallel `edit` calls through two names of one file read the same original text and each writes the whole file, so the later write silently drops the earlier edit while both calls report success.

Pi bug: the same loss in Pi (an `edit` call is read, replace, write of the whole file). Pi's queue is meant to "serialize file mutation operations targeting the same file" and does not for a hard link.

Scope: `FileMutationQueue.Reserve` in `internal/codingagent/tools/mutation_queue.go`, so the `edit` and `write` tools, the parallel tool dispatcher's reservations and `WithFileMutationQueue` share it. The key is still Pi's; the identity check only adds waits, in call order, on tickets of the same existing file. A path that does not exist when the call is queued keeps path ordering (a file a queued write creates is recognised when a later call is queued). Different files still run in parallel. The queue orders the mutations of this process only; another process, and a rename or link made behind the queue, are outside it, as in Pi. The Go, Python and Rust SDKs' own `withFileMutationQueue` is not this queue and is unchanged.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-21 (hard-link alias serialization beyond Pi's realpath keying, to prevent lost edits).

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/tools/mutation_queue.go` (`FileMutationQueue.Reserve`, the predecessor selection).

Evidence: `TestEditPreservesParallelAliasChanges` (`internal/codingagent/tools/mutation_queue_alias_test.go`) holds the first call's read until the second call has reached its queue wait or finished, then requires both edits through both names; its `hardlink` and `case` subtests fail without the identity check (the file ends as `ALPHA\nbeta\n`) and its `symlink` and `same` controls pass either way. `TestMutationAliasReservationsKeepDispatchOrder` checks the dispatcher's reservation order across an alias, for a file that exists and for one created while queued, and that a drained queue keeps no state.

Parity allowance: `TestHardLinkAliasesDifferFromPi` runs the pinned Pi's queue and PiG's over two hard links of one file: Pi starts both callbacks (event counts 2, 3, 4), PiG starts the second after the first ends. The 120-run paired corpus `TestWithFileMutationQueueMatchesPi` has no hard link or case alias and is unchanged.

Remove when: Pi orders hard-link aliases (and, on a case-insensitive filesystem where its `realpath` keeps case, case aliases) by file identity, or an owner decision reverses this one; then the identity check, its marker, the allowance test and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:21aa0f4d5ebde765

## D195 An empty AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK is no token, where Pi's AWS SDK treats the variable as set and the request fails before it is sent

What: with `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK` exported but empty, PiG's Bedrock provider ignores it and signs the request with SigV4 and the credentials it finds (static keys, a profile, the default chain, or the dummy keys of `AWS_BEDROCK_SKIP_AUTH=1`). Pi 1.4.0 sends nothing: its own lookup treats an empty value as unset (`getProviderEnvValue`, `packages/ai/src/utils/provider-env.ts:45-51`; `packages/ai/src/api/bedrock-converse-stream.ts:185-191`, so `skipAuth` stays on and no `config.token` is set at `:242-245`), but the AWS JavaScript SDK chooses bearer auth for a variable that is merely present (`@aws-sdk/core` `NODE_AUTH_SCHEME_PREFERENCE_OPTIONS.environmentVariableSelector`: `if (bearerTokenKey in env) return ["httpBearerAuth"]`, and `@aws-sdk/token-providers` `fromEnvSigningName`: `bearerTokenKey in process.env`, reading the empty value), so the stream ends with "request could not be signed with `token` since the `token` is not defined". The difference shows with a workflow or a shell profile that maps an unset secret to an empty variable.

Why: an empty variable names no credential. Failing every request because an unset secret was exported as an empty string breaks a working IAM or profile setup for no reason a user can act on, and PiG's own lookup, like Pi's, already treats the value as absent. The Go SDK PiG uses agrees: `resolveEnvBearerToken` returns on an empty value (`third_party/awssdk/service/bedrockruntime/api_client.go:980-984`).

Pi bug: the failure is an AWS JavaScript SDK side effect that Pi's own `getProviderEnvValue` does not intend. Pi decides "no token" and the SDK decides "bearer".

Scope: `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK` set to the empty string in the process environment (the AWS SDKs read only that one), on the `bedrock-converse-stream` API, with or without `AWS_BEDROCK_SKIP_AUTH`, static keys or a profile. An unset variable and a non-empty one behave as in Pi: the first signs with SigV4, the second sends `Authorization: Bearer <token>` (the case over `http://` with skip-auth is D196).

Workaround: none needed in PiG. In Pi, unset the variable instead of exporting it empty.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-24 (record the difference, do not mirror the SDK's presence check: a stray empty variable must not break a working credential setup).

Call-site markers: `ai/providers/bedrock/bedrock.go` (`loadBedrockConfig`, the bearer token selection).

Evidence: `TestBedrockBearerTokenDivergencesFromPi` (`ai/providers/bedrock/bedrock_bearer_divergence_test.go`) runs Pi's `bedrock-converse-stream` through the Node oracle and PiG's provider against a loopback server with the variable empty, under skip-auth and under static keys: Pi sends nothing and ends with the signing error, PiG sends a SigV4 request; its controls (variable unset, with keys) agree. The test fails when either side changes.

Parity allowance: none needed beyond that test. The paired Bedrock oracles (`TestBedrockRequestBodyMatchesPi` and the others) scrub every `AWS_*` variable before they run Pi, so they do not see the difference.

Remove when: Pi or its AWS SDK treats an empty `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK` as unset (Pi passes `authSchemePreference` itself, or the SDK tests for a non-empty value), or an owner decision reverses this one; then the marker, the D195 cases of the test and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:7d47af3511db1d57

## D196 Under skip-auth a bearer token from the process environment is not sent over an http:// endpoint, where Pi's AWS SDK sends it

What: with `AWS_BEDROCK_SKIP_AUTH=1` and a non-empty `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK` in the process environment, and a model `baseUrl` that is `http://` (a local proxy or a gateway that "needs no authentication"), PiG's request fails before it is sent with "bearer token with HTTP request requires HTTPS". Pi 1.4.0 sends it with `Authorization: Bearer <token>` over plain HTTP. In both products the AWS SDK selects bearer auth from the variable although `AWS_BEDROCK_SKIP_AUTH=1` asked for dummy keys (Pi: `packages/ai/src/api/bedrock-converse-stream.ts:185-191` sets no `config.token` under skip-auth, `@aws-sdk/core` `NODE_AUTH_SCHEME_PREFERENCE_OPTIONS.environmentVariableSelector` picks `httpBearerAuth` and `@aws-sdk/token-providers` `fromEnvSigningName` reads the variable; PiG: `resolveEnvBearerToken`, `third_party/awssdk/service/bedrockruntime/api_client.go:980-984`). The difference is the signer: PiG's own bearer path installs `bedrockBearerSigner`, which sends the header over HTTP as Pi's SDK does, but only when `loadBedrockConfig` set a token, which it never does under skip-auth, so the SDK's default signer applies and refuses a request that is not HTTPS (`smithy-go` `auth/bearer/middleware.go:97`, `SignHTTPSMessage.SignWithBearerToken`). Without skip-auth, or over `https://`, PiG and Pi send the same header.

Why: the refusal keeps a bearer token off an unencrypted connection. Pi's behavior sends it in clear text to a host the user chose; PiG does not override the Go SDK's guard to repeat that.

Pi bug: Pi sends a credential over plain HTTP because the JavaScript SDK has no HTTPS check for bearer tokens, and its skip-auth option does not stop the SDK from picking up the token.

Scope: `AWS_BEDROCK_SKIP_AUTH=1` with `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK` non-empty in the process environment and an `http://` `baseUrl`, on the `bedrock-converse-stream` API. A token passed in the provider environment, as `bearerToken` or as the API key, is not read under skip-auth by either product's own code, and takes PiG's HTTP-tolerant path when skip-auth is off.

Workaround: none needed in PiG. Use an `https://` endpoint, or unset the variable. In Pi, the same two steps avoid sending the token in clear text.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-24 (record the difference, do not mirror: PiG keeps the Go SDK's HTTPS-only bearer guard where skip-auth leaves the token to the SDK).

Call-site markers: `ai/providers/bedrock/bedrock.go` (`loadBedrockConfig`, the bearer token selection; `bedrockClientOptions`, the bearer signer).

Evidence: `TestBedrockBearerTokenDivergencesFromPi` (`ai/providers/bedrock/bedrock_bearer_divergence_test.go`) runs Pi through the Node oracle and PiG's provider against an `http://` loopback server with skip-auth and a token: Pi's request arrives with `Authorization: Bearer tok`, PiG sends nothing and ends with the HTTPS error; its control (a token without skip-auth over `http://`) sends `Bearer tok` in both. The test fails when either side changes.

Parity allowance: none needed beyond that test. The paired Bedrock oracles scrub every `AWS_*` variable before they run Pi.

Remove when: Pi refuses to send a bearer token over HTTP (its SDK adds an HTTPS check, or Pi installs a signer that does), or PiG installs `bedrockBearerSigner` under skip-auth and the owner approves sending the token over HTTP there; then the markers, the D196 case of the test and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:19e7e5a0c65a6cdc

## D197 The env daemon's limit of open files is the hard limit, where Pi's daemon keeps the soft limit it inherited

What: PiG's env daemon (`env/daemon`) runs under the Go runtime, which raises the soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` of the process to the hard limit (on Linux to one below it, `syscall/rlimit.go`) before `main` starts. Pi's daemon (`packages/env/daemon`, Rust) never touches the limit, so it runs under the soft limit the client's shell passed on. On a default shell (soft limit 1024) Pi's daemon answers an `open`, `write` or `opendir` with `EMFILE` once about 1020 handles are open (each handle holds one descriptor), where PiG's daemon goes on until the hard limit or its own handle table (4096 handles, the same `EMFILE` Pi's daemon reports at that count, `packages/env/daemon/src/main.rs:42-43` and `:116-120`) stops it. A client that opens fewer handles than the soft limit sees no difference. The commands the daemon starts are not affected: Go restores the inherited limit for a child process.

Why: nothing in PiG reaches the limit on purpose. The Go runtime's raise is the toolchain's own behavior (every Go program on Unix has it), and undoing it to give a program Pi's smaller limit would make PiG fail on workloads that succeed, in a daemon whose handle table already caps the count.

Pi bug: none. Pi's daemon leaves the limit alone (no `setrlimit` call in any file of `packages/env/daemon/src`) and maps the system's `EMFILE` as it should (`src/sys/unix.rs:35`). The two counts differ because the two runtimes differ.

Scope: Unix hosts only (Windows has no such limit), and only a daemon started with a soft limit below both its hard limit and 4096. On macOS the Go runtime raises the soft limit to the hard limit or `kern.maxfilesperproc`, whichever is lower. Everything else, including the handle table limit and its message, is equal to Pi's (`TestDaemonHandlesAreLimitedLikePisDaemon`).

Workaround: none needed in PiG. Pi's count cannot be reproduced in PiG: the runtime raises the limit again whatever the daemon inherits.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-24 (record the difference, do not lower the limit to Pi's).

Call-site markers: `env/daemon/server.go` (`Serve`, where the daemon's connection starts).

Evidence: `TestDaemonDescriptorLimitIsTheHardLimitUnlikePisDaemon` (`env/daemon_descriptor_limit_divergence_test.go`) starts Pi's daemon (built from the pin) and PiG's with a soft limit of 64 and the host's hard limit, and opens directories until one fails: Pi's refuses with `EMFILE` before 64, PiG opens all 300. `TestDescriptorLimitIsTheHardLimit` (`env/daemon/descriptor_limit_linux_test.go`) pins that the runtime leaves PiG's soft limit at the hard limit or one below it. The first fails when either daemon changes its behavior, the second when the runtime stops raising the limit.

Parity allowance: the wire tests that open thousands of handles (`TestDaemonHandlesAreLimitedLikePisDaemon`) start both daemons with the soft limit raised to the hard limit (`startWireDaemon` in `env/daemon_wire_oracle_test.go`), so the count under test is the handle table's, equal on both sides.

Remove when: the Go runtime lets a program keep its inherited soft limit, or Pi's daemon raises its own soft limit to the hard limit at start, or an owner decision reverses this one; then the marker, the two tests and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:866cc87d371341c6

## D198 The Go, Rust, Python and Lisp `Type` has no Codec family (`Codec`, `Decode`, `Encode`, `DecodeBuilder`, `EncodeBuilder`, `IsCodec`)

What: pi-ai's `Type` is TypeBox 1.3.27's builder namespace. The Go (`sdk.Type`), Rust (`pig_sdk::Type`), Python (`pig_sdk.Type`) and Lisp ports serve its schema constructors and guards, but not the six members that attach a JavaScript decode/encode callback pair to a schema (`Codec(type).Decode(f).Encode(g)`, `Decode(type, f)`, `Encode(type, g)`, the `DecodeBuilder` and `EncodeBuilder` classes, and the guard `IsCodec`). They are absent: a call fails to compile in Go and Rust and raises `AttributeError` in Python, and the Lisp SDK defines no such function. The Node runtime serves TypeBox's own and behaves as in Pi.

Why: a Codec's callbacks have no consumer outside `Value.Decode` and `Value.Encode` (`typebox/build/value/codec/callback.mjs`, the only reader of `~codec`), which pi-ai does not export (`ai/src/index.ts` exports `Static`, `TSchema` and `Type` from typebox) and no SDK serves. Pi's own tool-argument validation does not call them (at Pi b556cfe5, `ai/src/utils/validation.ts:15,59` compiles the schema with pi-schema and calls `parse`). A port could store the callbacks, but nothing in any SDK or in the host could ever call them, which is an inert member that accepts the call and does nothing.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: the six members above only, in `extensions/sdk/type_schema.go`, `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/type_schema.py` and `extensions/sdk-rs/src/type_schema.rs`. Every other member of `Type` is ported and tested against Pi's own answers (`test/parity/sdk-vectors/pi-ai/Type.json`); the schemas `Type` builds are plain JSON on the wire, so a schema that a Node extension builds with a Codec registers the same JSON it would without one (`~codec` is a hidden member that JSON drops).

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-24 (Codec family of `Type`: numbered divergence; `Refine` and `IsRefine` are not part of it: the SDKs run an extension tool's refinements themselves).
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:cf9843804c33e01e

Amendment 2026-11-02 (owner decision of the same day): the approval above (2026-10-24) was given when the SDKs ran an extension tool's refinements themselves. Pi 1.5.2 no longer runs them (`ai/CHANGELOG.md:66`, `coding-agent/docs/extensions.md:176`: "`Type.Refine()` refinements are not run"), and this branch removed the SDK refinement round trip to match. `Refine` and `IsRefine` therefore stay schema constructors whose refinements no tool-argument validation runs, as in Pi. They are not part of D198: the owner approved a separate record for them, D226 (Go, Rust, Python and Lisp `Type.Refine` store a callback that no API runs).

Remove when: an SDK serves `Value.Decode`/`Value.Encode` (or a host call runs a codec) so a stored callback pair has a consumer.

Call-site markers: `extensions/sdk/type_schema.go` (`Type`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/type_schema.py` (module doc), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/type_schema.rs` (module doc), `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/type-schema.lisp` (file header).

Evidence: `TestTypeOmitsTheCodecFamily` (`extensions/sdk/type_vectors_test.go`), `test_type_omits_the_codec_family` (`extensions/sdk-py/tests/test_pi_ai_type_vectors.py`) and `type-omits-the-codec-family` (`extensions/sdk-lisp/tests/type-tests.lisp`) require that the six members do not exist; the Rust members are absent by construction (`type_schema.rs` doctest `compile_fail`). The Node row of `TestConformance_TypeSchemaToolParametersAgreeAcrossSDKs` (`test/extension-conformance/type_schema_registration_test.go`) runs TypeBox's own `Type`.

## D199 PiG lists OpenRouter's :nitro and :floor routing variants beside every OpenRouter model, where Pi lists only what OpenRouter's /models returns

What: for every `openrouter` chat model of Pi's catalog that does not already end in a routing variant, PiG also lists `<id>:nitro` and `<id>:floor` (named `<name> (nitro)` and `<name> (floor)`), right after the model, and resolves a lookup of either. The variants are derived from the base entry when listed or looked up, from the current OpenRouter list: the bundled catalog, or the list `pig update --models` saved, which replaces it (the saved list never holds a derived variant); the bundled catalog (`ai.GeneratedModels`) stays exactly Pi's. A variant is a copy of its base entry in everything but its id, name and JSON record: API, base URL, prices, limits, input types, reasoning and thinking levels are the base model's. `--list-models`, the model selector, `/scoped-models`, RPC `get_available_models` and the SDKs list them like any other built-in model.

Why: OpenRouter accepts `:nitro` (providers sorted by throughput, priority service tier endpoints admitted) and `:floor` (providers sorted by price, flex service tier endpoints admitted) on every model ID but never lists them in `GET /api/v1/models`, which is where Pi's catalog comes from, so a user cannot pick them in Pi's selector (issue #315). OpenRouter's variants page (https://openrouter.ai/docs/guides/routing/model-variants/overview, "Offering routing variants in a model picker") tells clients to derive them from the base entries and to read the metadata from the base entry.

Scope: one rule, `ai/openrouter_variants.go` (`OpenRouterRoutingVariants`, `OpenRouterVariant`, `WithOpenRouterRoutingVariants`, and `WithOpenRouterRoutingVariantModels` for a saved list), data-driven from OpenRouter's documented suffix list; no model is named. Used by `ai.ListModels` (OpenRouter and the whole catalog), `ai.LookupModel` and `ai.LookupModelExact` (a variant the catalog does not list is derived from its base and cached), `builtinChatCatalogListed` (the registry's selector, `--list-models`, RPC and SDK lists; built on first use), `builtinChatCatalogListing` (`RuntimeModels` and `GetAllModelDataSources`, which read each listed model's id and name from `ai.OpenRouterListing`, the walk that builds the list, and build no variant, so a caller that only resolves a model, or reads the available models of a registry without OpenRouter auth, builds none until one is selected), `GetProviderModelDataByID` and the model reference resolver (`withNamedRoutingVariants`: a reference that names `:nitro` or `:floor` finds the variant of the model it means). A partial model text (`--model 4o`, scoped-model patterns, the resolver's fuzzy match) is not matched against a variant unless the text itself names `:nitro` or `:floor`, so every text Pi resolves resolves to the same model here (Pi's reverse-collation tie break would otherwise prefer `:nitro`). The listed price is the base model's rate; OpenRouter bills a request at the rate of the endpoint that served it (a priority endpoint for `:nitro`, a flex endpoint for `:floor`). Image and classifier models, other providers, the remote catalog overlay and the other routing variants (`:exacto`, `:online`) are unchanged.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-24 (issue #315, an additive extension that overlaps Pi's catalog).
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

Call-site markers: `ai/openrouter_variants.go` (`OpenRouterVariant`), `ai/registry.go` (`ListModels`, `LookupModel`, `LookupModelExact`), `internal/codingagent/builtin_chat_catalog.go` (`builtinChatCatalogListed`), `internal/codingagent/model_catalog_data.go` (`GetProviderModelData`, `GetProviderModelDataByID`), `internal/codingagent/model_registry.go` (`ResolveRemoteCatalogModel`), `internal/codingagent/remote_catalog_provider.go` (`withPersonalizedCatalogOverlay`), `internal/codingagent/model_pattern.go` (`withNamedRoutingVariants`, `fuzzyMatches`).

Evidence: `TestRefreshedOpenRouterProviderListsRoutingVariantsOfTheRefreshedList`, `TestSavedOpenRouterListShowsRoutingVariantsOnEveryRegistryListing` (`internal/codingagent/remote_catalog_openrouter_variants_test.go`) and `TestRegistryPersonalizesTheOpenRouterCatalogThroughTheConfiguredBaseURL` pin the refreshed list; `TestOpenRouterRoutingVariantsMirrorTheirBaseEntries`, `TestOpenRouterRoutingVariantsAreLookedUpFromTheirBase` and `TestRewriteRecordIdentity` (`ai/openrouter_variants_test.go`) pin the rule from OpenRouter's documentation: both variants after each base entry, equal to the base but for id, name and record, found by lookup, none on another provider, none stored in `GeneratedModels`; `TestAvailableModelDataDerivesRoutingVariantsOnlyForAConfiguredOpenRouter` and `TestSelectorModelLookupBuildsOnlyTheModelRead` (`internal/codingagent`) pin that a variant is derived only where it is listed or named, and `TestPartialModelTextIgnoresOpenRouterRoutingVariants` (`internal/codingagent/model_pattern_openrouter_variants_test.go`) pins the resolver rule against the failure the paired `TestResolveCliModelAndScopeMatchPi` showed when a variant was a candidate (`--model 4o` resolved to `:nitro`, Pi to `:batch`).

Parity allowance: the bundled catalog and every comparison of it with Pi (`TestCatalogModelJSONMatchesPiForEveryBuiltinModel`, `TestGeneratedCatalogsMatchOraclePi`, `TestModelObjectsMatchPisModelObjects`) are unchanged. The tests that compare a provider list with Pi's (`TestBuiltinCatalogsMatchOracleProviderData`, `TestProviderCatalogExportsMatchOracleData`, the Anthropic catalog denominators, `TestModelRegistryMatchesPi`) leave the derived variants out of the comparison. `TestResolveCliModelAndScopeMatchPi` is unchanged and still equal to Pi.

Remove when: Pi lists the routing variants (or OpenRouter lists them in `/models`), or the owner reverses the decision; then the derivation in `ai/openrouter_variants.go`, its markers, the allowance in the oracle test and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:15bf08a790edf797

## D200 With no home directory PiG still runs the commands that load no resources, where Pi crashes at import, and fails with Pi's error where Pi's home lookup would run

What: on a host where Node's `os.homedir()` throws (no `HOME`, and no entry for the process's user in the system's user database: a container run as a uid its passwd file does not list), the pinned Pi 1.4.0 fails to load in every mode, `--version` and `list` included. It exits 1 with Node's uncaught `SystemError [ERR_SYSTEM_ERROR]: A system error occurred: uv_os_homedir returned ENOENT (no such file or directory)`: `tools-manager.ts:11` evaluates `getBinDir()` at import, `getAgentDir()` expands even an explicit `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` through `normalizePath`, and `normalizePath` calls `homedir()` before it looks at its input (`utils/paths.ts:89`). PiG runs `--version`, `list` and every other command that loads no resources, and fails with the same message (exit 1, nothing on stdout) only where Pi's `resolve()` would look the home directory up, `getHomeDir()` at `package-manager.ts:2464`: startup in print, RPC and interactive mode, a reload and `pig config`.

Why: PiG's config root (`PIG_HOME`, else `XDG_CONFIG_HOME/pig`, else `~/.pig`, internal/configroot) is meant to run without a home directory, as containers and CI jobs do; refusing `--version` and `list` there would remove the one thing `PIG_HOME` is for, and nothing in those commands needs a home directory.

Pi bug: none claimed; Pi's behaviour is a crash at load rather than a reported error, and is kept for the paths that need a home directory.

Scope: `PackageManagerHomeDir` (`internal/codingagent/resource_paths.go`) is Pi's `getHomeDir` and returns Node's error where Node's call throws (`internal/nodehome`); `ValidateAutoDiscoveredOverrides` returns it first, at the position of `package-manager.ts:2464`, so startup, a reload and the config command fail there with exit status 1 and nothing on stdout. The collectors (`AgentsSkillScan`, `AmbientSkillResources`) list no user `~/.agents/skills` when asked without a home directory. PiG's other lenient home lookups (`resolvepath`'s `~` expansion, `configroot.ExpandTilde`) are not part of this record and unchanged. The record's condition is the host's, not an option: with a home directory PiG and Pi behave alike.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-24 (keep `--version`, `list` and resource-free modes running where Pi crashes at import; fail with Pi's error where Pi's home lookup runs).

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/resource_paths.go` (`PackageManagerHomeDir`, `ValidateAutoDiscoveredOverrides`).

Evidence: `TestNoHomeDirectoryPiCrashesAtImportWherePiGRunsResourceFreeCommands` (`coding/cli/no_home_directory_test.go`) runs the pinned Pi (`pi-coding-agent/dist/cli.js` with `os.homedir()` made to throw Node's error, so it runs on any host) and requires exit status 1, Node's message on stderr and an empty stdout for `--version` and `list`, then runs PiG's `--version` and `list` with no home directory and requires them to succeed. `TestStartupFailsWhereNoHomeDirectoryExists` requires print and RPC startup to exit 1 with that message and an empty stdout; `TestStartupFollowsNodeWhenThereIsNoHomeDirectory` runs the binary against `node -p "require('os').homedir()"` with `HOME` removed on the host itself, and on a host with no passwd entry for the uid (checked on xd670: Pi `--version`, `--mode rpc` and `-p` all exit 1 with the SystemError, PiG `--version` and `list` exit 0 with `PIG_HOME` set).

Parity allowance: `TestNoHomeDirectoryPiCrashesAtImportWherePiGRunsResourceFreeCommands` is the allowance: the Pi half pins the crash, the PiG half pins the run. No paired corpus runs without a home directory, so none changes.

Remove when: Pi stops resolving the home directory at import (so `--version` and `list` run there without one), or an owner decision reverses this one; then PiG's startup fails at the same point as Pi's, the markers, the allowance test and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:b71c7bd53225888d

## D203 registerProvider with an empty `apiKey` leaves the provider unconfigured where Pi treats the empty string as a configured key

What: Pi's `registerProvider(id, { apiKey: "" })` (the extension registration of `ModelRuntime` and `ModelRegistry`) keeps the empty string as the provider's configured raw key: `configuredApiKey` is `extension?.apiKey ?? config?.apiKey` (`packages/coding-agent/src/core/provider-composer.ts:397`), and `""` is not `undefined`. `configuredRequestAuthStatus` then reports `{ configured: true, source: "fallback" }` (`provider-composer.ts:723-736`, the `"fallback"` source is the extension's literal key), and the composed API-key `check` returns a configured check (`provider-composer.ts:450-457`: an empty key has no environment names to miss). After the availability refresh settles, `hasConfiguredAuth(provider)` is true, `getProviderAuthStatus` reads configured, and the provider's models are available. PiG reads an empty `apiKey` as no key (the registration's `APIKey` is a Go string, and `""` means unset on every path that reads it): the provider stays unconfigured and its models unavailable until a stored credential, an environment variable or a non-empty key supplies one.

Why: Pi's behaviour is an accident of `??` on a string. The empty string is not a form the option documents (`packages/coding-agent/src/core/extensions/types.ts:1936`: "API key literal, env interpolation ($ENV_VAR or ${ENV_VAR}), or leading !command"), and Pi treats `""` as missing where it validates the same option (`provider-composer.ts:318`, `!config.apiKey`). Its effect is a provider that reads configured and available whose every request fails with `No API key for provider: <id>` (for example `packages/ai/src/api/openai-completions.ts:95`), so an extension that passes `process.env.KEY ?? ""` gets a selectable provider that cannot answer. Distinguishing `""` from an absent key needs presence tracking on the public `ProviderConfigInput.APIKey` string and on the models.json path, for a quirk no caller can use.

Pi bug: the inconsistency above: `""` is a configured key to `configuredApiKey`, `configuredRequestAuthStatus` and the composed `check`, and a missing one to `provider-composer.ts:318`.

Scope: the extension registration (`ModelRuntime.RegisterProvider`, `ModelRegistry.registerProvider` over every SDK, `ProviderConfigInput.APIKey`) and the configured-auth reads that follow it: `ConfiguredRequestAuthStatus`, the registration's request auth, and `mergeProviderConfigInput`, whose merge keeps the earlier key when a re-registration passes `""` (Pi's merge keeps the earlier key only for `undefined`, `model-runtime.ts:921-933`). A non-empty key, `$VAR` and `!command` keys behave as in Pi.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-25 (record, no port).

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/model_configured_auth_status.go` (`ConfiguredRequestAuthStatus`), `internal/codingagent/extension_model_registry.go` (the registration's request auth and its configured status), `internal/codingagent/native_model_runtime.go` (`mergeProviderConfigInput`).

Evidence: `TestRegisterProviderWithEmptyAPIKeyIsNotConfigured` (`coding/register_provider_empty_apikey_test.go`) registers one provider with `APIKey: ""` and one with a literal key: after the refresh settles the first is unconfigured and its status unconfigured, the second configured. Pi's side was read through the session-graph `ModelRegistry` over Pi's own `ModelRuntime` (the Node row of `TestSessionGraphModelRegistryAcrossSDKs`): `{ configured: true, source: "fallback" }`, available `true`, for `apiKey: ""`.

Parity allowance: no parity scenario registers a provider with an empty `apiKey`.

Remove when: Pi treats `""` as missing in `configuredApiKey`, or the SDK API gains explicit presence for `apiKey` (a pointer or an option) and an owner decision asks for Pi's behaviour; then the three markers, the test and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:79d224216e8eeb63

## D205 Fullscreen frames scroll the rows that only moved

What: when the transcript grows by a line or is scrolled, every row of the fullscreen viewport changes, though all but a few rows only moved. Pi 1.4.0, whose default `tuiMode` is fullscreen, clears and writes every row that differs from the previous frame (`packages/tui/src/tui-alt-screen.ts` `doRender`). PiG finds the distance at which the most rows of the changed part of the screen are rows of the previous frame. It scrolls that part inside a scroll region (`CSI top;bottom r`, then line feeds at its bottom or reverse index at its top, then `CSI r`) and draws only the rows that differ after the move. A frame scrolls only when this saves at least three row writes and no row of the moved part holds an image, because images are placed by their own sequences. The screen after every frame is the one Pi's frame shows, except for a component row wider than the screen (the known visible difference below); the bytes differ. A 20,000-character answer streamed into a 160x48 tmux pane made PiG write 3,578 KB to the terminal; with rows scrolled it writes 544 KB, and the tmux server's CPU for that answer falls from 0.97 s to 0.23 s (medians of four runs). `PIG_TUI_SCROLL_ROWS=0` draws every changed row, as Pi does.

Why: the bytes are work for the terminal, and traffic for a multiplexer or an ssh connection between PiG and the screen. The technique comes from Pi-Bolt (MIT; see THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md).

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-27, contingent on terminal-compatibility proof, and confirmed the same day with that proof. The proof: a terminal PiG does not trust with scroll regions (`TERM` unset, `dumb`, `unknown` or a terminal with no scroll-region capability such as `vt52`, or Zellij) and `PIG_TUI_SCROLL_ROWS=0` get Pi's bytes (`scrollRegionsUsable` in `tui/tui_alt_screen_scroll.go`); the tmux parity families that touch the alternate screen (`interactive-rendering`, `fullscreen`, `tui-components`) pass with their comparators unchanged; `make test-term-engines` shows the same screens and scroll margins with and without scroll regions in xterm.js, alacritty_terminal, wezterm-term, libvterm and pyte; the owner compared both by hand in Ghostty, Terminal.app and Tern.app; and Windows conhost gets Pi's bytes. The owner accepted landing without a Windows Terminal run: native Windows consoles leave `TERM` unset and so get Pi's bytes, and `PIG_TUI_SCROLL_ROWS=0` restores them everywhere else.

Call-site markers: `tui/tui_alt_screen.go` (`doRender`), `tui/tui_alt_screen_scroll.go` (`minRowsSavedByScrolling`).

Evidence: `TestAltScreenScrolledFramesShowPisScreens` (`tui/alt_screen_oracle_test.go`) replays Pi's bytes and PiG's for every step of the 500 scenarios of the pinned Pi's alternate-screen fixture on a terminal model with scroll regions, and requires equal rows after every step; 55 steps scroll. `TestAltScreenFramesMatchPiOracle` still compares the bytes, with scrolling off. `TestAltScreenScrolledFramesShowEveryRow` (`tui/tui_alt_screen_scroll_test.go`) draws random frames (lines added, extended, deleted, inserted and changed, the viewport scrolled, the dock changed) and requires the terminal model to show each frame's rows. `TestAltScreenScrollsTheRowsThatMoved`, `TestAltScreenScrollsRowsDownWhenScrolledBack`, `TestAltScreenDoesNotScrollImageRows`, `TestAltScreenDoesNotScrollAnImageRowThatStays` and `TestAltScreenScrollRowsOff` cover the moves, images and the switch. `BenchmarkAltScreenFrameWhileTheTranscriptGrows` writes 153 bytes per frame against 5,417.

Terminal-engine evidence: `make test-term-engines` (`docs/testing/term-engines.md`) replays a deterministic corpus (streamed transcripts, wheel and PageUp scrolling, resizes, wide and styled text, overlays, random edits and the pinned fixture's scenarios) twice, as the scroll-region frames and as Pi's bytes (`PIG_TUI_SCROLL_ROWS=0`), through xterm.js, `alacritty_terminal`, `wezterm-term`, libvterm and pyte, and requires the same screen (rows, styles, shown cursor) and the whole-screen scroll margins after every frame in every engine. Removing the region reset after a move fails all five.

Known visible difference (accepted by the owner on 2026-10-27): a component that renders a row wider than the screen. Pi clips such a row after it has added the row's closing SGR reset (`applyLineResets`, then `sliceByColumn(line, 0, width, true)` in `packages/tui/src/tui-alt-screen.ts` `doRender`; `applyLineResets` is in `tui.ts`), so the reset is cut off with the clipped text and the row's attributes stay on in the terminal; Pi's own recorded bytes show this (`tui/testdata/alt-screen-oracle.json.gz`, scenario 485 step 3: a component renders a bold row 157 columns wide, and Pi writes its clipped text followed directly by the next row's cursor move, with no reset). The attributes color the rows written after the clipped row, so which rows show them depends on which rows a frame writes. Pi writes every changed row and PiG's scroll frames move the others, so where the leaked attributes appear differs between the two (`known-clipped-sgr-leak` in `tui/alt_screen_term_corpus_test.go`, which the engines must keep showing; fixture scenarios 173 and 485 in `termLeakingOracleScenarios`). Rows that fit the screen never leak, and with the clipped rows closed by a reset the same scenarios are equal in all five engines. The Pi-identical bytes (`PIG_TUI_SCROLL_ROWS=0`) are unchanged.

Fallback evidence: `TestAltScreenScrollFallsBackToPisBytes` (`tui/alt_screen_oracle_test.go`) replays the 500 scenarios with `TERM` unset, `dumb`, `vt52` and `unknown`, and with `PIG_TUI_SCROLL_ROWS=0` in an xterm, and requires each step's bytes to equal Pi's exactly; `TestScrollRegionsUsableIn` lists which terminals are trusted, Zellij included.

Parity allowance: the fullscreen parity scenarios compare the captured screen, which is the same, and the alternate-screen oracle compares Pi's bytes with scrolling off and Pi's screens with it on.

Remove when: Pi scrolls the rows that moved, or an owner decision reverses this one. Then every changed row is drawn again, and the markers, `tui/tui_alt_screen_scroll.go` and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved

## D206 The Durable core resolves HarnessOptions.settings once before each event, where Pi resolves it at each use

What: Pi calls `HarnessOptions.settings` at each use: the scheduler, a generation's phases, the context retention checks, the queue modes of a submission and `resolveAgent` each resolve it (`packages/durable/src/harness/harness.ts`), so a settings callback sees every resolution (`harness-restart-failure.test.ts` counts 49 reads of `extensions` for a round of one generation with one tool call). The Durable core reads the settings from the snapshot the host sends before each event it delivers (`durable/core/host/cf/src/facade/harness.ts` `syncSettings`; `sqlhost` `Options.Settings` for the Go host), and only `resolveAgent`, which stays with the host, resolves the callback at its use. The callback is therefore called once per event, and a change of the settings takes effect at the next event rather than at the next use within an event. The host reads no `extensions` for that snapshot, so the test above counts PiG's 6 agent resolutions of a round where Pi counts 49.

Why: the core runs in a Wasm module with no import to call the host's callback at each use; a host call at each use costs a crossing at every phase, retention check and queue-mode read, and a settings notice that made the host call the callback as often as the core read it (ABI 12) cost an ABI change and a count per event for a callback no consumer observes by its count.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-28.

Call-site markers: `durable/core/host/cf/src/facade/harness.ts` (`syncSettings`), `durable/core/turnhost.go` (`turnSettings`), `durable/core/sched.go` (`contextRetentionMs`).

Evidence: the `U-harness-restart-failure` row of `durable/contract/corpus.toml` states the measured value (`measured_test`, `measured_failure`, `measured_divergence = "D206"`): the candidate fails Pi's own test "resolves the settings no more often than before" with `expected 6 to be 49`, and the row fails if it passes or fails otherwise (`durable/contract/lib/gate.mjs`; `vitest-paired.test.mjs` pins the three cases). `TestCoreTheRestartLimitOnTheNormalPath` (`durable/core/driver/harness_restart_failure_core_test.go`) counts the calls of the settings source over the core for the same round and asserts 9 a round, where Pi's callback is called 49 times; `TestTheRestartLimitOnTheNormalPath` (`durable/harness/harness_restart_failure_test.go`) asserts the reference Harness's 15 + about 34 resolutions, as Pi's 49.

Parity allowance: the measured row above is the allowance; no other row changes.

Remove when: the core can read the settings by a host call at each use without a measurable cost (the count budgets of `make durable-counts`), or Pi resolves the settings once per step. Then the snapshot, `syncSettings` and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:c963e8de1771a1ca

## D207 TuiAltScreen.children is a copy in the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs, where Pi's is a live array

What: Pi's `TuiAltScreen.children` is the container's own array: a caller that holds it sees every later change of the screen's children, and can change them by writing to it. The Go (`TuiAltScreen.Children`), Rust (`TuiAltScreen::children`) Python (`TuiAltScreen.children`) and Lisp (`tui-alt-screen-children`) SDKs return a snapshot of the children as the SDK knows them after its last `addChild`, `removeChild` or `clear`. Writing to the snapshot changes nothing, and the snapshot does not follow later changes: read `children` again after the call that changed it. `addChild`, `removeChild` and `clear` are the only ways to change the children; the SDKs have no `setChildren`.

Why: the renderer, with the screen's child list, runs in the host process and the extension's components in the extension's process, so an array in the extension cannot be the renderer's array. The SDKs keep the children they sent to the host and update that list after each call returns; a list a caller could write to would need a call to the host on every write and a way for the host to change the extension's list, which Pi's array is not.

Pi bug: none claimed.

Scope: `TuiAltScreen.children` of the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs. Every other member behaves as Pi's, and a read after `addChild`, `removeChild` or `clear` returns what Pi's `children` holds then, in order, with equal-content components kept distinct and a duplicate child kept twice.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-28.

Call-site markers: `extensions/sdk/tui_alt_screen.go` (`Children`), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/tui_alt_screen.rs` (`children`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/tui_alt_screen.py` (the `children` property), `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/tui-alt-screen.lisp` (`tui-alt-screen-children`).

Evidence: `TestTuiAltScreenChildrenAcrossSDKs` (`test/extension-conformance/tui_alt_screen_test.go`) reads `children` after each `addChild`, `removeChild` (of a child and of a component that is not one), a duplicate `addChild`, `removeChild` of the first of two equal entries and `clear`, with three components of equal content, and requires the order Pi's own class reports in the Node cell, in every isolated and packed SDK cell.

Parity allowance: no paired Pi corpus holds `TuiAltScreen.children` across a change of the children, so none changes.

Remove when: Pi's `children` stops being a public live array, or an owner decision asks for a live view across the process boundary; then the four markers, the evidence note and this record go.

Owner decision 2026-11-17: approved as it stands (group 2 remainder)
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:678fcaddd136c552

## D208 From inside a component's render, a TuiAltScreen of the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs fails the calls that re-enter its frame

What: Pi runs a component's `render` on the one thread that draws the frame, so a render that calls a member of its screen is a nested call, and Pi lets it recurse or re-enter: `renderNow()` and `render()` render the document again inside the frame, `start()` and `stop()` nest a terminal start or stop, `copyActiveSelectionToClipboard()` and `queryTerminalColors()` start Promise-returning work from inside the frame, and the `onInput` callback that `Terminal.start` received dispatches input from inside it. In the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs every other call a render makes to its screen runs at once, while the host holds the frame lock for that render, and a later read in the same render sees it, as in Pi. These six calls and the input callback would take the frame lock again, which the frame holds and does not release for the render it waits on: from inside a callback the frame waits for (a component's render, an overlay's `visible` callback, a style callback, an `invalidate` or focus change the frame triggered) they return the host's error `tui.alt: <op> cannot run while a component renders`, and the screen goes on. Called from any other thread they wait for the frame, as before.

Why: the renderer takes one non-reentrant lock for the whole frame, and a re-entered frame would need it while the frame waits for the render that re-enters it, which is a deadlock; supporting Pi's recursion needs a frame that renders again inside itself, which the renderer's single pass and its differential state do not allow.

Pi bug: none claimed.

Scope: the calls `render`, `renderNow`, `start`, `stop`, `copyActiveSelectionToClipboard`, `queryTerminalColors` and the terminal's `onInput` callback, made from a callback the frame waits for, in the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs (the host serves `tui.alt` calls; the SDKs report the host's error as the call's error). The Node SDK serves Pi's own class and recurses as Pi does.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-28, for the six calls and for the terminal `input` callback.

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/tui_alt_bridge.go` (`run`).

Evidence: `TestTuiAltScreenFrameReentryAcrossSDKs` (`test/extension-conformance/tui_alt_screen_test.go`) calls each of the six from inside a render in every isolated and packed Go, Rust, Python and Lisp cell, requires the error for each, and requires the screen to answer the next call in the same render (`setShowHardwareCursor` then `getShowHardwareCursor`).

Parity allowance: no paired Pi corpus constructs `TuiAltScreen` through an SDK and recurses in a render, so none changes.

Remove when: the host's frame can be entered again from inside a render and these calls run as Pi's do; then the call-site marker, the error and this record go.

Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 2 re-approval, the owner's dated word covers the current text; checked by Claude and Tern
SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:b81840436a4513ea

## D212 `/llama` explains a 404 from llama-swap's missing load and unload endpoints, where Pi shows "llama.cpp returned HTTP 404"

What: Pi's `/llama` loads and unloads a model with `POST /models/load` and `POST /models/unload` (`extensions/llama/client.ts`, `load` and `unload`) and shows an HTTP error as the server's `error.message`, else `llama.cpp returned HTTP <status>`. llama-swap lists its models at `/models` with a `status.value`, so the `/llama` list renders, but it has no load or unload endpoint of that shape: it loads a model when a request names it in `model`, and Pi's `/llama` ends in the bare `llama.cpp returned HTTP 404` (reported as #380). When `/models/load` or `/models/unload` answers 404, PiG asks whether the server is llama-swap, by the `"src": "llama-swap"` of the 404's error envelope or a `GET /running` that answers `{"running": [...]}`, and if it is, shows: `/llama cannot load or unload models on llama-swap: it loads a model when a request names it. Add llama-swap to models.json as an openai-completions provider with baseUrl <server>/v1, then pick models with /model. See <docs link>.` Any other 404, and every other status, keeps Pi's text. `GET /running` is the one request PiG sends that Pi does not, and only after such a 404.

Why: Pi's text names neither the server nor the way that works, and the reporter took it for a PiG bug. The way that works is the models.json provider, where PiG and Pi behave the same (journey `llama-swap-models-json`); `/llama` stays a llama.cpp router manager.

Pi bug: none claimed.

Scope: the `Load` and `Unload` calls of the llama.cpp client, so the `/llama` load, unload, restore-after-failed-load and stop-loading paths. `Download` (`POST /models`) and every other request keep Pi's text.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-10-29.

Call-site markers: `internal/codingagent/llama/client.go` (`explainLlamaSwap`).

Evidence: `TestLlamaClientAndCatalogAgainstLlamaSwapAndLlamaServerMatchPi` (`internal/codingagent/llama/llamaswap_test.go`) runs Pi's client and PiG's against a fake llama-swap and a plain llama-server, requires Pi's `llama.cpp returned HTTP 404` from Pi for load, unload and unload-and-wait on both, the llama-swap message from PiG on llama-swap, Pi's text from PiG on the plain server, and every other outcome and request equal. `TestLlamaCommandAgainstLlamaSwapExplainsTheMissingEndpoints` runs the `/llama` command, `TestLoadAndUnloadRecognizeLlamaSwapByItsErrorEnvelope` the envelope, and `TestLoadAndUnloadKeepPisTextForA404ThatIsNotLlamaSwap` and `TestLlamaCommandKeepsPisTextForARouter404` the 404s that keep Pi's text.

Parity allowance: `TestLlamaClientRequestsMatchPi` compares load and unload against Pi over random statuses and bodies, 404 included; its server answers `GET /running` with 404, which is not llama-swap's, so every outcome there stays Pi's.

Remove when: Pi's `/llama` names llama-swap itself, or can manage llama-swap models; or the owner withdraws the decision. Then the call-site marker, the `GET /running` probe, the docs section and this record go.

SCRUTINIZED:approved text-sha256:18fa63d09534ac3d

## D213 A crashed or exited extension runtime releases the read the host holds for an autocomplete provider chain

What: Pi's editor awaits an autocomplete request in the Promise jobs that follow the terminal read that typed the text (`editor.ts` `requestAutocomplete`, `startAutocompleteRequest`), and its extensions run in Pi's own process. A provider chain that needs no I/O therefore opens its menu before the next read, and a chain that awaits real I/O (a timer, a socket, a process) has not settled when the next read arrives. PiG's extension chain crosses a process boundary, so after each read PiG starts the request of the extension chain and holds the next read until the chain settles and its menu is published, or until the chain announces that it awaits real I/O (`ui.autocomplete.pending`), which is when Pi's next read would have come first. The Node runtime announces at the end of its macrotask (`setImmediate`), Pi's rule exactly; the base provider's awaited work (an `@` file search, a command's Promise-returning argument completer) is announced by the host, also through a chain another packed member calls directly. The Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs have only synchronous providers, which settle when they return, as a synchronous Pi provider holds the loop until it returns, so they announce nothing. No timer releases the read.
