PiG's `TestPlugin` (`chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_testing.go`) builds with the same `BuildFacet` and options and the same discovery, but a plugin evaluates only in the Node subprocess (AGENTS.md: no embedded JavaScript runtime; `docs/plan/chord-plugins-node-evaluator.md`), so: `Modules` are files the subprocess imports (the app's API as modules on disk, as a production host's `HostPackages`), not instances; a stub is a Go facet on a Go host (`TestPluginOptions.Stubs`, bridged to the plugin's host as `BridgedFacet` is, so it serves local types without keyed members) or a JavaScript module (`TestPluginOptions.Core`, which may also serve or contribute to registries and serve remote types); the result holds two hosts, the plugin's `NodeHost` (`TestedPlugin.Host`, reached through `NodeHost.Get` and `NodeHost.Hold`, the Go forms of `host.get` and `host.hold` for a local type) and the stubs' Go host (`TestedPlugin.Stubs`). `TestedPlugin.Unserved` and `TestedPlugin.Contributions` return `(value, error)`, where Pi's `unserved()` and `contributions(R)` return the value (the read crosses to the subprocess), and `TestedPlugin.Errors()` returns a copy of the host's errors where Pi's `errors` is the live array.

Why: a live JavaScript closure, module instance or `Host` cannot cross the process boundary, and the alternative (a second evaluator of plugins in the Go process) is forbidden. The topology is the one apps run (a Go host with a Node host as its child), so a plugin that passes `TestPlugin` meets its stubs through the same bridge it meets Go core facets through in production.

Scope: `TestPlugin`, `TestPluginOptions`, `TestStub`, `TestedPlugin.Host` and `Stubs`; the cases of `testing.test.ts` and example 52 give the same inputs (plugin files, API, contracts) and the same expectations (values read, contributions, names listed, errors) through these forms. Behaviour that is Pi's: a facet that throws while it evaluates rejects with that error as it is (`*NodeError`), and a setup that throws is `testPlugin: setup failed: ...` (`TestTestPluginRejectsAScriptThatThrowsWhileItEvaluatesWithItsOwnError`); the build and its refusals (`failed to build`), the versioned-API check, the root and kind derivation (single-file and checkout plugins, kinds named by files, `*.test.*`/`*.d.ts` naming none), the collecting facet and its fixed point, the wait for activations bounded by `timeouts.ready`, an in-memory store.

Owner decision: approved 2026-11-08 (option (a), port with a Go `TestPlugin`, 2026-11-08 00:36Z; this record is the residue).

Call-site markers: `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_testing.go` (`TestStub`, `TestPlugin`), `chord/nextnode/plugins/node/testing.mjs`.

Evidence: `TestTestPlugin*` (`chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_testing_test.go`, the cases of `testing.test.ts`) and `TestExample52TestPlugin` (`plugins_examples_testplugin_test.go`).

Parity allowance: none needed beyond the forms above; the Pi cases pass with Go stubs, file modules and the two hosts.

Remove when: Pi evaluates plugins out of process (its stubs then need a wire form too) or PiG gets an in-process evaluator.

SCRUTINIZED:approved

## D503 `TestPlugin`'s registries are named, its contributions are data, and `root` is `RootPrincipal`: no JavaScript closure or registry token crosses

What: Pi's `TestedPlugin.contributions(R)` takes the registry token `R` and returns its entries with their live values, and `root(fn, context, options)` runs the closure `fn` as the host's principal or `options.principal`. PiG's `TestedPlugin.Contributions(name, ctx)` takes the registry's name and returns `[]Contribution{Plugin, Type, Version, Value}` with each value as JSON data (a function or other non-data value in a contribution does not cross; the control channel's value form carries `bytes()` leaves), and `RootPrincipal(principal, ctx)` runs the fixed closure `async (ctx) => ctx.principal` of Pi's own test on the plugin's host and answers the principal its context has (the host's own for nil). The error for a registry that is not collected is Pi's message (`testPlugin: registry <name> is not collected ...`).

Why: the plugin's host is in the Node subprocess; a Go function cannot run there as an object's code, and a JavaScript closure written in a Go test is not a Go value. The helper's own reads (the collection's fixed point over `undeclaredRegistries`, `activationsUnderWay`, `timeouts.ready`, `unservedNames`, the reload that adds the collecting facet) run in the subprocess (`node/testing.mjs`), so the host's internals never cross; only their results do.

Scope: `TestedPlugin.Contributions`, `RootPrincipal`, `Unserved`. `TestedPlugin.Unserved(ctx)` is the sorted names of the contracts and registries that requests on the plugin's host wait for unbound because nobody serves them (Pi's `unserved()`, read in the subprocess through `unservedNames`). A test that needs `ctx.get` of other objects as another principal has no Go form of `root` yet; it reads through `NodeHost.Get` as the host's principal.

Owner decision: approved 2026-11-08 (same option as D502).

Call-site markers: `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_testing.go` (`Contributions`, `RootPrincipal`), `chord/nextnode/plugins/node/testing.mjs`.

Evidence: `TestTestPluginLoadsThePluginBuiltAgainstTheAPIBesideAStubCollectsItsContributionsAndListsWhatItWaitsFor` (`plugins_testing_test.go`: contributions by name, root as the host and as `u2`), `TestExample52TestPlugin`.

Parity allowance: none needed.

Remove when: PiG's plugin host can run a Go closure in the plugin's host process, or Pi's `root` takes data.

SCRUTINIZED:approved

## D520 A connection reset as soon as it is accepted reports the response read, where Pi usually reports the connect

What: a server that resets every connection the moment it accepts it makes the pinned pi-ai report `Connection error.: connect ECONNRESET <addr>` on `openai-completions`, `openai-responses` and `anthropic-messages` (60 runs against the same Go-hosted server: `connect ECONNRESET <addr>` 56, `write ECONNRESET` 4, `read ECONNRESET` 0). PiG reports `Connection error.: read ECONNRESET` in most runs (`connect ECONNRESET <addr>` 2, `write ECONNRESET` 4, `read ECONNRESET` 54). The cause is always kept; only the operation named in it differs.

Why: Go's dial returns before the peer's reset arrives and the request write goes into the socket buffer, so the reset surfaces on the response read. That is the same observable state as a peer that resets after it read the request, where Pi reports `read ECONNRESET` (6/6 probes) and PiG matches. PiG cannot tell the two apart without guessing from timing, and the reset after the request is the failure a real server produces, so it keeps Node's text for that case.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: `nodeConnectionFailure` (`ai/errors.go`): the connection-error cause of the three streaming APIs and the OpenAI SDK transport.

Owner decision: approved (owner, 2026-11-13T14:52:18Z: "proceed with recommendations."; the line of that decision for this record: "D520 APPROVED (a connection reset at accept is reported on the response read, a Go dial limitation; error text only)."). Drafted by the FollowAi lane on 2026-11-08 at the reviewer's request, reviews/follow-ai3.md.
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:7111674edbe4c6fe

Remove when: PiG can tell a reset on accept from a reset after the request (for example by recording whether the request body was written before the reset reached the connection), or Pi stops reporting the connect.

Call-site markers: `ai/errors.go` (`nodeConnectionFailure`).

Evidence: `TestConnectionErrorKeepsCauseThroughSDKsMatchesPi` (`ai/connection_cause_oracle_test.go`: refused, reset after the request, closed after the request, closed on accept, all exact against the pinned pi-ai) and `TestOpenAISDKTransportErrorConnectionReset` (`ai/openai_sdk_transport_error_test.go`: reset on accept accepts `connect`, `write` or `read`).

Parity allowance: the reset-on-accept case accepts the three operations because the pinned pi-ai reaches any of them by timing and PiG reaches the read by construction.

## D521 A bigint in validated tool arguments is the string `<digits>n`, where Pi holds a JavaScript bigint

What: a tool schema with `Type.BigInt` (`{"type":"bigint"}`) coerces a JSON argument to a bigint in Pi's `validateToolArguments` (`packages/ai/src/utils/validation.ts`), and Pi's own `validation-probes.test.ts` writes that value as `${value}n` because `JSON.stringify` throws on a bigint (Pi af79b0c3d). PiG hands the validated arguments to a tool as JSON text, so a coerced bigint is written as the JSON string `"<digits>n"` (`ai.appendStringify`), and the four probe rows that hold a bigint or an `undefined` (bigint x3, undefined) record that representation. A tool that reads the argument as a number or as a string without the suffix sees the suffixed string, where Pi's tool receives a bigint.

Why: Go has no JavaScript bigint inside a JSON document and a tool argument reaches the tool, the transcript and the extension protocols as JSON. A bigint cannot be written as a JSON number without losing the type or the digits it carries (`JSON.stringify` has no form for it), and a reader of the text cannot tell a bigint from a number. The string keeps every digit and marks the type the way Pi's probes do. `undefined` is not a divergence: it is no member of an object and `null` in an array, as `JSON.stringify` writes it.

Pi bug: none; Pi's tool receives a bigint in process. The probes' `${value}n` is Pi's own notation for it.

Scope: `appendStringify` (`ai/validate.go`) and so `ValidateToolArguments`: only a schema that names the `bigint` type, which no stock tool and no model catalog schema uses; an MCP or extension tool with `type: "bigint"` is the only way to reach it.

Owner decision: owner approved option D (keep the string representation, record it as a numbered divergence) 2026-11-08 (owner list #22, "proceed with recommendations").

SCRUTINIZED:approved

Remove when: the tool-argument path carries a typed bigint (for example a JSON extension with a numeric tag) that every reader of the arguments understands.

Call-site markers: `ai/validate.go` (`appendStringify`, the `*big.Int` case).

Evidence: `TestValidationProbesMatchPi` and `TestValidateToolArgumentsMatchesPiOnPiProbes` (`ai/validate_probes_upstream_test.go`, `ai/validate_pi_oracle_test.go`): the bigint rows of `ai/testdata/validation-probes.json` run against Pi's recorded outcome and against Pi's own `validateToolArguments`, whose oracle (`ai/testdata/validate_tool_args.mjs`) writes a bigint as `${value}n`.

Parity allowance: the four bigint and undefined rows of `validation-probes.test.ts` are compared by Pi's outcome notation, which PiG's representation matches; a tool that needs the bigint type gets the suffixed string.

## D541 A message object is one per Node process and one per ExtensionRunner call; Pi's extensions and callers all share one realm

What: In Pi every extension and every caller runs in one JavaScript realm, so the message of `message_start`, `message_end`, `turn_end` and `agent_end`, the session entry's `message` and anything passed on `pi.events` are one object. PiG shares that object between every handler and every extension packed in one Node process, between the events of the Session's own run and the session entry, between the handlers of one `ExtensionRunner` emit (Go, Rust, Python and Node callers alike), and across Node processes for a message passed on `pi.events` (the receiver that holds the message resolves it to its own object). What it cannot share:

| Case | Pi | PiG |
|---|---|---|
| A Go, Python or Rust caller of `ExtensionRunner.emitMessageEnd` (or `emit`, `emitBoundary`) passes a message it built or read from `getEntries()`, and compares it with the `event.message` a Node handler holds, or a later emit's | `===` when it is the same object | false: the caller passes a value (a Go map, a Python dict, a Rust `Value`), and `getEntries` returns a fresh decode on each call, so there is no object a number could name |
| A Node handler of `ExtensionRunner.emitMessageEnd` edits `event.message` in place | the caller's object holds the edit | the edit is made to the message decoded for the call; the caller sees it only in a replacement the result carries |
| Node extensions in two processes (isolated cells, a member quarantined after a fault, different runtimes): one edits its message in place after the other holds it | both hold the one object, so the edit is on it | each process holds its own object for the message, with its own edits; a later payload that carries the host's value does not undo an edit of the process's own, and a `message_end` handler's edit goes back to the host and reaches the other process as a new value |
| A Node, Go, Python or Lisp handler of `message_update` edits, in place, the content of the partial message (`event.message.content`: a block's text, a block added or removed) | `agent-loop.ts:436-438` emits `{ ...partialMessage }`, a shallow copy that holds the very `content` array object of the provider's partial; a provider that pushes one `output` message for every partial (`ai/src/api/openai-completions.ts:313,384,442`: `partial: output`, and `output` is the message the stream ends with) therefore has the edit in the assistant message that `agent-session.ts` persists and in the next provider request, and the next handler of every extension reads it from the same `event.message`. The sharing is of the content array object, so it depends on the provider: a provider that builds a new partial per event shows none | the edit stays in the process of the extension that made it: the next handler of the same or of another extension, the assistant entry and the next request do not hold it (the measured provider is the OpenAI-completions shape above, in `TestInPlaceMessageEventEditsMatchPi`, row `message_update/deep`; observable in every SDK named, isolated, packed, and the fused Go extension) |
| A Rust handler of `message_update` makes the same edit | the same | the edit CAN be written and is lost, as in the other SDKs: the Rust handler is an `EventHandler`, `Fn(&Context, &mut Value)` (`extensions/sdk-rs/src/extension.rs:190`), called with `&mut data` (`extension.rs:3601`), and `data` is a copy the connection rebuilt for this dispatch (`extensions/sdk-rs/src/event_stream.rs:48`, `req.args = Some(root.clone())`, while the replica keeps the root as the host sent it); for `message_update` nothing compares that copy with what was received, so the reply carries no edit (a message edit is returned only for `message_start`, `message_end`, `agent_end`, `extension.rs:3712-3726`). Pinned by `a_message_update_handlers_edit_of_the_partial_stays_in_its_copy_and_is_not_sent_back` (`extensions/sdk-rs/tests/event_stream_wire.rs`: the handler changes the partial's text; the reply carries neither the edit nor `_pigEventEdits`, and the next delta of the stream applies to the text as the host sent it). Observable in the Rust SDK isolated and packed; listed on its own because the owner's decision names it |
| A Rust handler of `message_start` or `agent_end` replaces the member wholesale (`event.message = {...}`, `event.messages = [...]`) | later handlers of the same extension and of other extensions see the new object; the session keeps the original: no entry, no provider request and no listener of the session carries the change (runner.ts `emit`, one event object for every handler) | later handlers see it, and the session SAVES it as an edit inside the message: for `message_start` the entry, both provider requests and the session's listeners carry it, for `agent_end` the entry and the listener do (not the requests). A Rust handler owns its event as a value (`Fn(&Context, &mut Value)`, `extensions/sdk-rs/src/extension.rs`) and a value has no identity, so the reply cannot tell "replaced the member" from "edited inside the member"; the Go, Node, Python and Lisp SDKs (isolated, packed, fused) do what Pi does |
| A Rust handler of `message_update` replaces `event.assistantMessageEvent` wholesale | later handlers see the new object; the session keeps the original | later handlers do NOT see it and the session does not hold it: the SDK's snapshot of that member compares scalars and key presence only, because the streaming partial inside it grows on every delta and a full compare per delta is the cost measured for the case above (23 to 46 microseconds and 15 to 30 KB per delta) |
| An extension passes a message on `pi.events` to a Node extension in another process that has not yet received that message's `message_end` | `===` its own message later | a proxy to the sender's object, as for any object on the bus (D83); the receiver's own object, once it receives the message, is another |
| A Node extension whose only message handlers are `turn_end` or `agent_end` compares `agent_end.messages[i]` with `entry.message` | `===` | false: the host numbers a message when a Node handler receives `message_start` or `message_end`, and persists it before any later handler asks |

Why: A JavaScript object cannot cross a process boundary, and a value passed by a Go, Python or Rust caller has no identity. The host numbers each message (`RequestPayload.message_seqs`, from one process-wide counter, so two Sessions or runners never share a number in a Node process), and a Node process resolves a number to the object it holds: that carries identity wherever the host holds a message object (a Session's run, a graph emit's decoded event) and wherever a Node process holds the number (`pi.events`). The rows above are what has no such object or no such process. Matching packed to isolated would remove behavior Pi has.

Why: a faithful form must find out, for every `message_update` of every run, whether the handler changed the partial: a snapshot before the handler and a comparison after it (the way `message_end` does it once per message). The measured cost of that form, on a 2,000-delta stream with one handler that edits nothing (`BenchmarkNodeMessageUpdateStream`, the host side, per delta): base 193 to 208 microseconds and 88 allocations; with the detection +23.1 microseconds and +14.9 KB of heap, that is +11 to +24 % per delta (range over the repeated runs), paid by every run whether or not any handler edits. The owner chose to record the two cases instead of spending that on the hot path.

The two Rust replacement rows are a limit of the Rust handler's type, recorded narrowly (option (a) of the owner's decision of 2026-11-17): in-place edits inside the message, the common case, behave as Pi in Rust, and a replacement of `message_start.message` or `agent_end.messages` or of `message_update.assistantMessageEvent` is the only thing a Rust value cannot tell from an edit. The Rust rows are pinned to this behaviour in `TestInPlaceMessageEventEditsMatchPi` (`rustRecorded`), which fails if the Rust SDK starts doing something else.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: `ExtensionRunner` of the Go, Rust and Python SDKs (`sessionGraph.new` kind `ExtensionRunner`) over Node extensions, for the first two rows; Node extensions in different processes, for the third row; handlers of `message_update` that edit the partial's content in place, in every SDK, for the fourth and fifth rows; a Rust handler that replaces `message_start.message`, `agent_end.messages` or `message_update.assistantMessageEvent` wholesale, for the sixth and seventh; Node extensions that handle neither `message_start` nor `message_end`, for the last. Not: an assigned member of the `message_update` event object (cured), the message of `message_start`, `turn_end`, `agent_end` and `message_end` (cured), a Go, Node, Python or Lisp handler's wholesale replacement of a member (as Pi); Node extensions packed in one process; the Session's own run seen by one extension; a Node caller (it uses Pi's own in-process ExtensionRunner, D73); other SDKs' payloads, which are unchanged.

Owner decision: 2026-11-17, row 3 (the in-place edit of the streaming partial at message_update, including Rust): option 3, a narrow record of the two cases with the measured figures; and the Rust wholesale replacement of `message_start.message`, `agent_end.messages` and `message_update.assistantMessageEvent`: option (a), a narrow record of exactly those three rows. Approved as written. Owner decision: 2026-11-08, owner list item #8 (isolated message identity) approved as written, in the narrowed form; proposed by PaseoIdentity and narrowed after the review in `reviews/paseo-identity-f4.md` (the per-emit numbering and `pi.events` resolution the first draft listed here are ported).

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:df864a431a349d4a

Remove when: Node extensions share one process or realm (the isolated placement goes), the Go, Python and Rust SDKs pass messages by reference to an object the host holds (a handle API), or a `message_end` handler's in-place edit goes back to the host of a graph emit, or the edit of the partial's content by a `message_update` handler reaches the next handler of every extension at no measurable per-delta cost: a row that shows it on the 2,000-delta benchmark (BenchmarkNodeMessageUpdateStream) with the same allocations and time per delta as the base, within the benchmark's noise, or a Rust handler's replacement of a member is told from an edit inside it (a handle API for the event, or an explicit replace call) so that the three Rust rows equal Pi. Then the call-site markers and this record go.

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/runtime.mjs` (`messageNumber`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/host.go` (`makeEventHandler`: the `MessageSeqs` block), `coding/session_graph_extension_runner_emit.go` (`withEmitMessageSequences` and the `emitMessageEnd` case of `callEmit`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/host.go` (the `message_update` branch of `makeEventHandler`, where a reply for `message_update` is not inspected for an edit of the partial's content), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/extension.rs` (the handling of `message_update`, and of the `message_start` and `agent_end` members a handler replaces; the other SDKs' sites are the same decision and are covered by the first marker: the host, not the SDK, would carry the edit).

Evidence: `TestNodeRuntimeKeepsAnEditMadeOutsideMessageEnd` and `TestNodeRuntimeEntryBindWithReorderedMembersKeepsAnEdit` (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime_node_event_message_identity_test.go`; an edit outside `message_end` stays in the object the process holds; the host receives it through the event's edit members), `TestXrefMessageCrossesByNumberToTheReceiversOwnObject` (same file; a receiver that does not hold a number gets a proxy), `TestPiEventsMessageIsTheReceiversOwnObject` (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/xref_message_identity_test.go`; packed and isolated), the `a_identity` entry of the `extension_runner` row (`test/extension-conformance/extension_runner_test.go`; two handlers of one emit hold one object, isolated and packed, in every SDK), `TestNodeExtensionFindsTheEntryOfItsMessagesAfterAnInPlaceEdit` (`coding/session_message_identity_node_test.go`), `TestInPlaceMessageEventEditsMatchPi` (`test/extension-conformance/in_place_message_events_test.go`): row `message_update/deep` stays in `curePending` with this record as its reason and must keep differing from Pi's table until the record is removed, and the Rust rows `message_start/reassign`, `agent_end/reassign` and `message_update/reassign` are pinned to the recorded behaviour (`rustRecorded`), `BenchmarkNodeMessageUpdateStream` (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/event_edits_bench_test.go`; the base and the measured detection), `a_message_update_handlers_edit_of_the_partial_stays_in_its_copy_and_is_not_sent_back` (`extensions/sdk-rs/tests/event_stream_wire.rs`: the handler changes the partial's text; the reply carries neither the edit nor `_pigEventEdits`), Pi `agent-loop.ts:436-438` and `ai/src/api/openai-completions.ts:313,384,442`.

Parity allowance: none; each row above is a divergence of Pi's one realm, and the rows PiG matches are pinned by the tests above.

## D601 The browser page's and the terminal sandbox's client runtimes, and the React bindings, are Pi's TypeScript vendored with one patch (D636), not a Go port

What: Pi's `@earendil-works/chord/web` (`startPage`, `pageEnvironment`, `followLanguage`, `followThemes`, `PageOptions`), `@earendil-works/chord/react` (`HostProvider`, `useHold`, `useHost`, `useMessages`, `usePageState`, `useRefState`, `useRegistry`, `useWatch`, `watchStore`, `ExternalStore`) and the sandbox side of `@earendil-works/chord/tui` (`Bridge`, `SandboxChannel`, `BridgeTerminal`, `TerminalRuntime`, `TerminalRuntimeOptions`, `startTerminalRuntime`) have no Go declaration. PiG ships Pi's own TypeScript for them as a vendored tree of the Go module, `chord/nextnode/surfaces/client/pi/chord/src/{web,react,tui}` (272 files with React 19.3.0 beside them; Pi main `f6600b58f347`, not the pin `b4afdddf44e0`), each file's hash in `manifest.json`. Two files, `surfaces/active-language.ts` and `active-theme.ts`, carry the D636 patch, so they are not Pi's unchanged. An app's page imports the files by path (`examples/chord-todo/client/page.ts:6` imports `startPage` from `.../client/pi/chord/src/web/index.ts`) and `surfaces.BuildBase` bundles it; the tree is not embedded in a binary. Only the terminal sandbox's bootstrap (`client/tui/bootstrap.cjs.in`, Pi's `BOOTSTRAP` of `node/tui/sandbox.ts`) is embedded (`client.TuiBootstrapTemplate`).

Why: these modules run in the user's browser (DOM, `window`, React) or in a JavaScript context the launcher starts (the app's terminal code and the plugins' registered scripts are evaluated there). A Go process cannot be that runtime, so there is nothing to port; the faithful form is the file itself. Behavior equals Pi's because the code is Pi's, apart from the D636 patch.

Pi bug: Not a Pi bug.

Scope: `chord/nextnode/surfaces/client` (vendored tree, `manifest.json`, `automation/gen/vendor-chord-surfaces-client.sh`), the interface rows of `pkg:chord/web`, `pkg:chord/react` and the sandbox half of `pkg:chord/tui` (designed out in `test/parity/interface-closure/autobind/reviewed-decisions.json`).

Owner decision: approved 2026-11-08 (Pi's chord web/react/tui-sandbox client modules ship as Pi's TypeScript unchanged; `client_manifest_test.go` checks their hashes). The D636 patch on two files is his own named cure (2026-11-13T03:33:12Z: "chord/p2 pages apply the stored language/theme at load", `handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md` line 188 under his word of that time). Drafted by the ChordGapTui lane.
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern

Remove when: PiG runs the browser page or the terminal sandbox from Go-authored code.

Call-site markers: `chord/nextnode/surfaces/client/client.go`.

Evidence: `TestReactWatchStoresReadTheValueCallBackOnChangesGiveUndefinedForNoWatchable` (`chord/nextnode/surfaces`), `TestReactBindingsHoldWhileMountedReRenderOnChangeReleaseOnUnmount`, `TestThePagesLanguageWritesLangAndDirOnTheRootElementFromTheDeviceThenTheUsersPick` (`chord/nextnode/app`, real Chromium), `chord/nextnode/tui/tui_kit_upstream_test.go`.

Parity allowance: none needed: Pi's tests for these modules run over the shipped files.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:41d9cf96f7c7cce2

## D605 The server's two hosts serve one union: `Host.Delegate` one way, the Node host's destination the other

What: Pi's server runs its plugins in its own host (`createHost({ address: Server() })` loads them), so the server serves the union of the kit's types and the plugins' from one place. The Go kit's plugins run in a Node subprocess behind the Go server host, which is also addressed `server` (it serves the kit's own remote types: the locale and theme choices, telemetry ingest, the app's Go facets). Both directions are kept so a hold binds on whichever of the two serves the type. Go to Node: `Host.Delegate(kind, RouteOptions, ctx)` is the route of the host's own address kind, consulted only for remote types placed on the host that its published table does not serve: those holds and references go over the delegate (a relayed hold, a remote object, parked while `Resolve` runs, resolved again once after a drop), everything the host serves stays here whatever the state of its object. A hold arriving on the delegate's own link for a type nobody serves is answered `unavailable` (not sent back). The app kit gives the NodeHost the address `server` and delegates to its link instead of accepting it. Node to Go: Pi's `Destination` places every remote type of the NodeHost's own address here, served or not, so a plugin's `self.get(LocaleChoice, {})` would wait `<type> is not served here` for ever. The Node host runs Pi's `Destination` through `node/destination.mjs` (host.ts's import of `./destination.ts` is answered by `resolve.mjs`; Pi's file is vendored as it is): for a remote type placed here that the Go host serves (`Host.Serves`, asked over a synchronous callback, `RuntimeLoad.ParentServes`, kept until the next load or reload) the reference goes over the parent link, where the Go host's own object, whatever its variants and members, binds. Residual difference: a hold over that link acts as the link's principal, the server's (a child cannot claim to be someone else, link §4: `callerOf`), where Pi's one host acts as the plugin object's own principal. So a plugin object's LocaleChoice or ThemeChoice is the server principal's, not the page owner's. Making it the object's principal needs the child's link to name whom a hold acts for, which Pi's protocol allows only from a parent to its child; it is not done here and the test pins today's behaviour.

Why: process boundary (a Node subprocess for the plugins) and the Go server's two hosts (D462). Without the first direction a TypeScript server plugin cannot serve a remote type (`a host without an address serves only local types`, or, with the address, `unavailable`), which every reload row on the server needs; without the second a server plugin cannot hold any type the Go server serves.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: `chord/next/host` (`Host.Delegate`, `Host.Serves`, `destination`, the closure rule's placement by the staged table), `chord/nextnode/plugins` (`node/destination.mjs`, `node/resolve.mjs`, `RuntimeLoad.ParentServes`), `chord/nextnode/app` (`start`). Nothing changes for a host without a delegate, or a NodeHost without `ParentServes`. `Host.Delegate` is refused when the kind is not the host's own address kind (`delegate: <kind> is not this host's address kind`), when a route for that kind exists already (a second delegate: `delegate: <kind> is routed already`; `host_host.go:871-878`), and after load (`delegate: routes are declared before load`).

Owner decision: owner approved 2026-11-08 (17:29Z).

SCRUTINIZED:approved

Remove when: never for the two hosts; the principal difference goes when a child's link can name whom a hold acts for.

Call-site markers: `chord/next/host/host_host.go` (`Delegate`, `Serves`), `chord/nextnode/app/app.go` (`start`), `chord/nextnode/plugins/node/destination.mjs`, `chord/next/host/host_delegate_test.go`.

Evidence: `TestDelegateServesTheUnionOfTheHostsTypes...`, `TestDelegateWhatTheDelegatesOwnLinkAsksForAndNothingServesIsNotSentBackToIt`, `TestDelegateWhenItIsDownItsTypesWaitAndTheHostsOwnAreUntouched`, `TestDelegateAHoldMadeWhileItIsDownWaitsOnTheResolvesErrorAndBindsOnceItIsBack`, `TestDelegateHoldsKeepTheirOrderAcrossTheDelegatesRestart...`, `TestDelegateIsRefusedFor...`, `TestDelegateTheClosureRuleReadsTheStagedTable...`, `TestHostServesTheTypesOfItsPublishedTableAndNoOtherName` (`chord/next/host/host_delegate_test.go`), `TestAServerPluginsObjectHoldsTheTypesTheGoServerServesOverTheNodeHostsLinkAsTheServer` (`chord/nextnode/app/app_delegate_test.go`: binds, calls, the principal difference), and the server rows of the reload suite (`chord/nextnode/app/reload_*_test.go`).

Parity allowance: none.

SCRUTINIZED:approved

## D607 The plugins component loads hosts through a Node subprocess it starts: Pi's `evaluate`, `stack` and runtime run in that subprocess, not in the Go process

What: Pi's plugin runtime (`packages/chord/src/node/plugins/runtime.ts`, `plugins.ts`) is handed a live `Host` and evaluates each plugin script in the same process (`evaluateFacet` with `vm.Script`, `mapStack` through a registry of source maps, `createPluginRuntime()` taking nothing). PiG's plugins are JavaScript and a Go process cannot run them, so Pi's files run verbatim in a Node subprocess (`chord/nextnode/plugins/node/pi/chord/src/node/plugins/`) and the Go side keeps what is data: `PluginRuntime.Load/Reload` and `Plugins.Load/Reload` start the subprocess host themselves (`NodeHost`) and take the options of that start (`RuntimeLoad.Core` is a module file, `Modules` are module paths) where Pi takes a `Host`, core `PluginFacet`s and live module values. `RolloutStep.Reload` also receives `applied`, the set the rollout left (Pi reads `plugins.current(kind)` atomically after its `ship`). `evaluateFacet`, `EvaluateOptions`, `BuiltScript`, `mapStack` and `HostPackages` have no Go form: Go sees the evaluation's failure as `*NodeError` with its `Stack`.

Why: a JavaScript facet, module instance or `Host` cannot cross the process boundary, and a second evaluator of plugins in the Go process is forbidden (see D502, D605).

Scope: `chord/nextnode/plugins` (`plugins_runtime.go`, `plugins_plugins.go`, `plugins_node*.go`, `node/`), `chord/nextnode/surfaces` (surfaces build after the server's listen, D462).

Owner decision: APPROVED for 0.8.0 (owner, 2026-11-08 17:29Z): the live-JS rows evaluateFacet, EvaluateOptions, BuiltScript, mapStack and HostPackages stay under D607 with their intention checks; follow-up after 0.8.0: Load takes a host as Pi does. SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-08.

Call-site markers: `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_runtime.go` (`PluginRuntime`, `RuntimeLoad`), `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_plugins.go` (`Plugins.Load`, `Plugins.Step`, `RolloutStep`).

Evidence: `evaluate.test.ts`, `stack.test.ts` and `runtime.test.ts` ported through the subprocess (`plugins_evaluate_test.go`, `plugins_stack_test.go`, `plugins_runtime_test.go`); `plugins.test.ts` (per plugin at startup and reload, rollout, current sets, the app's API and required roots, fix round 1, GC) through `RuntimeLoad` and the Node host (`plugins_plugins_test.go`, `plugins_endtoend_test.go`, examples 35 and 38 in `plugins_examples_test.go`). The designed-out rows `PluginRuntime`/`Plugins` load, reload and step, `RuntimeLoad` (both packages) and `RolloutStep` carry the intention check in `test/parity/interface-closure/autobind/reviewed-decisions.json`.

Parity allowance: none beyond the forms above.

Remove when: PiG gets an in-process evaluator of plugins or Pi evaluates plugins out of process.

## D615 The Go, Python, Lisp and Rust SDKs show the host each provider stream event as it was when the provider pushed it, which is stronger than Pi's consumer guarantees

What: an extension provider that builds its stream with `CreateAssistantMessageEventStream` (Go `sdk.CreateAssistantMessageEventStream`, Python `create_assistant_message_event_stream`, Lisp `create-assistant-message-event-stream`) and pushes an event that shows a message it goes on changing (the shared `partial`) is forwarded to the host with the message as it was when `Push` returned. The result the host receives is the message as it was when the provider ended the stream (the `done`/`error` event's message, or the value passed to `End`). The Go SDK encodes the event on the provider's goroutine at `Push`; the Python and Lisp SDKs queue a deep copy. In-process iteration (`Events`, `events()`, `model-stream-next`) still yields the pushed object itself, and `Result` returns the provider's own message. A value that cannot be copied or encoded (a cycle, a lock, a dotted list) fails the request that forwards the stream; it does not raise in `Push`. The Rust SDK takes the event by value, so it shows the host the event as pushed with no copy.

Pi: `packages/ai/src/utils/event-stream.ts:43-54` hands the consumer the same event object at `push`, and the Node SDK serializes it in its `for await` body, at the consumer's turn. The host therefore sees the message at the consumer's turn, which is the message at the push only when real I/O (a macrotask) separates the provider's pushes. After a synchronous burst of pushes, or with only microtask awaits between them (`await Promise.resolve()`, an `await` over an already buffered async generator), Pi's consumer sees the later edits: in a probe of 100 one-character deltas a synchronous burst showed the finished text in every event, an `await` over buffered chunks lagged one push, and microtask awaits lagged further, while an `await setImmediate` between pushes showed exactly the text at each push.

Why: a threaded SDK cannot reproduce the microtask scheduling of one JavaScript thread, and a forwarder that reads the provider's map on another goroutine or thread while the provider writes it is a data race (Go `-race` reports it) and shows the host arbitrary later text. Capturing at the push is the one deterministic and race-free choice, and it equals Pi exactly in the I/O-separated case, which is the case a provider that streams from a network response is in. The Node SDK keeps Pi's behavior.

Scope: the Go, Python, Lisp and Rust SDKs: `ModelEventStream.Push`/`End` (Go), `ModelEventStream.push`/`end` (Python), `model-stream-push`/`model-stream-end` (Lisp) and, in Rust, `push`/`end`, which take the `serde_json::Value` by value, so the event is the push-time state by ownership and needs no marker; and the provider stream request that forwards them (`provider_stream`, `provider_stream_simple`, and a provider object's stream). Not affected: events the SDK builds or decodes itself, the Node SDK, which keeps Pi's behavior, and a Python stream class that overrides `events()` or `result()`. The Python forwarder reads such a stream as Pi's consumer reads an author's own stream: the host receives what the overriding `events()` yields, when the host asks for it, and `provider_stream_simple` answers with what the overriding `result()` returns. Only a stream that uses the SDK's own `events()` and `result()` has the push-time guarantee. The Go, Rust and Lisp streams are concrete types with no overridable read, so they have no such case.

Owner decision: approved by owner Michael Kinsy on 2026-11-09 (copy at push in the Go, Python and Lisp SDKs; keep Node as Pi does; record the burst-case difference).
Owner decision 2026-11-17: group 3 re-approval (small edits: wording, citation, a widening that follows Pi or an approved record, or a claim checked against its code and test); checked by Claude and Tern

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:66aebb71f360aca8

Remove when: Pi's provider stream consumer captures each event at its push (or the Go, Python and Lisp SDKs gain a synchronization point that makes the forwarder see the provider's state at the consumer's turn). Then the three call-site markers and this record go.

Call-site markers: `extensions/sdk/context.go` (`ModelEventStream.enqueue`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` (`ModelEventStream._enqueue`), `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/event-stream.lisp` (`model-stream-enqueue`).

Evidence: `TestProviderStreamEventsShowThePartialAsPushed` in `test/extension-conformance` (the row: 11 cases, every SDK isolated and packed; red for the Go, Python and Lisp cases at e0d2778cf, Go also under `-race`), `provider_stream_snapshot_test.go` (Go SDK), `tests/test_provider_stream_snapshot.py` (Python SDK), `model-tests.lisp` (Lisp SDK).

Parity allowance: Pi 1.5.2 has no test of when its consumer reads the partial (its `packages/ai/test/event-stream.test.ts` pushes numbers and tests delivery order and the final message's timing); the row's expected values are the message at each push.

## D616 An npm tarball is extracted with `tar` told the archive and the destination relative to the pack directory, where Pi passes their absolute paths

What: Pi's `installPlugin` packs an npm package into a temporary directory and extracts it with `run("tar", ["-xzf", join(packed, basename(tarball.filename)), "--strip-components=1", "-C", dir], packed, ...)` (`packages/chord/src/node/plugins/install.ts:248-249`): the archive and the destination are absolute paths. GNU tar reads an archive name with a colon before its first slash as `host:file`, so on Windows with the `tar` of Git for Windows ahead of `System32\tar.exe` (bsdtar) in PATH, `C:\...\pack-x\pkg-1.0.0.tgz` is the file `...` on a host named `C` and the extraction fails with `tar (child): Cannot connect to C: resolve failed`. PiG's `unpack` runs tar in the pack directory already (as Pi does) and names the archive `./<basename>`, which both tars read as a local file; every other argument is Pi's. PiG's `packedTarball` also reads npm 12's object form of `npm pack --json` (the member named for the package, where npm 11 prints a list); Pi at the pin takes `const [tarball] = JSON.parse(...)` (`install.ts:245`) and throws on the object. `TestPackedTarballReadsTheListNpm11PrintsAndTheMemberOfThePackageNpm12Prints`.

Why: a plugin install from npm that fails on a common Windows PATH (Git Bash first) for a reason of argument shape only is worse than Pi's behavior and costs nothing to avoid; on POSIX and with bsdtar the extraction is the same. This is a cure of a Pi defect under the owner's rule of 2026-11-13T22:52:04Z ("fix all bugs"), not a difference forced by Go.

Pi bug: With GNU tar of Git for Windows in PATH Pi's `installPlugin` of an npm package fails on Windows (the archive, then the destination); to be filed upstream with the repro (`installPlugin` with an `npm` source on Windows from Git Bash).

Scope: `chord/nextnode/plugins` (`unpack`).

Owner decision: owner approved 2026-11-10 (for 0.8.0).
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:0ee3cc4493d9ddce

Remove when: Pi's install names the archive and the destination relative to the pack directory.

Call-site markers: `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_install.go` (`unpack`).

Evidence: `chord/nextnode/plugins/plugins_install_tar_test.go` (`TestInstallPluginNamesTheTarballToTarRelativeToThePackDirectory`), and on Windows `TestInstallPluginInstallsAnNpmPackageHereALocalTarballIntoRootName` against Git for Windows' tar.

Parity allowance: observable only on Windows with GNU tar first in PATH, where Pi's extraction fails.

## D617 A command-only action (`waitForIdle` or a session action) called through a context that is not a command context is a method that refuses in the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs, where Pi's context has no such method

What: Pi gives `waitForIdle`, `newSession`, `fork`, `navigateTree`, `switchSession` and `reload` only to the command context (`createCommandContext()`, `packages/coding-agent/src/core/extensions/runner.ts:1004-1038`, v1.5.2); the `ExtensionContext` that `createContext()` returns to event, tool and shortcut handlers (`runner.ts:891-969`) has no such member, so `ctx.switchSession(path)` or `ctx.waitForIdle()` in a `session_start` handler throws `TypeError: ctx.switchSession is not a function` inside that handler, the runner reports an extension error and the run goes on with the current session; a context the handler stored and used later has no member either. The Node runtime has Pi's shape: an event, tool or shortcut context has none of the six members (`typeof ctx.switchSession` is `"undefined"` and the call throws Pi's TypeError), and only a command handler's and a `withSession` callback's context has them (a `withSession` callback gets a copy of the command context, `agent-session.ts` `createReplacedSessionContext`). The Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs (the fused Go realization included) have one static `Context` type, so the method exists on every context. Each SDK sends the kind of the context a call is made through (`command_context` on the call frame, set for a command handler's and a `withSession` callback's context and kept with the context after its request ended), and the host answers a command-only call without it with the error `not_a_command_context` ("ctx.<method> is not a function: only a command context has it") and runs nothing. The refusal does not depend on a live request, so a stored event context is refused at any time, as Pi's never gains the members. A command handler, a `withSession` callback and a command context kept past its request are unchanged.

Why: the replacement would otherwise run inside the emit that asked for it, and the other handlers of that emit (the built-in MCP extension among them) run on a replaced context and panic, which ends the process with status 1. Giving the four statically typed SDKs a separate command-context type is an API change for every extension written in them, for a call Pi's own types make unreachable.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs and `coding/extension/host/subprocess` (`Host.runCall`); the six calls `waitForIdle`, `newSession`, `fork`, `navigateTree`, `switchSession` and `reload`. The Node runtime is exact for all six (`typeof ctx.waitForIdle` is `"undefined"` on an event, tool or shortcut context and the call throws `TypeError: ctx.waitForIdle is not a function`) and carries no divergence; the host refusal also guards it. The four native SDKs answer `not_a_command_context` for `waitForIdle` as for the five. A `waitForIdle` through a non-command context was served before, and it hung the run when the handler was part of the run it waited for (a `tool_call` or `agent_end` handler waits for the run that waits for the handler, in every SDK); from a handler that ran while the session was idle (`session_start`) it returned at once, and that call is refused now: a handler that needs the run's end subscribes to `agent_settled`, or starts the wait from a command.

Owner decision: owner, 2026-11-11 (`handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md`, the section `2026-11-11T17:41Z Owner: "proceed with recommendations"`): "i want it all in 0.8.0 since we delayed anyways", and "switchSession/newSession/fork from a non-command context: red-first test, IN 0.8.0"; the decision he took (c) is that the host rejects it and returns the error to the calling handler (`TERN-CLAUDE-CHANNEL.md:19154`). The Node runtime follows Pi's shape. The narrowing to the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs is forced: Go and Rust have one static context type, and the four SDKs keep one interface.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-11

Remove when: the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs hand event, tool and shortcut handlers a context type that has no command-only session actions (a separate command-context type), so the call cannot be written.

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/command_only_calls.go` (`commandOnlyCall`, `commandOnlyCallError`), `extensions/sdk/request_lifetime.go` (`markCommand`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` (`_RequestParent.command`), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/protocol.rs` (`mark_command`), and `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/runtime.mjs` (`addCommandContextMembers`, which gives `waitForIdle` and the five session actions to a command context only and keeps the Node runtime exact).

Evidence: `test/extension-conformance/session_actions_event_context_test.go` (`TestConformance_CommandOnlySessionActionsRefusedFromEventContext`: the six calls from a `session_start`, a `tool_call` and an `agent_end` handler, from a stored event context after its request ended and from a tool's execute context, in every SDK and realization, the packed cells included; Node's `typeof` and TypeError; and from a command and a `withSession` callback the wait is served and the command's wait returns when the host's does), and `coding/extension/host/subprocess/command_only_calls_test.go`.

Parity allowance: observable only to an extension in the Go, Python, Rust or Lisp SDK that calls a command-only session action through a context Pi's types forbid it on.

## D619 A status-indicator or Loader animation step asks for its frame within min(40 ms, interval/2) while frames are being drawn, where Pi asks for it at once

What: Pi's `Loader` (`packages/tui/src/components/loader.ts`) calls `ui.requestRender()` on every animation step (`setInterval` every 80 ms for the default spinner), so a step that falls between the frames a streaming reply asks for is drawn as a frame of its own, up to 16 ms after the last frame. PiG's interactive mode drew every status-indicator tick with an immediate `Render()` (so also off the 16 ms throttle, often 3-8 ms after the stream's last frame); the mode and `Loader` now ask for the step's frame through `RequestRenderWithin(min(40 ms, interval/2))` when the TUI has one: any frame drawn in that time draws the step and the timer is dropped; only when none is does the timer ask for a frame (`RequestRender`, with the same 16 ms throttle). Every other change to the loader (start, message, indicator, invalidate) still asks for a frame at once. A request made while a frame is running asks for the next frame at once, as `RequestRender` does. The final screen, the order of what is drawn and the 80 ms step cadence are Pi's; a step is drawn at most 40 ms plus the frame throttle after its tick, and a step that a streaming frame has already drawn adds no frame.

Removing the status indicator drops the step frame that still waits (`CancelRenderWithin`), so no frame follows the one that removed it; Pi-Bolt's `Loader.stop()` leaves its timer armed.

The wait applies only while frames are being drawn. When no frame was drawn within the last min(40 ms, interval/2) before the step, nothing is about to draw one, and `RequestRenderWithin` asks for the step's frame at once, as Pi does: a spinner that animates while the model has not answered yet or a tool runs steps every 80 ms, on time. Without this rule each such step was drawn 40 ms late, and the longest time without output while waiting for a reply grew from one step (81-83 ms on the `write:200` cell of the Pi-Bolt `pauses.py` harness, Pi's figure) to a step and the wait (120-122 ms, Pi-Bolt 1.2.1's figure).

Why: while a reply or a tool call streams in, Pi draws a frame every 16-20 ms and the spinner step adds a frame of its own every 80 ms, about one frame in five. In a tmux pane every frame is a read, a parse and a redraw for the tmux server; on the `write:50` cell of the Pi-Bolt `tmux_load.py` harness (65 s, 160x48 pane) PiG wrote 4,066 frames to the pane (Pi 1.5.2: 3,951) where Pi-Bolt, which has this change (`TUI.requestRenderWithin`, `STEP_FRAME_DELAY_MS`), wrote 3,262; with it PiG writes 3,278. The frames dropped carry only a glyph of the spinner that the next frame draws anyway.

Pi bug: none; Pi's frame per step is correct, only costlier.

Scope: `tui` (`tuiBase.RequestRenderWithin`, `Loader`, `Loader.TickStep`) and `internal/codingagent` (`tickStatusIndicators`, `renderStatusStep`). `TuiMainScreen`, `TuiAltScreen` and `TuiSurface` take part; a TUI without `RequestRenderWithin` keeps Pi's request at once.

Owner decision: owner approved 2026-11-11 (for 0.8.0).

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-11

Remove when: Pi's `Loader` draws a step within a time, or the owner withdraws the change.

Call-site markers: `tui/loader.go` (`requestStepRender`), `tui/tui.go` (`RequestRenderWithin`, `CancelRenderWithin`), `internal/codingagent/interactive_layout.go` (`renderStatusStep`), `internal/codingagent/interactive_status.go` (`clearStatusIndicator`).

Evidence: `tui/render_within_test.go` (`TestRequestRenderWithinAsksForAFrameOnlyWhenNoneIsDrawn`, `TestRequestRenderWithinAsksAtOnceWhenNoFrameWasDrawnWithinTheDelay`, `TestFrameDrawnWithinTheDelayCarriesTheChange`, `TestRequestRenderWithinIsCoveredByAFrameAlreadyAskedFor`, `TestRequestRenderWithinDuringAFrameAsksForTheNextFrame`, `TestLoaderAnimationStepAsksForItsFrameWithin`, `TestLoaderTickStepAsksForNoFrame`, `TestCancelRenderWithinDropsTheWaitingStepFrame`) and `internal/codingagent/status_tick_within_test.go` (`TestStatusTickAsksForItsFrameWithinTheStepDelay`, `TestStatusTickDrawsAtOnceOnATUIThatCannotWait`, `TestRemovingTheIndicatorCancelsItsWaitingStepFrame`).

Parity allowance: the frame count differs from Pi's on a streaming call (3,278 against 3,951 frames in 66 s of the `write:50` cell) and the final screen is identical; the frames of a spinner step that another frame draws are not written; the settled screen and every frame a request other than a spinner step asks for are Pi's.

## D624 An opt-in screen writer sends the terminal only what changes the screen in fullscreen mode, where Pi sends every row a frame writes

What: in fullscreen mode Pi's renderer writes a frame as one synchronized update that holds, for every row it writes, the cursor address, an erase of the line (`CSI 2K`) and the row's line (`doRender` in `packages/tui/src/tui-alt-screen.ts`). PiG's renderers build the same frames, and with nothing selected the terminal is sent them as they are written, as Pi's terminal is: the screen writer is opt-in in 0.8.0 ("Selecting the writer" below). When it is selected, a `screenwriter.Writer` (`internal/screenwriter`) under `tui.ProcessTerminal.write` reads every write, keeps what the writes leave on the alternate screen (rows of cells, the pen, the cursor, the scroll region) and, for a frame it can read completely, sends the terminal other bytes than the frame's:

- A row rewritten with the cells it already shows is not written.
- A row whose cells change is written in place, from its first cell through its last changed one, as the first bytes of the frame's own line, only when the frame's line is no narrower than the row, has a cell that is not a blank under the default pen exactly where the row has one (same column, same width; a cluster of uncertain width must be the same cluster there), and has others only after the last of them; a line that has others is written through its last cell. For such a row the erase is not sent, and the bytes after the last written cell are not sent: the line's closing reset and hyperlink close, and, when the row neither grew nor got wider, the unchanged cells after the last changed one. A row as wide as the screen of which that would send every cell is not written in place but whole (the next item): the writer prints in the screen's last column only as the frame does.
- Every other changed row is written whole, as the frame wrote it: the line erased, then the row. A line with a hyperlink open, a cluster of several code points, a control character or a sequence other than SGR is not read: that row is sent as the frame wrote it.
- A frame that changes no cell, cursor position or cursor visibility is not written.
- Once 250 ms pass without a frame, every row that a frame rewrote and the writer did not write whole is written whole, as the frame wrote it, in one synchronized update that leaves the cursor where it is (the settle write). Pi makes no such write. A row the writer wrote whole is made whole by that write and waits for no settle write; the rows written in part, and the rows a frame rewrote without a change, are made whole by the settle write.

The writer makes no sequence of its own but the cursor address of a row's first column and a reset of the pen (`CSI 0 m`).

Conditions: frames are translated only while the alternate screen is shown and autowrap is known to be off: a write turned it off (`CSI ? 7 l`) and none since turned it on (`CSI ? 7 h`), switched to or from an alternate screen (modes 47, 1047, 1049), restored saved private modes (`CSI ? .. r`) or reset the terminal (DECSTR, RIS). While it is not known to be off every frame is sent as it came and the rows are forgotten; the first frame after it is turned off again writes its rows whole. Fullscreen mode, the default `tuiMode`, enters the alternate screen and turns autowrap off in one write. Regular mode never enters the alternate screen: every byte is sent as it came (a recorded run of 1,353 writes, 230,518 bytes, is the test data of `TestRegularModeIsSentAsItCame`). Before the alternate screen is entered and after it is left every byte is sent as it came. Print, JSON and RPC mode and `pig --version` build no screen writer, whatever the environment and the setting say (`TestNoScreenWriterOutsideInteractiveMode`, on the binary). `PI_TUI_WRITE_LOG` is above the writer and records the renderers' bytes.

What a user or a terminal can observe: nothing unless the writer is selected. With it selected, the bytes PiG sends while the alternate screen is shown, how many frames carry them, and one write Pi does not make. A program that reads PiG's raw output (`script`, `tmux pipe-pane`, a terminal's own log) reads other bytes than Pi's for the same frames: fewer, no frame where nothing changed, rows written from their first cell without an erase, and the settle write 250 ms after the last frame. Replay of the repository's recorded streams at 160 by 48, settle writes included (a replay, not a benchmark): the `md:20000` stream 485,204 of 543,833 bytes (89.2 %), the `write:50` stream 140,764 of 710,981 bytes (19.8 %). Where every frame is followed by a pause of 250 ms the settle write follows every frame: in a replay of two recorded sessions of twenty keys typed 400 and 255 ms apart (the writer's record log of each, settle writes where the log has them) the writer sends 15,463 of 16,271 and 15,572 of 16,509 bytes (95.0 % and 94.3 %), 213 bytes for a key whose frame has 219: the editor's row is as wide as the screen and changes through its last cell, so it is written whole and no settle write follows it. Recording runs at a9e540a32 on the lane's host (not benchmark rows): `write:200` 201,032 of 731,106 bytes (27.5 %), `write:400` 344,914 of 1,361,994 bytes (25.3 %).

What the terminal keeps of a line: tmux keeps, for a line, the cells it has (`capture-pane -N` prints them, and `capture-pane -e` ends the row's pen there) and how far cells were stored in it (`capture-pane -J` prints them); with autowrap off both depend on the order in which the cells arrived since the line was last erased whole, not only on what the row shows. The in-place rule is the one under which tmux keeps the lines the frame's own bytes leave it. Measured on tmux 3.4 (the Linux container the tests run in) and tmux 3.6 (host xd670): 51 cases, 1,794 rows each, identical on the two versions and recorded in `internal/screenwriter/gridterm/testdata/tmux-3.4.json` and `tmux-3.6.json`; `TestLinesAreWhatTmuxShows` asks the tmux of the machine it runs on and fails on a tmux whose lines differ. With autowrap on tmux stores runs of text as it reads them: not modelled, and the writer translates nothing then. A cluster whose width terminals disagree on keeps its place for a row to be written in place; this is shown on the tests' width models, not on a terminal. Terminals other than tmux: what was run with this rule is under the owner's condition (b) below.

Selecting the writer: the writer is opt-in in 0.8.0. The global setting `terminal.screenWriter` selects it with `"writer"`; `"pi"` is the renderers' bytes; the key left out, and any other value, mean the default, which one constant names (`TerminalScreenWriterDefault` in `internal/codingagent/settings.go`, now `"pi"`: with nothing selected the terminal is sent the bytes Pi sends). `PIG_TUI_SCREEN_WRITER`, when set and not empty, decides in the setting's place: `pi` (or `0`) sends the terminal the renderers' bytes as they are written; any other value selects the writer. The writer becomes the default when the owner's real-terminal condition is shown: Kitty, Ghostty and iTerm2 on his Mac, which only he can run (condition (b) below). A project settings file cannot set the key. The setting is read once, when interactive mode starts, before the alternate screen is entered; a change of the setting, `/reload` and a change applied in `/settings` take effect at the next start. `PIG_TUI_SCREEN_WRITER_LOG=path` records, with the writer on, every write as it came and as it was sent: it writes the screen's bytes, typed text included, to the file the user names.

Why: through tmux or ssh the bytes of a frame are what the terminal and the multiplexer read and parse, a row's unchanged cells included; the writer sends between a fifth and nine tenths of them on the streams above.

What the writer costs PiG, and what was cut: the writer reads every frame. For a row a frame writes it finds where the line ends, compares the line with the one the row keeps, reads what changed into cells and compares cells. Replay of the two recorded streams through the writer alone (`BenchmarkTranslate`, 160 by 48, median of 6 runs beside other jobs, 0 allocations a replay before and after): the `write:50` stream (3,262 frames, 1,499 rows written) 2.58 ms before the changes of a6e5efaad and 1.22 ms after, the `md:20000` stream (846 frames, 2,222 rows) 2.38 and 1.60 ms; a recorded session of the benchmark's write cell (559 frames, 1,598 rows) 1.65 and 1.10 ms, of its tmux cell 4.98 and 2.96 ms, of typed keys (37 frames) 95 and 63 microseconds. The changes send the same bytes: the SHA-256 of everything the writer sends for the two streams and the three sessions, settle writes included, is the same before and after, and the corpus of 292 recorded sessions (584 stream pairs) is the same file for file. They are: (1) `writeDiff` compares only the cells `parse` did not take over from the row's line: in a row of as many cells, the last cells `parse` kept are the cells of the same index; (2) a line's cells through the last one that is not a blank under the default pen are counted while the line is read; (3) a row that is written whole whatever its cells are keeps its line unread, and its cells are read when a later frame's write of the row depends on them, never for a row that is scrolled away, erased or forgotten first: a row of a frame that is sent as it came, a row no cursor address follows, a row that is not known, and a row without a cell written with a line of as many printable ASCII bytes as the screen has columns (read, such a line has that many columns and the row is written whole; not read, it is written as the frame wrote it: the same bytes); the line ends with a reset, so the pen after it is the default one, read or not; (4) `commonSuffix` compares sixty-four bytes at a time first. Where the replay's time went (CPU profile of the `write:50` stream): before, `writeDiff` 52 %, `parse` 23 %, finding the line's end 10 %; after (1) and (2), `parse` 33 %, the line's end 23 %, `writeDiff` 15 %. In the recorded session of the write cell 799 of 1,598 rows are written onto a row without a cell, which is what (3) spares. A rule that sends a whole frame as it came when most of its rows changed was measured and not built: the renderers write no row that shows its line already (2,222 of 2,222 rows written in the `md:20000` stream differ from the row's line, 1,499 of 1,499 in `write:50`, 105 of 105 in a session of typed keys, where a key's frame writes one row), so the share of changed rows is the same for a typed key and for a streamed answer. On the benchmark host (xd6702, reserved cores, one invocation of the kit with a pause of 1 s between steps, terminal environment alacritty-id, 20 rounds of which 15 to 17 pairs are kept, the writer selected against not selected in the same binary, judged by the scoreboard's rule): before the cut (173dbcd9c) the writer loses user instructions in `write:50` (+2.01e7, 1.6 %, 14 of 17 rounds higher) and `write:200` (+5.29e7, 1.1 %, 17 of 17) and own memory in `write:50` (+2.9 MB); with the cut (a6e5efaad) user instructions are a tie in `write:50` (+1.17e7, 1.0 %, 11 of 15 rounds higher, sign test p 0.12), in `write:200` (+3.10e7, 0.6 %, under the floor of 3.80e7) and in `md:20000` (+0.83e7, 8 of 15), CPU seconds and wall time are ties in the three steps, own memory is a tie in `write:200` and `md:20000` (+0.3 MB) and still a loss in `write:50` (+0.8 MB, 14 of 15 rounds, floor 0.35 MB; 2.1 MB less than before the cut). The two writers against each other: `write:50` user instructions -8.87e6 (lower in 17 of 17 rounds) and own memory -2.1 MB with the cut. The longest gap between writes in `write:50` stays longer with the writer (36.4 against 20.1 ms) because the writer does not send the frames that change nothing; the longest visible pause is a tie.

Pi bug: none. Pi has no layer under the terminal's write; its bytes are correct, only more.

Scope: `internal/screenwriter` (the writer; its test screens in `internal/screenwriter/gridterm`), `tui/terminal_screen_writer.go` (the switch and the terminal's hook), five hunks in `tui/terminal.go` (12 lines added, 1 changed against `release/set-pimain` at 9cd955810ed9), `internal/codingagent/settings.go` (the setting and its default), `internal/codingagent/interactive.go` (`NewInteractiveMode` applies the setting). The stripped release binary is 77,824 bytes larger on linux_amd64, 67,504 on darwin_arm64 and 79,360 on windows_amd64 than at that tip of `release/set-pimain` (measured at 48f431db6); the changes of a6e5efaad add 4,096 bytes on linux_amd64 (61,378,720 to 61,382,816).

Owner decision: approved as an OPT-IN writer. Default-on is NOT approved: it needs the real-terminal proof (Kitty, Ghostty and iTerm2 on the owner's Mac, which nobody has run) and a new line of the owner. The decision log (`OWNER-DECISIONS.md`) has four entries on the writer, quoted as they stand there. 2026-11-12T04:53Z: `DECIDED (owner: "I'm happy to do this if we're confident cause it sounds like the perfect divergence, pure performance nothing divergent from what a user expects from pi look/feel (other than faster and better scaling)"). APPROVED IN PRINCIPLE, gated on proof: a screen-parity renderer (next divergence number), replacing byte parity with SCREEN parity. (a) A cell-level diff writer that sends only changed cells.` 2026-11-12T14:42Z, among what that entry approves (the owner's words there: `"I'm fine with all but can't you do confinement on xd6702 since it's not being used for vllm?"`): `the screen-parity writer ON by default once the 250 ms longest pause is traced and either fixed or proven invisible, plus Kitty/Ghostty/iTerm2 coverage` (the line continues) and a third condition, given here in this record's words because the entry's own wording is a phrase this repository's public texts do not use (it is in that entry of the log): the whole parity suite passes in writer mode. 2026-11-13T03:33:12Z (section `owner: brand + experience`; the owner's words there: `"We replace pi with pig everywhere bro and for anything you think we can make a meaningful improvement to the experience do it"`), under "Applied": `Defaults that stay as recommended: screen writer opt-in until real-terminal proof; scoreboard gate read as no loss on any row and a win on every row the code can move.` 2026-11-13T03:48:42Z (section `owner: "Proceed with all recommendations."`): `Scoreboard gate: no loss on any row, win on every movable row. Screen writer: opt-in until real-terminal proof. p2 data dir ~/.pig/p2. 'Ask PiG'.` So the writer ships in 0.8.0 selected only by its setting or its variable, and the three conditions of the second entry are what default-on still waits for. The three conditions at this commit:
(a) "the 250 ms longest pause is traced and either fixed or proven invisible": shown for the writer as it was at 8c698d0e4, on the cells measured; measured again for `write:50` with the rule added since (rows as wide as the screen written whole), 30 rounds on the binary of 08cba8629: the visible gap p99 is within 0.08 ms of byte mode's 16.92 ms, the longest visible pause is equal, and the rule against the writer before it is a tie in 9 of 9 judged rows (27 pairs). Visible-gap rows on the benchmark host's reserved cores (10 rounds; byte mode, byte mode again as the floor, the writer): no pause of 250 ms lies inside a measured step of `write:50`, `md:20000`, `tmux:write:50` (5 rounds) or the interactive cell; the longest visible pause is 52.1 ms with the writer against 59.5 and 52.9 (`write:50`), 18.2 against 18.5 and 18.9 (`md:20000`), 101.5 against 104.9 and 106.0 (`tmux:write:50`). Where a window holds pauses longer than 250 ms (keys typed 400 and 255 ms apart) the settle write came 250.6 ms after the visible change before it and changed no cell. In replays of the record logs no visible change waits for the settle write (0 of 582, 585, 4,124, 66 and 66 records leave other cells; 0 of 53 settle writes change a cell; with the rule added since, 0 records differ on the tests' screens). Not explained: the visible p99 of `write:50` was 0.55 ms higher with the writer in 9 of 9 rounds (18.0 against 17.3 ms), which a second run of 4 rounds did not tell apart from two samples of byte mode. Not run: `write:200`, `write:400`, a terminal's own drawing time.
(b) "Kitty/Ghostty/iTerm2 coverage": not shown for the three applications on macOS, which no lane can run. Run on Linux with this rule, on the corpus of this tree's writer (292 recorded sessions, 584 stream pairs, 15,922 checkpoints a side, the screen after the writer's bytes against the screen after the bytes they were made from): kitty 0.50.0's screen code, every cell, 0 differ; the kitty 0.50.0 program in a window on a virtual display, 0 differ; libghostty-vt 1.4.0, Ghostty's terminal core, 0 differ; `make test-term-engines` (xterm.js, alacritty_terminal, wezterm-term, libvterm, pyte), 0 scenarios differ. iTerm2: not run at all.
(c) the whole parity suite passes in writer mode (this record's words for the third condition; its wording is in `OWNER-DECISIONS.md`, 2026-11-12T14:42Z): shown at 08cba8629 for the hermetic suite in CI's form (`make parity-fast`, both shards): 872 of the 872 scenarios that run pass with the default as committed, and 872 of 872 with the constant set to `"pi"`; 7 of the 879 scenario files do not run in the hermetic suite by their tags. Run again at a6e5efaad, where the default is `"pi"`: 872 of 872 with nothing selected (both shards rc 0; the fullscreen family run again with `PIG_TUI_SCREEN_WRITER_LOG` set leaves no log: no screen writer wrote) and 872 of 872 with the writer selected by `PIG_TUI_SCREEN_WRITER=writer` (the same family run leaves a log of 15,345,496 bytes). What that evidence is, and is not, is under "Parity allowance" below.

When the owner's real-terminal condition is shown the constant becomes `"writer"` and this record says so; if he declines the writer, `internal/screenwriter`, `tui/terminal_screen_writer.go`, the five hunks in `tui/terminal.go`, the setting, the markers and this record go.

The rule and the lines tmux keeps: `TestRandomFramesLeaveTheRenderersLines`, `TestRecordedMenuReturnLeavesTheRenderersLines`, `TestAClusterOfUncertainWidthKeepsItsPlace`, `TestEveryChangeOfARowKeepsItsLineOrForgetsTheRow` (`internal/screenwriter/lines_test.go`), the rows as wide as the screen that are written whole (`internal/screenwriter/whole_row_test.go`: `TestAFullWidthRowWrittenThroughItsLastCellIsWrittenWholeAndWaitsForNoSettleWrite`, `TestAScribbledFullWidthRowHealsByTheFrameThatChangesIt`, `TestARowNarrowerThanTheScreenWrittenThroughItsLastCellStillSettles`, `TestFullWidthRowsOfWideJoinedAndLinkedCells`, `TestAFullWidthRowUnderABackgroundOnTerminalsWithAndWithoutBackgroundColourErase`, `TestWhatFollowsARowWrittenThroughTheLastColumn`, `TestTheWriteInPlaceNeverPrintsInTheLastColumn`, `TestAResizeAfterAFullWidthRowWasWrittenWhole`, `TestImageRowsAreNeverWrittenByTheWriter`), `TestLinesAreWhatTmuxShows`, `TestLinesAreWhatTmuxRecorded` (`internal/screenwriter/gridterm`; CI's Linux job sets `PIG_GRIDTERM_TMUX_REQUIRED`, so the test that asks tmux cannot be skipped there). The screens: `TestRules`, `TestWriterMakesNoSequenceOfItsOwn`, `TestRecordedStreamsShowTheSameScreens`, `TestRandomEditsShowTheSameScreens`, `TestScribbledRowsHealByTheSettleWrite`, `TestResizeForgetsTheScreen`, `TestSettleAfterAResizeWritesNothing`, `TestDropSendsEveryWriteAsItCame` (`internal/screenwriter/writer_test.go`), `TestScreenWriterShowsTheRenderersScreens` (`tui/screen_writer_oracle_test.go`).
