## D647 A message or entry that an extension renders is drawn as the placeholder `[<customType>]` until the extension's first answer, where Pi draws the component it returned

What: Pi runs a custom message or entry renderer in its own process, inside the render that builds the component (`packages/coding-agent/src/modes/interactive/components/custom-message.ts` and `custom-entry.ts`, `rebuild`), so the first frame that shows the message shows the component the renderer returned, with its mouse regions. In pig the renderer runs in the extension's subprocess, and the host does not wait for it on the render loop (AGENTS.md: no blocking extension IPC on the TUI input or render loop). The first frame that shows the message therefore draws the proxy's placeholder, the one line `[<customType>]` (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/render_proxy.go`, `fallback`, read by `Render`), the request goes to the extension off the loop at that first `Render`, and the component is drawn by the frame that follows the answer. The placeholder is on screen from the message's first frame to that frame: when a session is resumed or switched, when the transcript is rebuilt, and when a new custom message or entry arrives. A click on the placeholder row is not lost: it is kept and given to the component by the frame that paints it (`holdEarlyClick`).

Measured 2026-11-14 on the line tip 8ca87fa349fa (branch `release/set-pimain`, lane RecordD647, host xd670, image tern-ci:v11, tmux 100x60), job `s1` (`~/lanes/RecordD647.out/s1.log`, summary `~/lanes/RecordD647.out/window.txt` on xd670) with the scripts of lane ClickPairLoss (`tmp/lane/s1.sh`, `trace_patch.py`, `window.py`; a scratch trace of the proxy and the frames, restored before the arms ran and never committed) on the session of scenario `extensions-runtime/106-click-pair-in-one-write-hits-drawn-layout` (two cards of a Node renderer; the tree after restore had no changed file): time from the first transcript frame that shows `[click-card]` to the answer of the last card.

| Host state | Runs | Placeholder on screen, ms (min / median / 90th / max) |
|---|---|---|
| Quiet | 5 | 36 / 45 / 46 / 46 |
| Two Go build loops (`go build -a ./cmd/pig`) on 8 cpus | 45 | 37 / 53 / 206 / 412 |

Under that load 10 of 45 windows were longer than 150 ms and none longer than 1 s. The renderer inactivity boundary (D56, five seconds) ends the wait: after it the placeholder stays and the request is repeated after a backoff.

Earlier measurements of the same window, each on its own tip: lane ClickPairLoss at 03547f16b650 (job s1, `~/lanes/ClickPairLoss.out/s1.log`; before the load fixes of car 15): quiet 44 / 62 / 73 / 73 ms (5 runs), under two build loops 34 / 78 / 297 / 587 ms (80 runs) and 38 / 118 / 301 / 597 ms (60 runs). Lane PlaceholderWindow (Main's log: 52.7 to 46.2 ms quiet and 104.5 to 74.2 ms loaded across the load fixes of car 15; its jobs ran at 8189055f4 on xd6702; I did not find its final table, so these two pairs are quoted from Main's log, not re-read). The window depends on the host's load, not on the extension's work: the renderer of the scenario answers in 1 to 2 ms (`proxy built->request sent` 1.6-3.0 ms and `first request->first answer` 27-209 ms in the p1 job of PlaceholderWindow, 6 runs at f8c75a47c, the time lost is in the loop and the process start).

| | Pi 1.5.2 | pig |
|---|---|---|
| First frame of the message (run, 03547f16b650 and 8ca87fa349fa) | the component: `card:second card:closed` | `[click-card]` on the component's first row, one row whatever the component's height |
| A click on that row before the component's frame (run, 03547f16b650) | the component's `MouseRegion` takes it | answered unhandled, kept, and given to the component by the frame that paints it: every click once, in the order made, before any later click (`holdEarlyClick`). Before the fix of lane ClickPairLoss it was dropped: 5 of 80 loaded runs of the click pair ended with the card closed, Pi 0 of 80 |
| Press, release, drag, move and wheel in that time (read, not run) | a region that names the event takes it | left to the host's text selection and scrolling: the host has acted on the gesture when the answer arrives |
| Selection, copy and search of the row in that time (read, not run) | the component's text | the placeholder's text |
| The rows below a component that is not one row high (read, not run) | drawn once | move when the component replaces its placeholder |

The three cells marked read were found in the code (`HandleMouse` passes only a click to `holdEarlyClick`; the host's selection and search read the drawn rows) and were not run: no test of the line runs a press, release, drag, move, wheel, selection, search or a taller component over a placeholder.

Why: the renderer lives in another process. Waiting for its answer inside the frame is blocking IPC on the render loop, which stalls every key and every other frame for as long as the extension takes, up to the inactivity boundary. Drawing nothing for the message instead of the placeholder moves every later row when the answer arrives.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: the `render_message` and `render_entry` proxies of every subprocess SDK, isolated and packed. Not a renderer that runs in the host's process (the in-process Go reference returns its component at once). Tool renderers, widgets, header and footer were not examined here.

Observable effect: for the time above a user sees `[<customType>]` where Pi shows the component. A user can click the row and the component acts on the click when it appears. A user cannot drag-select the component's own text, scroll a region inside it or use a region that answers press, drag, move or wheel until the component is drawn; and a click made on a placeholder whose extension never answers (the request fails, or the five-second boundary passes) does nothing.

Owner decision: owner approved 2026-11-14 (handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md, entry `2026-11-14T20:05:57Z owner: "I'm fine with all recommendations and if you can't reproduce the compaction error maybe it's fixed"`): "D647: APPROVED as a record WITHOUT a held frame (a message or entry that an extension renders is drawn as the placeholder `[<customType>]` until the extension's first answer; ...; numbers after the load fixes: about 46 ms quiet, 74 ms loaded)." The in-between asked for before that entry (the first request at the proxy's build, and a kept frame of 50, 100 or 150 ms on a load or resume, measured by lane PlaceholderWindow) is not taken, as the entry of 2026-11-14T20:05:57Z says ("WITHOUT a held frame"): the transcript's first frame would come later by the window; the first request still starts at the first `Render`.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-14

Remove when: the first frame that shows a message or entry draws the component the renderer returned, not `[<customType>]`, without a wait on the render loop: that is, a test that builds the proxy, draws it once and gets the component's rows with the loop never blocked (the renderer's answer obtained before the message's first frame, off the loop), or the renderer runs in the host's process.

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/render_proxy.go` (`Render`, where the placeholder rows are drawn; `holdEarlyClick`).

Evidence: `TestRenderProxyClickBeforeTheFirstFrameReachesTheDrawnComponent`, `TestRenderProxyClickBeforeAFailedFirstAnswerIsDropped`, `TestRenderProxyHeldClickDiesWithItsProxy`, `TestClickPairOfOneReadReachesTheCardBehindItsPlaceholder` (`coding/extension/host/subprocess/render_proxy_early_click_test.go`; they assert the placeholder `[card]` before the answer); `TestRenderProxyRenderNeverWaitsForIPC` (`render_proxy_test.go`, the placeholder `[notice]` with the loop never blocked); `TestRendererClickBeforeTheFirstFrameAcrossSDKs` (`test/extension-conformance/renderer_click_test.go`); the tmux scenario `extensions-runtime/106-click-pair-in-one-write-hits-drawn-layout` (the second card's click lands on the drawn component) and `extensions-runtime/96-message-click-expansion`, whose session passes through the placeholder and then the component. Mutations run 2026-11-14 at 8ca87fa349fa (`~/lanes/RecordD647.out/mut.log` on xd670): the placeholder not drawn turns six tests red (the four above, `TestRenderProxyRenderNeverWaitsForIPC`, `TestRenderProxyOutputPadRefreshRejectsStaleGeneration`); the early click dropped instead of kept turns `TestRenderProxyClickBeforeTheFirstFrameReachesTheDrawnComponent` and `TestClickPairOfOneReadReachesTheCardBehindItsPlaceholder` red.

Parity allowance: no comparator compares the placeholder frame itself: the parity scenarios `extensions-runtime/96-message-click-expansion` and `extensions-runtime/106-click-pair-in-one-write-hits-drawn-layout` pass through the placeholder and compare the screen once the component is drawn (106 also pins the kept click: the second card opens as in Pi), and the unit tests of `render_proxy_early_click_test.go` and `render_proxy_test.go` pin the placeholder row and the kept click. No test and no scenario of the line runs a press, release, drag, move or wheel over a placeholder, the selection, copy or search of the placeholder row, or the rows below a component taller than one row moving when it replaces the placeholder: those cells of the table above rest on reading the code. A scenario that sends input to a rendered card must wait for the card's text: the runner's ready gate passes on `capture_start` alone (`waitForPaneOrCapture`), which the placeholder frame already shows.

## D648 A Rust extension receives U+FFFD for a lone UTF-16 surrogate, and writes it back when it echoes text, where Pi's JavaScript string keeps the unit

What: a JavaScript string can hold a lone UTF-16 surrogate (`"\ud83d"` alone), and the host spells it as the escape `\ud83d` in a frame. The Go, Node, Python and Lisp SDKs hand the extension the unit and write it back unchanged. A Rust `String` is UTF-8 and a `serde_json::Value` string cannot hold the unit, and `serde_json` refuses the frame that spells it, which ended the connection. The Rust SDK's frame decode (`extensions/sdk-rs/src/protocol.rs`, `decode_frame` and `decode_json_str`) therefore decodes a refused frame or value again with each lone surrogate escape written as U+FFFD. A Rust handler (`on_event` over a `Value`, `on_before_agent_start` and its `snapshot()`, a command's `args: &str`, a tool's arguments) holds ONE U+FFFD for the unit, and a result or host call it builds from that text (an `input` transform, a `tool_result` content, a `context` message, `sendUserMessage`, `sendMessage`, `registerProvider`) carries ONE U+FFFD back to the host. Pi's extension in JavaScript echoes the unit. The typed UTF-16 fields (`JsString`: terminal input, editor text) keep every unit, and a frame without a lone surrogate is untouched. Since v0.5.0 (the first Rust SDK that survived such a frame).

Why: the lossless alternative is a Rust value type that holds UTF-16 units wherever a `Value` or `String` is in the public API (`serde_json::Value` in `on_event`, `&str` in `args`, `String` in every snapshot field). That is a breaking change of the Rust SDK's public types for every Rust extension, and it is its own lane after the release. Until it lands the decode must not refuse the frame, and a replacement is what Go's decoder makes in a `string`.

Pi bug: none. (The host-side loss the same rows found, eight event types that wrote U+FFFD on the way out to every SDK, is cured in 0.8.0 and carries no divergence.)

Scope: the Rust SDK (isolated and packed) on every frame position that is not a `JsString` field. No other SDK; no host code.

Owner decision: owner approved option (c) 2026-11-14T03:47:02Z ("c", row 92, Rust SDK lone surrogates): a numbered Rust-SDK divergence for 0.8.0, pinned by a conformance row and a docs line; the lossless Rust value type is a post-release lane.
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:17d47183a2944905

Remove when: the Rust SDK has a lossless value type for JavaScript strings (UTF-16 units) in its public event, command and snapshot types, so a Rust handler holds the unit and writes it back.

Call-site markers: `extensions/sdk-rs/src/protocol.rs` (`decode_frame`, `decode_json_str`).

Evidence: `test/extension-conformance/lone_surrogate_passthrough_test.go` (`TestConformance_LoneSurrogatePassThroughAcrossSDKs`: every Rust cell asserts one U+FFFD by `rustHoldsD648`, every other bridge asserts the unit) and `TestConformance_ToolArgumentStringsAgreeAcrossSDKs`.

Parity allowance: observable only to a Rust extension that is sent a lone surrogate (a model or tool that produced one) and echoes the text.

## D649 A native extension's settle-tail handler in flight when RPC input ends does not hold the exit, where Pi's process stays while the handler holds a handle

What: In RPC mode Pi starts its shutdown when stdin closes. With a second extension's `session_shutdown` handler that never settles, so that the dispose cannot return, Pi's process stays alive as long as its event loop holds a handle: an `agent_settled` handler of a Node extension that waits on a 3 s timer holds the exit for 3010-3020 ms (Pi 1.5.2 under a pipe, Node 24.22.2, 2026-11-14; without the second extension Pi exits at once, 5-15 ms). pig counts a settle-tail handler (`agent_before_settle`, `agent_settled`, a reload's handlers) of a runtime that has no microtask continuation as waiting once input ended (`Host.tailWaitsLocked`), so it does not hold the exit: a Go SDK `agent_settled` handler that waits on a 3 s timer, with and without watching its context, lets pig exit 0 after 15-40 ms where Pi holds 3.0 s. The cells are equal for a handler that waits on nothing (Pi: exit at once; pig: exit at once).

Why: a native SDK cannot tell a settle-tail handler that waits on a timer from one that waits for the shutdown itself to cancel it, and the second kind must not hold the exit: `TestRPCInputEndDuringGoSDKSettleTailHandlerShutsDown` pins a Go handler that waits until the shutdown cancels it, which Pi has no counterpart of (a Node handler that waits for a shutdown signal holds no handle, so Pi's process ends under it). Holding for every native settle-tail handler would make that process wait for ever.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: an `agent_settled`, `agent_before_settle` or reload handler of an extension in a Go, Rust, Python or Lisp runtime that waits on a timer or another handle when RPC stdin closes. `coding/extension/host/subprocess` (`Host.tailWaitsLocked`). The interactive quit is in scope too: in the settle tail Pi's quit does not wait for an `agent_settled` handler (Pi 92-98 ms, pig 71-95 ms with a 3 s timer handler and no second extension), but with a second extension whose `session_shutdown` handler never settles beside it, Pi's loop stays alive for the handler's timer: Pi holds the quit 2397-2415 ms and a Node handler holds it 2398-2415 ms in pig, while a Go handler on the same 3 s timer ends it after 92-95 ms (five runs a side, Pi 1.5.2, Node 24.22.2, tmux, tip of `fix/quit-against-event-handlers-as-pi`; `~/lanes/QuitHandlerRows.out/CELLS-AFTER.md`).

Owner decision: owner approved 2026-11-14T14:10:39Z ("proceed", on the recommendations of 13:2xZ; handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md, entry `2026-11-14T14:10:39Z owner: "proceed" (on Claude's recommendations of 13:2xZ)`): "D649 APPROVED: at RPC input end a native-SDK settle-tail handler does not hold the exit." The Scope sentence for the interactive quit is approved by the owner on 2026-11-14T20:05:57Z (handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md, entry `2026-11-14T20:05:57Z owner: "I'm fine with all recommendations and if you can't reproduce the compaction error maybe it's fixed"`, item ROWS 112 + 113: "D649 Scope: the interactive quit is in scope").

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-14

Remove when: the SDKs report whether a handler waits on a handle (a timer, a socket, a child process), so that the host can hold the exit for exactly the handlers Pi holds it for.

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/host.go` (`Host.tailWaitsLocked`).

Evidence: `coding/cli/rpc_shutdown_drain_go_test.go` (`TestRPCInputEndDuringGoSDKSettleTailHandlerShutsDown`: the handler that waits for the shutdown's cancel does not hold the exit) and `coding/extension/host/subprocess/quit_every_runtime_test.go` (`TestQuitDrainDoesNotWaitForANativeSettleTailHandler`).

Parity allowance: observable only to an extension in a Go, Rust, Python or Lisp runtime whose `agent_settled`, `agent_before_settle` or reload handler waits on a handle when RPC stdin closes during a quit whose dispose cannot return: pig exits where Pi holds until the handle is gone.

## D650 A native extension handler that waits on nothing holds a quit whose dispose cannot return, where Pi's process exits at once

What: Pi runs every extension in one Node process. At a quit (interactive: ctrl+d after the TUI stopped; RPC: stdin closed) whose dispose cannot return, because a `session_shutdown` handler or an awaited run handler never settles, the process still exits 0 as soon as its one event loop is empty: a handler that awaits a promise that never settles holds no handle. A handler that waits on a 3 s timer holds the exit for as long as the timer (and an interval or a listening socket for ever). pig runs a Go, Rust, Python or Lisp extension in a process of its own that reports no event loop, so the host cannot tell a handler that waits on a timer from one that waits on nothing: it sees only that the handler is in flight. The host holds the quit for a handler that is in flight where Pi holds it for a handle, so a handler that waits on nothing holds the quit for ever. The extension command is the kind this record decides on purpose (`Conn.holdsLoop`: an unanswered command of a native runtime holds the quit, unless the host serves only dialogs for it, which nothing can answer after input ended and which hold no handle in Pi either); the other kinds are held by the dispose itself, which awaits the run (message_end, turn_end, agent_end, tool_call, tool_result, context) or the handler (session_shutdown).

Each cell was run on 2026-11-14 with Pi 1.5.2 and pig of this record's place, Node 24.22.2, under tmux (interactive) or over a pipe (RPC), a second extension with a `session_shutdown` handler that never settles beside the handler of the row; "never" is a handler that awaits a promise that never settles (Node) or a goroutine that blocks for ever (Go SDK, with and without watching its context), "timer" a handler that waits 3 s on a timer. Milliseconds from the key or from the close to the exit; "no exit in 8 s" is the watch's end:

| Kind (RPC close / interactive ctrl+d) | Pi, never | pig native, never | Pi, 3 s timer | pig native, 3 s timer |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| command, RPC | exit 0 after 10-20 ms | no exit in 8 s | 3012-3020 ms | 3011-3015 ms |
| command, interactive | 96 ms | no exit in 7.7 s (before this place: 92-96 ms) | 2690-2698 ms | 2696-2700 ms |
| turn_end, RPC | 15 ms | no exit in 8 s | 3012-3020 ms | 3024-3031 ms |
| turn_end, interactive | 95-129 ms | no exit in 7.7 s | 2391-2404 ms | 2394-2402 ms |
| agent_end, RPC | 15 ms | no exit in 8 s | 3014 ms | 3025-3033 ms |
| agent_end, interactive | 93 ms | no exit in 7.7 s | 2413 ms | 2394-2402 ms |
| agent_settled, interactive | 92-97 ms | 90-95 ms (before this place: no exit in 7.7 s) | 2397-2415 ms | 92-95 ms (Go; before this place: 2390-2406 ms; D649) |
| session_shutdown, RPC | 20-45 ms | no exit in 8 s | 3021-3024 ms | 3025-3041 ms |
| session_shutdown, interactive | 97 ms | no exit in 7.7 s | 3092-3100 ms | 3089-3095 ms |

A native (Go, Python, Rust) handler of `tool_call`, `tool_result` or `context` that never returns and ignores the cancel signal holds an abort and a quit, because the host awaits the run's handler as Pi does (`runner.ts:1256-1274`, no race against the abort; `TestToolCallEventHandlerIsAwaitedThroughTheAbort`, `coding/extension/host/subprocess/quit_event_handlers_test.go`) and a native process reports no empty loop: the owner accepted this on 2026-11-14T20:05:57Z; ctrl+c, SIGINT, SIGTERM and SIGHUP end it (`TestInteractiveHeldQuitOfANativeCommandEndsOnTheKeyAndTheSignalsPiEnds`), and a handler that honours `ctx.Done()` (Go) or `is_cancelled()` (Python, Rust) returns and the run ends about 305 ms after the abort. The timer columns are equal. Only the Go SDK was measured for the cells with a native handler; the host's rule (`Conn.holdsLoop`) does not depend on the SDK, and the rows of the command kind run over the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs.

What a user sees and what ends a quit that is held for ever (the same run, interactive, a Node interval in Pi against a native command that never returns in pig): the screen keeps the last frame, and the final frame and the restore sequence written after the key are the same in Pi and pig (2.70 KB in Pi, 2.64 KB in pig). ctrl+c ends it after 28 ms in Pi, 27-29 ms in pig (exit 130); SIGINT 24 ms and 24-25 ms (130); SIGTERM 26 ms in Pi (143); pig did not end at SIGTERM or SIGHUP at such a quit before this place's cure (no exit in 5.5 s) and now ends at once (143), as Pi does; SIGHUP 26 ms in Pi (129). A second ctrl+d does nothing in Pi and in pig. In RPC mode, where a second stdin close cannot happen, a single SIGTERM or SIGHUP during the shutdown does not interrupt it in Pi (no exit in 5 s) and in pig; a second SIGTERM exits 143 after 415 ms in both, SIGINT ends the process by the signal after 10 ms in Pi and 15 ms in pig.

Why: a native SDK cannot tell a handler that waits on a timer, a socket or a child process from one that waits on nothing, and ending the quit while a handler holds a handle would cut off work that Pi finishes: a command on a TCP read ended 53 ms after its peer answered in Pi, a 3 s timer held Pi for 3.09 s. Holding while the handler is in flight keeps Pi's results for the cases that hold, and costs the quit that Pi's gets from a handler that waits on nothing. The user is never without an exit: the keys and signals above end the process at once, with the terminal restored and Pi's status.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: a handler of an extension in a Go, Rust, Python or Lisp runtime (the host cannot read its wait) of the kinds above, in a quit whose dispose cannot return. `coding/extension/host/subprocess` (`Conn.holdsLoop`, `Host.evaluateLoopDrain`). A dispose that returns ends the process at once as in Pi; a Node extension reports its own idle loop and is not in scope.

Owner decision: owner approved 2026-11-14T14:10:39Z ("proceed", on the recommendations of 13:2xZ; handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md, entry `2026-11-14T14:10:39Z owner: "proceed" (on Claude's recommendations of 13:2xZ)`): "D650 APPROVED: a native handler that waits on nothing holds a quit until ctrl+c / a signal (27-29 ms, exit 130, as Pi); SIGTERM / SIGHUP end a held quit."

Owner decision (rows 112 + 113): owner approved 2026-11-14T20:05:57Z (handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md, entry `2026-11-14T20:05:57Z owner: "I'm fine with all recommendations and if you can't reproduce the compaction error maybe it's fixed"`, item ROWS 112 + 113): the run-awaited kinds name message_end, tool_call, tool_result and context; the Node-runtime defect sentence is deleted; ACCEPTED: a native handler of tool_call / tool_result / context that never returns and ignores the cancel signal holds an abort and a quit, ended by ctrl+c, SIGINT, SIGTERM or SIGHUP.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-14

Remove when: the SDKs report whether a handler waits on a handle (a timer, a socket, a child process), or the host can read it.

Call-site markers: `coding/extension/host/subprocess/conn.go` (`Conn.holdsLoop`).

Evidence: `coding/cli/interactive_quit_every_runtime_linux_test.go` (`TestInteractiveQuitWaitsForACommandInFlight`: a command in flight holds the interactive quit in the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs and in a packed Node cell; `TestInteractiveHeldQuitOfANativeCommandEndsOnTheKeyAndTheSignalsPiEnds`: ctrl+c, SIGINT, SIGTERM and SIGHUP end the held quit within 0.5 s with Pi's status, a second ctrl+d does not), `coding/cli/rpc_held_quit_native_command_signal_test.go` (`TestRPCHeldQuitOfANativeCommandEndsOnTheSignalsPiEnds`), `test/extension-conformance/quit_every_runtime_conformance_test.go` (`TestConformance_QuitWaitsForACommandInFlight` over every SDK, isolated, packed and fused) and `coding/extension/host/subprocess/quit_every_runtime_test.go` (`TestQuitDrainWaitsForANativeCommandInFlight`).

Parity allowance: observable only to an extension in a Go, Rust, Python or Lisp runtime whose handler of the kinds above never returns and holds no handle, at a quit whose dispose cannot return: pig holds the quit where Pi exits, and the keys and signals end it.

## D651 Credential and state files are replaced through a temporary file where that changes nothing but the inode, where Pi writes every file in place

What: in the files pig and Pi share (`auth.json`, `settings.json`, `trust.json`, `keybindings.json`, the MCP credential store, `mcp.json`, the models store `models-store.json`, and the two start-up migrations that write them) pig and Pi write differently. The record has two parts: part A and part C (the lock heartbeat) are built and landed.

### Part A: the writes (landed)

Pi writes each of these files with `writeFileSync`: it opens the file, truncates it and writes. A write that stops after the truncate (a full disk, a size limit, a kill, a power loss) leaves the file empty or cut short, and the next login or token refresh of one provider then saves a file that holds that provider alone (the owner's report of being signed out of every provider). pig writes a temporary file in the same directory (a new file is created with the mode its writer asks for under the umask, as Pi's open does; a file that exists is replaced through a private 0600 temporary file that then takes the mode, owner, group, attributes and access of the file it replaces), syncs it, syncs the directory, and renames it over the file (`internal/atomicfile` `WriteFile`), so a failed write leaves the old file. The rule: pig replaces the file when that changes nothing but the inode; otherwise it writes the file in place with the same bytes, exactly as Pi does, once, and says so in one line of the debug log (`PIG_DEBUG`, `internal/codingagent/atomic_debug_log.go`); that is not an error and not a warning. In place is the answer where

- the file has more than one hard link (a rename would leave the other names with the old bytes);
- an extended attribute cannot be copied onto the replacement, for a reason other than a file system that keeps none (they are copied when the file system takes them: POSIX ACLs on Linux, the user's attributes on macOS; the file keeps exactly the access it had, so an ACL entry that the directory's default ACL would give the new file is removed when the replaced file had none);
- on Windows, the DACL's entries (with each entry's flags) or SE_DACL_PROTECTED cannot be given to the temporary file, which is read back before anything is replaced (see "Windows security descriptor" below);
- the temporary file cannot be created in the directory (EACCES, EROFS, EPERM: a directory the process cannot write, holding a file it can), or the rename is refused (EBUSY, EXDEV, ETXTBSY: a file mounted into a container on its own is a mount point);
- the file is not a regular file (a device such as /dev/null, a named pipe): a rename would replace the node itself with an ordinary file that has its mode, and the bytes would never reach it.

A write that fails for a real reason (ENOSPC, EIO) is an error, in place or not, as in Pi; a read-only file is refused as Pi's write refuses it. A directory flush that fails for a real reason is an error; EINVAL, ENOTSUP, EOPNOTSUPP and ENOSYS on the flush and EACCES on opening the directory are accepted as a file system that has none. What is kept as Pi leaves it: the bytes, the mode, owner and group (where the process may set them), a symbolic link (its target is replaced and the link stays; a link to a missing file creates the file, a link to a missing directory fails with ENOENT and a loop with ELOOP, as Pi's open does), the extended attributes, and on Windows the DACL, attributes and streams of the replaced file (ReplaceFileW). EXDEV cannot happen for the same-directory temporary file; it is on the list only for a file system that reports it for a rename within one directory (an overlay or a FUSE mount). The writers that take no lock (the keybindings save and migration) write in place in a directory the process cannot write, as Pi does.

Windows: ReplaceFileW refuses with ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED, ERROR_SHARING_VIOLATION or ERROR_UNABLE_TO_REMOVE_REPLACED (another process holds the file open without delete sharing: an editor, an indexer, a sync client) and ERROR_UNABLE_TO_MOVE_REPLACEMENT; pig retries the replacement for `fsretry.RenameBudget`, then returns the error, and never turns that refusal into an in-place write. A ReplaceFileW that Windows stops half way (ERROR_UNABLE_TO_MOVE_REPLACEMENT_2, 1177: the replaced file under its backup name, nothing at the path) is finished by moving the replacement into place, or reversed by moving the backup back; when neither works the error names the kept files and nothing is deleted. The case that may break a working Pi setup is a credential file another program keeps open without delete sharing: Pi writes it in place and pig fails after the retry budget. Run on NTFS (GitHub Windows runner, run 43709055821, job `Windows native-4`: `ok github.com/MichaelKinsy/PiG/internal/atomicfile 12.800s`, not -v): the whole Windows test set of `internal/atomicfile` passes, among them `TestWindowsWriteFileWaitsOutAHandleHeldWithoutDeleteSharing`, `TestWindowsWriteFileFailsWhenTheHandleIsNeverClosed`, `TestWindowsReplaceKeepingBackupFinishesAReplacementThatStoppedHalfWay` and `TestWindowsReplaceKeepingBackupRestoresTheOriginalWhenItCannotFinish` (a pass of the package; the names are not in that log). Still unrun: another file system than NTFS, a real editor, indexer or sync client holding a credential file (the tests hold a handle themselves), and a Windows run of the release line.

Windows security descriptor: a write leaves the whole security descriptor of the file as Pi's in-place write does. The parts that decide who may open the file (the DACL's entries with each entry's flags, and SE_DACL_PROTECTED) are carried onto the temporary file before any data is written to it and read back there; if the temporary file cannot take them nothing has been replaced yet, and the write goes in place as Pi writes it, without an error. After ReplaceFileW succeeds the descriptor of the old file, which the backup still is, is carried back and read back; if the entries or SE_DACL_PROTECTED still differ the old file is moved back from the backup and the write fails, with an error that names the file at the path and the backup when even that cannot be done. A file that already has the descriptor is not touched. The carry sets the entries and SE_DACL_PROTECTED with SetKernelObjectSecurity (a call that runs no inheritance), which the first Windows run (the matrix, run 43699097748) showed keeps them in every starting descriptor and clears SE_DACL_AUTO_INHERITED whatever the source had. SE_DACL_AUTO_INHERITED is bookkeeping, not access, and is carried too: when the source has it and the call left it off, the same call is made with the request for auto-inheritance in the descriptor's control (the matrix prints it as a probe), and then SetNamedSecurityInfo, which the run showed sets the flag but also runs inheritance (it adds the parent's inheritable entries to an unprotected DACL and clears the inherited flag of the entries of a protected one); a call that changes the entries is undone by the first call, which leaves the flag off, and a carry that leaves only the flag different is a success. SetNamedSecurityInfo is made on a temporary file only (before any data is written to it, or, when ReplaceFileW stopped half way, while it is not yet at the path) and on the file at the path never: there the flag that ReplaceFileW always leaves set stays, and only the request for auto-inheritance can set it again after the entries were set. ReplaceFileW itself, the run showed, always leaves SE_DACL_AUTO_INHERITED set on the file and may drop the inherited flag of entries or add explicit copies of inherited entries (the same principals, types and rights); the carry from the backup puts the old entries back, and the matrix compares what ReplaceFileW left with the old file by access (protection and each entry's type, rights and trustee) and the final descriptor whole. A source that is not protected, has no flag and whose DACL equals the temporary file's leaves the temporary file protected (the call does not unprotect it then): the write goes in place, as Pi writes it. The owner and group are carried as far as the token may set them (ERROR_INVALID_OWNER, ERROR_INVALID_PRIMARY_GROUP, ERROR_PRIVILEGE_NOT_HELD and a file that cannot be opened for WRITE_OWNER are ignored, as EPERM from chown is on Unix). SHOWN BY RUN 43709055821 (GitHub Actions, the Windows runner, NTFS, on the tip of the main-side branch fix/credential-store-atomic-write-main, faaf12c29e58; the release line carries the files of internal/atomicfile and the Windows caller test byte for byte as that tip has them): every cell of the matrix passed (ten starting descriptors on five routes: 50 cells, each checked after the first write and after a second one), and `TestAuthStoragePreservesExistingACL`, the new-file test and the test of a parent with an inheritable Everyone entry passed, 0 failures. NOT SHOWN: a file system other than NTFS (ReFS, FAT and exFAT, a network share or a mapped drive may refuse the carry, in which case the write goes in place, or may set SE_DACL_AUTO_INHERITED whatever it is given); which call set SE_DACL_AUTO_INHERITED in a cell that needed it (the call as made, the request for auto-inheritance or SetNamedSecurityInfo: a passing run prints no probe, so the run does not say); that SetNamedSecurityInfo leaves the entries of an auto-inherited file as they are when the parent's inheritable entries changed after the file was made (the cells have the parent as it was); a run on the release line itself (nothing on the line runs Windows: the transfer rests on the byte equality of the files the run compiled). The evidence is `TestAuthStoragePreservesExistingACL`, `TestWindowsSecurityDescriptorMatrix`, `TestWindowsWriteFileOfANewFileGivesTheStoresDescriptor` and `TestWindowsInheritanceDoesNotReachAProtectedFileAfterAWrite` in the Windows job, and, run on Linux with injected steps, `TestCarryAccessChoosesTheCallsByWhatTheReadBackShows`, `TestCarryAccessReturnsAFailedReadBack`, `TestWriteFileWritesInPlaceWhereTheTemporaryFileCannotTakeTheAccess`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupCarriesTheOldDescriptorOntoTheNewFile`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupSucceedsWhenOnlyTheAutoInheritedFlagDiffers`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupRestoresTheOldFileWhenTheAccessDiffersAfterTheReplacement`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupRestoresTheOldFileWhenTheCarryFails`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupKeepsBothFilesWhenTheOldFileCannotBeRestored`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupCarriesTheDescriptorAroundTheMoveOfAHalfWayReplacement`, `TestCarryVerdict` and `TestIgnoreOwnerRefusal`.

Windows SE_DACL_AUTO_INHERITED: run 43709055821 (NTFS, the GitHub Windows runner) passed every cell of the matrix, including the cells of the routes this record had named as possibly not round-tripping the flag, and with them the whole-descriptor check of the final file: (1) the file at the path after ReplaceFileW for a source that has the flag and whose entries ReplaceFileW changed (cells `AI inherited entries (os.WriteFile)`, `owner Administrators, AI inherited entries`, `AI explicit entry and inherited entries` and `AI, the token's default DACL as explicit entries, nothing inherited`, route `ReplaceFileW and the steps of replace`); (2) a temporary file whose source has the flag (the same four cells and `PAI owner-only entry` and `PAI Everyone-read entry (the administrator case)` on the routes `WriteFile`, `ReplaceFileW that stops half way (1177)` and `the file system without ReplaceFileW (carry, then MoveFileEx)`); (3) a source without the flag, and a source whose flag ReplaceFileW already leaves set with equal entries. On NTFS no route of the matrix left the flag different from the source's. Two fallbacks stay as written above: an auto-inherited source with explicit entries in an inheriting directory, and an unprotected source without the flag, are written in place. What the run does NOT show: any file system but NTFS; which call set the flag (a passing run prints no probe); a run of the release line.

What was run (2026-11-14, xd670, tern-ci:v11, container user kinsy, not root; logs in `~/lanes/CredStore3.out/` on xd670): each cell is the result of a login (`Set`), a token refresh, a settings change, a trust decision, a keybindings save and the keybindings migration, in `fs-before.log` (line tip before the branch, 640f2d2740aa), `fs-stamped.log` (49de76c76066, atomic replace without the fallback), `fs-after.log` (the fallback), `fs-pi.log` (Pi 1.5.2, vendored dist):

| Case | before the branch | 49de76c76066 | with the fallback | Pi 1.5.2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| auth.json, settings.json, trust.json each mounted as ONE file (`docker -v file:/work/file`) | every write succeeds, in place | every write fails: login `rename /work/auth.json.tmp-N /work/auth.json: device or resource busy`; the same text for the refresh, the settings change (shown as the startup diagnostic `Invalid settings file ...`), the trust decision and the keybindings save; the keybindings migration is skipped silently | every write succeeds, in place | every write succeeds, in place |
| `chmod 555` directory holding 600 files: auth, settings, trust | login and refresh fail `EACCES: permission denied, mkdir '<dir>/auth.json.lock'`; the settings and trust writes fail at their lock the same way | identical | identical | identical (the lock directory is created first; the settings setter does not throw and records the error) |
| the same directory: keybindings save and migration (no lock) | written in place | save fails `open <dir>/keybindings.json.tmp-N: permission denied`; migration skipped (file unchanged) | written in place | migration written in place |
| control: a plain writable directory | all succeed | all succeed | all succeed | all succeed |

So the regression the fallback removes is the single-file mount for every file, and the unwritable directory for the two lock-free writers. Auth, settings and trust in an unwritable directory fail at the lock directory on every build including Pi; nothing changed there.

### Part C: the async lock renewal (approved 2026-11-14; landed with `fix/credential-lock-heartbeat-on-line`)

Pi's async credential lock (an OAuth refresh in flight) is renewed by proper-lockfile every stale/2 = 15 s with a 30 s stale window, while Pi's sync acquirers (every process that opens a credential store: the constructor, a footer render, a login selector) take any lock older than 10 s. A refresh that runs more than 10 s therefore has its lock taken by the next process that opens a store, and the holder learns of it only at its next heartbeat. pig renews the async lock every 5 s (`asyncUpdate` in `internal/pilock/lock.go`), so a live holder is never taken over.

What a user observes: with two pig processes on one `auth.json` and a provider that rotates a single-use refresh token, a refresh that takes more than 10 s is the only one that spends the token (Pi 1.5.2 spends it twice, and the provider then revokes the login); a process that opens the credential store while the refresh runs waits 180 ms (the sync acquirer's ten attempts, `SyncAttempts` 10 at `SyncDelay` 20 ms) on the fresh lock and then carries on, where Pi takes the lock at once. A refresher that starts late reads the holder's credential and keeps it; Pi overwrites it.

What was run (2026-11-14, xd670, tern-ci:v11; logs in `~/lanes/CredStore3.out/` on xd670; scenario `hbjob2.sh` with `zz_hb_probe_test.go.ai` and `pi_hb_probe.mjs`): one `auth.json` with four providers; `slow` refreshes openai-codex (single-use refresh token `r-old`) and holds the async lock for 12 s; `queued` is a second refresher of the same provider started at 1 s; `late1`, `late2`, `late3` start at 10.2, 10.6 and 10.9 s; `newstore` opens a credential store at 11 s. Three runs per column:

| Per cell | pig without the 5 s heartbeat (47be7294807f), `hb2-before.log` | pig with it (ebbaab0ce85a), `hb2-after.log` | Pi 1.5.2, `hb2-pi.log` |
|---|---|---|---|
| the process that opens a store at 11 s | gets the lock at once (0 s) in 3 runs: the live holder's lock is taken | waits 180 ms in 3 runs: the lock is fresh and is not taken | gets the lock at once (2 ms, 5 ms, 2 ms) |
| times the single-use token `r-old` was spent | 2 in 3 of 3 runs | 1 in 3 of 3 runs | 2 in 3 of 3 runs |
| credential in the file at the end | `slow`'s; the refresh a late refresher wrote earlier is overwritten and its refresh token is lost | `slow`'s | `slow`'s; the late refresher's credential is overwritten |
| providers kept in the file | all four | all four | all four |

The red test is `internal/pilock` `TestAsyncLockHeldPastTheSyncStaleWindowIsNotTaken` (red at f0598bed36, green with fbe92b66a0). Not run: Windows, a real provider.

Why: (A) a credential or settings write must not be able to destroy the file it replaces, and every setup in which Pi's in-place write works (a single file mounted into a container, a hard link, an ACL, a directory the process cannot write) keeps working as before. (C) with a provider that rotates a single-use refresh token, a lock taken from a live holder lets a queued refresher spend the same token a second time, and the provider then revokes the login.

Pi bug: yes for part A: Pi's truncate-then-write can empty `auth.json` on a failed write, and part C's double spend of a single-use token.

Scope: pig's writes of the listed files through `internal/atomicfile`. Pi's file formats, names and locations are unchanged.

Owner decision: approved 2026-11-14T14:10:39Z ("proceed", handoffs/0.8.0/OWNER-DECISIONS.md, entry `## 2026-11-14T14:10:39Z owner: "proceed" (on Claude's recommendations of 13:2xZ)`); the line, verbatim: "D651 APPROVED, both parts: (1) auth.json, settings, trust, keybindings and the MCP config replaced through a private temp file + rename where that changes nothing but the inode; Pi's in-place write kept for hard links, uncarriable ACL/xattrs, bind-mounted single files and unwritable directories; (2) a 5 s refresh of the async lock so a live refresher is never taken over (single-use refresh token spent once)."

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-14

Remove when: (A) Pi writes these files through a temporary file and a rename, or otherwise keeps a failed write from truncating them; (C) Pi renews its async lock faster than its sync stale window, or raises the sync stale window to at least twice the async heartbeat.

Call-site markers: `internal/atomicfile/atomicfile.go` (`WriteFile`, `writeInPlace`); the writers `ai/auth.go` (`writeAuthFile`), `ai/models_store.go`, `coding/mcpext/storage.go`, `coding/mcpext/config.go`, `internal/codingagent/settings.go`, `internal/codingagent/trust_manager.go`, `internal/codingagent/keybindings.go` and `internal/codingagent/migrations.go` (three writes). Part C: `internal/pilock/lock.go` (`asyncUpdate` and `tryAcquireResolved`).

Evidence: `internal/atomicfile` tests: `TestWriteFileWritesInPlaceWhereTheRenameIsRefused`, `TestWriteFileKeepsTheFileWhenTheRenameFailsForAnotherReason`, `TestWriteFileWritesInPlaceInADirectoryItCannotWrite`, `TestWriteFileCannotCreateAFileInADirectoryItCannotWrite`, `TestWriteFileReportsARealFailureOfTheInPlaceWrite`, `TestWriteFileWritesInPlaceThroughAHardLink`, `TestWriteFileReplacesAPlainFileAndReportsNothing`, `TestWriteFileWritesInPlaceWhenAnAttributeCannotBeCarried`, `TestWriteFileCarriesExtendedAttributesOver`, `TestWriteFileKeepsAPosixACL`, `TestWriteFileFollowsSymlink`, `TestWriteFileRefusesReadOnlyFile`, `TestWriteFileCreatesPrivateAndKeepsMode`, `TestWriteFileKeepsExactlyTheAccessOfTheFileItReplaces`, `TestWriteFileGivesANewFileTheModeOpenGives`, `TestWriteFileWritesANonRegularTargetInPlace`, `TestWriteFileThroughALinkEndsWhereAnInPlaceWriteEnds`, `TestWriteFileFollowsALinkToAMissingTarget`, `TestWriteFileFollowsAChainOfLinksToAMissingTarget`, `TestWriteFileFailsLikeWriteFileSyncForALinkItCannotFollow`, `TestWriteFileReportsADirectorySyncFailure`, `TestWriteFileToleratesAFileSystemThatCannotSyncADirectory`, `TestGetXattrReadsAnAttributeThatGrowsBetweenTheCalls`, and `internal/codingagent` `TestStateFilesGetPiModeUnderUmask`; `TestReplaceKeepingBackupLeavesTheFileWhereBothKeepTheirNames`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupFinishesAReplacementThatCouldNotMoveTheReplacement`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupRestoresTheOriginalWhenItCannotFinish`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupKeepsBothFilesWhenNothingCanBeMoved`, `TestReplaceKeepingBackupRemovesTheBackupAfterASuccess`, `TestWriteFileKeepsTheTemporaryFileOfAReplacementThatKeptFiles` and `internal/fsretry` `TestTransientWindowsCode` (they run the Windows recovery and retry decisions with injected errors on Linux and macOS). Windows: not verified by run: the retry of ERROR_UNABLE_TO_REMOVE_REPLACED and ERROR_UNABLE_TO_MOVE_REPLACEMENT (`TestTransientRenameClassifiesWindowsErrors` has the cells and runs only in the Windows job), the half-way ReplaceFileW recovery (the tests named above are `!windows` files; the Windows-only tests of `internal/atomicfile`, in `atomicfile_windows_test.go` and `descriptor_matrix_windows_test.go`, include `TestWindowsReplaceKeepingBackupFinishesAReplacementThatStoppedHalfWay` and `TestWindowsReplaceKeepingBackupRestoresTheOriginalWhenItCannotFinish`, and no Windows run is cited for them here) and the Windows directory sync (a no-op there), until a Windows CI run shows them; the three-way table above (lane CredStore3, `fs-before.log`, `fs-stamped.log`, `fs-after.log`, `fs-pi.log`); part C: `internal/pilock` `TestAsyncLockHeldPastTheSyncStaleWindowIsNotTaken` and the table of part C (`hb2-before.log`, `hb2-after.log`, `hb2-pi.log`).

Parity allowance: observable only where a write is interrupted (the old file survives where Pi's is truncated), in the inode of the file after a write, and on Windows in a file another program holds open without delete sharing; no other file content, mode or location differs (a new file's mode under the umask is Pi's). The Windows case has had two runs: the first (run 43699097748) showed the calls above and the second (run 43709055821, on the main-side tip whose internal/atomicfile files this line carries) passed every cell on NTFS; no other file system has been run.

## D652 The compaction line shows the elapsed time and the streamed size of the summary, where Pi shows a bare label

What: Pi shows `Compacting context... (escape to cancel)` for the whole summary request (`status-indicator.ts:88-91`; the key text comes from the keybinding, and `fullscreen/09` asserts it on both sides). PiG shows the same line for the first second, then `Compacting context... 12s (escape to cancel)`, and once the summary streams `Compacting context... 1m 12s · ~14k tokens (escape to cancel)`. The auto-compaction and overflow labels (`Auto-compacting...`, `Context overflow detected, Auto-compacting...`) get the same suffix. The branch-summary indicator does not.

The size is the characters of text and thinking streamed by the request in flight, divided by 4 and rounded up, shown through the footer's token formatter (1.0k to 9.9k, then whole thousands). It restarts at 0 for each request of one compaction (a history chunk of D167, the turn prefix, a retry). The elapsed time spans the whole compaction. In a narrow terminal the elapsed time and the size give way before the cancel hint does: when the full line does not fit the editor border the line falls back to the elapsed time and the hint, then to Pi's line.

No event is added. RPC, print and JSON output are unchanged. The request bytes are unchanged.

Why: a summary of a long session streams for minutes to tens of minutes behind a spinner that looks hung (owner report, row 175). Measured: the provider's stream is the time; PiG's own work is tens of milliseconds. The size is an estimate of streamed output, not the provider's token count; the label says `~`.

Pi bug: none; Pi's bare label is correct, only silent.

Scope: `internal/compactprogress` (the counter), `ProviderCompleter` (counts stream deltas only when the context carries a counter; returns what it returned before), `Session.compact` and `runAutoCompaction`, the interactive status tick (`tickCompactionMessage`, which sets the existing `Loader.SetMessage` only when the text changed). One additive exported accessor: `coding.Session.CompactionStreamedTokens() int64`, the streamed size in tokens of the compaction in flight (0 when none runs); no `tui` type gains a member.

Owner decision: owner approved 2026-11-15 (OWNER-DECISIONS.md 2026-11-15T00:39:25Z); the narrow-terminal sentence, which narrows the difference toward Pi, approved as written at 2026-11-15T01:30:38Z.
By owner principle (decision 29, 2026-11-17), checked by Claude and Tern

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-17 text-sha256:ef0bf15871fd2338

Remove when: Pi shows progress for a compaction, or the owner withdraws the display.

Call-site markers: `internal/compactprogress/progress.go` (`Progress`), `internal/codingagent/compaction/provider_completer.go` (`CompleteSimple`), `coding/session.go` (`CompactionStreamedTokens`, `withCompactionProgress`), `internal/codingagent/session_handle.go` (`InteractiveSessionHandle.CompactionStreamedTokens`), `internal/codingagent/interactive_status.go` (`showCompactionStatusIndicator`, `compactionStatusMessage`, `tickCompactionMessage`), `internal/codingagent/interactive_layout.go` (`tickStatusIndicators`), `internal/codingagent/interactive_events.go` (`CompactionEndEvent`).

Evidence: `internal/codingagent/interactive_compaction_progress_test.go` (`TestCompactionLineShowsElapsedTimeAndStreamedTokens`, `TestCompactionEscapeDuringTheStreamLeavesNothingRunning`), `internal/codingagent/interactive_compaction_progress_unit_test.go` (`TestCompactionStatusMessage`, `TestTickCompactionMessageFollowsClockAndCounter`, `TestTickCompactionMessageChangesNothingWithinASecond`, `TestTickCompactionMessageFollowsTheWidth`, `TestCompactionLineInANarrowTerminal`, `TestCompactionProgressAllocations`), `internal/codingagent/compaction/provider_completer_progress_test.go` (`TestProviderCompleterProgressChangesNeitherRequestNorResult`).

Parity allowance: the compaction line's text after the first second. The settled screen, the session entry and the request bytes are Pi's.

At 40 columns both show Pi's line cut at the border: RUN 2026-11-15, `make parity-family FAMILY=fullscreen` (20 scenarios, rc 0), scenario `fullscreen/09b-main-screen-compaction-40-columns` against the Pi oracle, log `~/lanes/CompactDisplay.out/p40.log`; the capture of both is equal after the spinner glyph, and it carries no digit.

## D657 Every handler of an event gets its own copy of the payload, in every SDK; Pi's handlers of one emit share one event object

What: Pi calls every handler of an `emit` or an `emitToolCall` with the same event object (`runner.ts:1103-1110`, `1256-1262`: `handler(event, ctx)` in the loop over `snapshotEventHandlers`); `emitBoundary` builds a fresh shallow event for each handler (`runner.ts:1043-1060`, the event at `1053-1058`) whose `context` and `message` are the session's live objects; `emitContext` makes one `structuredClone` of the messages (`runner.ts:1312-1322`, the clone at `1314`) and every handler of the dispatch reads and edits that one copy. A handler's write to nested event data (a message in `event.messages`, a member of `event.context`, a block of `event.content`) can therefore reach the next handler, and at a boundary the session. In PiG a handler gets the payload from its own request: the host sends one request for each registered handler (`handler_id`), and each SDK hands the handler what it decoded for that request. A write to the payload reaches no other handler of the extension, no other extension and not the host, except what the two paragraphs after the table (`What is not different` and `What an assigned member carries`) state, and a handler that keeps the payload and writes it again after it returned (a Go or Python thread, a Node timer) writes into a copy nobody reads. What a handler returns is carried as in Pi.

The set is the same in every SDK. The conformance row `TestConformance_EventHandlersDoNotShareTheirPayload` (`test/extension-conformance/event_isolation_test.go`) runs two handlers of one extension on a `turn_end`, a `context` and a `context_with_system` payload through `Runner.Emit` (which hands every handler its own copy and does not chain; a session dispatches `context` and `context_with_system` through `EmitContext`, which chains, so the claim of this row is true of `Runner.Emit` only; the session's behaviour is `TestConformance_ContextEditsInASessionReachTheNextHandler`) in the isolated Go, Node, Rust, Python and Lisp realizations, the Node packed cell, the fused Go realization and the packed Go, Rust, Python and Lisp cells, and requires each handler to receive the original. How each realization copies:

| Realization | Two handlers of one extension | Two extensions in one packed cell or one process | Two extensions in separate processes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Go, isolated or packed | a copy: `extensions/sdk/extension.go:1696`, `unmarshalFrameObject(req.Args)` decodes each request's frame (a streamed payload: `event_stream.go:62`, `cloneStreamValue(root)` of the replica the connection keeps) | a copy each: every member has its own connection and its own frame | a copy each: a frame cannot be shared |
| Go, fused (`LoadInProcess`, D31) | a copy: `extension.go:1690-1697`, `fusedEventArgs.decodeWithMaster` decodes a payload once for the process and returns `cloneStreamValueOrdered` of the master (`event_args_memo.go:90-91`) | a copy each from the one master | n/a |
| Node, isolated or packed | a copy: one request frame for each `handler_id`, `request.args` at `runtime.mjs:5602`; the message objects of a message event are shared between the handlers of a process (`shareEventMessages`, `runtime.mjs:5603`), which is D541 | a copy each (a frame per member); message objects shared in the process, D541 | a copy each |
| Rust | a copy: `event_stream.rs:48`, `req.args = Some(root.clone())` (a streamed payload; a frame that carries `args` is decoded for the request) | a copy each | a copy each |
| Python | a copy: `pig_sdk/__init__.py:5979`, `req["args"] = _clone_stream_value(root)` (streamed payload; a frame with `args` is decoded for the request) | a copy each | a copy each |
| Lisp | a copy: `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/dispatch.lisp:206`, `(clone-stream-value root)` (streamed payload; a frame with `args` is decoded for the request) | a copy each | a copy each |
| Go, in-process (`inproc.Runner`: an extension that is a Go function in the host's own process) | NOT a copy: `coding/extension/host/inproc/runner.go:3005`, `callHandler(handler, event, dispatchCtx)` passes the one event to every handler, as Pi does | the same one event | n/a |

Across processes isolation is forced by the transport. Within one extension it is not: a frame decoded for each request is a copy by construction, but the streamed path of every SDK clones the replica it keeps, and the fused Go memo clones a shared master, where handing the handler the retained object would be Pi's sharing. Those are choices, made so that the realizations of one extension agree.

What is not different: the edits a handler's write carries back to the next handler and to the host, for the message of `message_start`, `message_update`, `message_end`, `turn_end` and `agent_end`, are governed by D541 (its table and its tests: three Rust rows differ, owner decisions 17 and 18 of 2026-11-17); this record adds no list to it. What D541 does not cover is the other events. A handler's in-place edit is carried as Pi's shared object carries it for these, in every SDK (Go, Node, Python, Rust, Lisp): the messages of `context` and `context_with_system` (a message edited, an element pushed: `TestConformance_InPlaceContextEditsFollowPi` on every isolated and packed realization, and `TestConformance_ContextEditsInASessionReachTheNextHandler`; Lisp pushes nothing in place because its arrays are fixed-size vectors); the `input` of `tool_call` (`TestToolCallInputMutationAcrossSDKs`, every isolated realization); the content and details of `tool_result` (`TestConformance_InPlaceToolResultEditsFollowPi`, every isolated and packed realization); the prompt options and system prompt of `before_agent_start` (`TestBeforeAgentStartChainSeesEveryEarlierEditAcrossSDKs`, `TestPromptOptionEditsAcrossSDKs`, the five SDKs, including across extensions); the payload of `before_provider_request` (`TestConformance_InPlaceProviderHookEditsReachTheRequest`, every isolated and packed realization); the `entries` of `turn_end` (`TestTurnBoundaryMetadataMutationAndPreviewAcrossSDKs`, every isolated realization). That the NEXT EXTENSION reads such an edit is shown for Node (`TestNodeInPlaceEditsReachTheNextExtension`: tool_call, tool_result, before_provider_request, turn_end, context removal and swap), Python (context push, `TestConformance_ContextListEditsInASessionReachTheNextExtension`) and, for before_agent_start, all five SDKs; the claim is made for no other SDK. What is not carried: the nested data of the `context` of a `turn_end` (contextMessages, llmMessages, contextEntries) and the event of every other event name; an in-place write there reaches neither the next handler nor the session (`TestEventHandlersOfOneDispatchShareTheirPayloadInPiAndNotInPiG` runs Pi and PiG on a `turn_end`; `TestConformance_EventHandlersDoNotShareTheirPayload`); a handler returns the change. Replacing the member itself (assigning a new list or object to `event.messages`, `event.input`, `event.entries`) is not an in-place edit, and the next paragraph states what each SDK does with it. In Pi the next handler reads an assigned member only where the handlers share one event object (`tool_call`, `runner.ts:1256-1262`); no Pi runner reads an assigned member back from the event (`emitContext` takes a list from the handler's result or an in-place edit, `runner.ts:1318-1329`; `emitBoundary` takes `entries` from the handler's result, `runner.ts:1062-1063`).

What an assigned member carries: a handler can assign a new value to a member of the event (`event.input = {...}`, `event.messages = [...]`, `event.entries = [...]`) instead of editing the old value in place. In Pi: for `tool_call` every handler gets the one event, so the next handler reads the assigned input, and the tool runs with the args object the agent loop handed the event, which the assignment does not touch (`runner.ts:1256-1262`; `agent-loop.ts:737-743` gives `validatedArgs` to `beforeToolCall`, `778` prepares the call with the same object, `844` runs the tool with it); for `context`, `emitContext` makes a fresh event for each handler from its own list (`runner.ts:1318-1322`) and takes a list only from the handler's returned `messages` or from an in-place edit of the list it handed (`runner.ts:1325-1329`), so an assigned `event.messages` is lost; for `turn_end`, `emitBoundary` builds a fresh event for each handler (`runner.ts:1053-1058`) and takes `entries` only from the handler's returned value (`runner.ts:1062-1063`), so an assigned `event.entries` is lost. In PiG the SDKs differ from Pi, and from each other, in two places. First, `context` and `turn_end`: the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs read the member again after the handler ran, so an assigned list is what the next handler reads, what the model request is built from (`context`) and what the session stores (`turn_end`); the Node runtime reads the list it handed the handler, so an assigned list is lost, as in Pi. Second, `tool_call`: a Rust handler holds the event as one `serde_json::Value`, so assigning `event["input"]` and editing it in place are the same edit: the tool runs with the input the handler left, and the next handler reads it too, where Node, Go, Python and Lisp give an assigned input to the next handlers only and the tool keeps the input it carried, as in Pi. Rust has done this in every release since v0.5.0, the first with a `tool_call` input reply; it is a limit of the Rust SDK's single `Value` (it cannot tell an assignment from an edit), and following Pi would need the author to say "replace" explicitly. Released behaviour (the tags `v0.5.0` to `v0.5.3` and `v0.7.0`; there is no `v0.6.0`, it was never released): the Go SDK carried an assigned `context` list and `turn_end` entries in every release since v0.2.0, the Rust SDK carried the context list since v0.3.0 and `turn_end` entries since v0.2.0, the Lisp SDK carried `turn_end` entries since v0.7.0, its first release; the Python SDK carried neither list in any release, and the Lisp SDK did not carry a context list: both carry them from this release line (0.8.0). Where each SDK does it:

| Realization | `tool_call` assigned `input`: the next handler / the tool | `context` assigned `messages`: the next handler / the request | `turn_end` assigned `entries`: the next handler / the session |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pi (`runner.ts`, `agent-loop.ts`) | the assigned input / the object the call carried (`1256-1262`, `agent-loop.ts:844`) | the list it was handed / that list (`1318-1329`) | the entries it was handed / those entries (`1062-1063`) |
| Node, isolated or packed | the assigned input / the object the call carried: `runtime.mjs:5743-5745` sends `_pigToolCallEventInput` apart from the edit | not carried: `runtime.mjs:5854` keeps the list the handler was handed and `5905` replies with it | not carried: `runtime.mjs:5713` keeps the list the handler was handed, `5726` replies with it |
| Go, isolated, packed or fused | the assigned input / the object the call carried: `extension.go:1548`, `1823` (`toolCallInput.assigned`) | carried: `context_event.go:26` reads `data["messages"]` after the handler (called at `extension.go:1797`); the request holds the list | carried: `event_edits_reply.go:163` reads `data["entries"]` after the handler (called at `extension.go:1837`); host `host.go:5203-5206` sets the event's `Entries` |
| Python | the assigned input / the object the call carried: `pig_sdk/__init__.py:6698-6699` | carried: `__init__.py:6845-6846` reads `data.get("messages")` after the handler; new in this release line | carried: `__init__.py:6673` reads `data.get("entries")` after the handler; new in this release line |
| Rust | the next handler and the tool both get what the handler left: `extension.rs:3704-3710` replies `_pigToolCallInput` when the JSON text of `data["input"]` differs from the one received | carried: `extension.rs:3658` falls back to `data.get("messages")` after the handler | carried: `extension.rs:3767` replies `data.get("entries")` after the handler |
| Lisp | the assigned input / the object the call carried: `dispatch.lisp:329` | carried: `dispatch.lisp:449` falls back to `(jget data "messages")` after the handler; new in this release line | carried: `dispatch.lisp:314` replies `(jget data "entries")` after the handler (since v0.7.0) |
| Go, in-process (`inproc.Runner`) | not covered by `TestAssignedEventMembersFollowPi` (no row); not verified | not covered; not verified | not covered; not verified |

The Rust sentence above and the table state PiG's behaviour on the batch tip (`6c30f3d653`), not Pi's.

Audience: an extension author who ASSIGNS the list in a handler (`event.messages = [...]` of a `context` handler, `event.entries = [...]` of a `turn_end` handler) instead of returning it or editing it in place. Under Pi and the Node runtime the assignment is ignored: the next handler and the request or session read the list they were handed. Under the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs it is carried. An author who edits the list in place or returns `{messages}` sees no difference in any SDK. A Python extension (both lists) or a Lisp extension (the `context` list) that assigns one behaves differently in 0.7.0 and in 0.8.0.

Why: the transport gives each handler a decoded copy of a frame, and a runtime that keeps one object per payload would have to hand every handler the same object across a thread, a process and a language boundary. Every realization that can share does not, so that an extension behaves the same in the isolated, the packed and the fused load, whichever the author or the Piglet Binary chooses (D31, D18), and so that a handler that writes after it returned (a goroutine, a timer) cannot change what another handler or a later dispatch reads. The fused path pays one decode and one clone for each handler (`TestFusedDispatchAllocations`). For an assigned member the reason is how each SDK builds its reply: a native SDK builds it from the event value as the handler left it, so an assigned list and an in-place edit look alike there (the SDKs were written one at a time and no row compared them before `TestAssignedEventMembersFollowPi`); the Node runtime builds it from the list it handed the handler, which is Pi's behaviour. The owner ruled on 2026-11-18 (below) that 0.8.0 ships the carrying and that the next breaking SDK release removes it. Making the SDKs follow Pi now would break released behaviour of the Go and Rust SDKs (both lists) and of the Lisp SDK (entries).

Pi bug: none; sharing the event is Pi's design.

Scope: the nested data of an event payload written in place by a handler, read by another handler of the same dispatch (of the same or another extension) or by the host afterwards, for an event that D541 does not cover, in the Go, Node, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs in every load mode; and a member a handler assigns in place of editing it (`input` of `tool_call`, `messages` of `context`, `entries` of `turn_end`), in the same SDKs and load modes. Not: the in-process Go runner (`inproc.Runner`), which shares as Pi does; the message objects of a message event, D541; what a handler returns.

Owner decision: 2026-11-17, decision 28: "D31 per-handler event copies ... become their own numbered divergence; behaviour kept." Numbered as its own divergence; behaviour kept.

Owner decision: 2026-11-18, owner Michael Kinsy approved the coordinator's recommendation "A for 0.8.0, C at the next breaking SDK release" with the words "proceed with recommendations". The recommendation was made on the three ways to stand for an assigned `context` list and `turn_end` entries list (A: Go, Python, Rust and Lisp carry the assignment, as merged on the batch; B: revert the Python and Lisp half; C: no SDK carries it, as Pi and the Node runtime). 0.8.0 ships A: the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs carry an assigned `event.messages` and `event.entries` (Python's two lists and Lisp's `context` list are new on this release line). The next breaking SDK release adopts C: no SDK carries an assigned list, and the Go and Rust authors are told so in that release's notes.

SCRUTINIZED:approved 2026-11-18 text-sha256:aeb17c2431d6d3a5

Remove when: the SDKs of every language hand the handlers of one dispatch one event object (a handle API for the payload) so that a write reaches the next handler as it does in Pi; the copy then goes from `eventArgsMemo`, the replica clones and the per-request decode of the shared object. For an assigned member: the next breaking SDK release adopts C (owner decision of 2026-11-18): the Go, Python, Rust and Lisp SDKs stop carrying an assigned `event.messages` and `event.entries` (they keep the list they handed the handler, as Pi and the Node runtime do), the Go and Rust authors are told in that release's notes, and the Rust SDK can tell an assignment of `event.input` from an edit.

Call-site markers (`pig divergence (D657): ...`, in each language's comment syntax): `extensions/sdk/extension.go` (the fused and non-fused decode of `req.Args`), `extensions/sdk/event_args_memo.go` (`decodeWithMaster`), `extensions/sdk/event_stream.go` (`cloneStreamValue(root)`), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/event_stream.rs` (`req.args = Some(root.clone())`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` (`_apply_event_stream`), `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/dispatch.lisp` (`apply-event-stream`), `coding/extension/host/subprocess/runtime-node/runtime.mjs` (the `event` case), and, where it does NOT copy, `coding/extension/host/inproc/runner.go` (`Runner.Emit`).

Evidence: `TestConformance_EventHandlersDoNotShareTheirPayload` (`test/extension-conformance/event_isolation_test.go`; two handlers of one extension in subprocess Go, Node, Node packed, Rust, Python, Lisp, fused Go and the packed Go, Rust, Python and Lisp cells; not the in-process Go runner and not two extensions), `TestFusedDispatchAllocations`, `TestOutboundFramesAreTheEncodersBytes` (the frames are the encoder's bytes). For assigned members (`test/extension-conformance/assigned_event_members_test.go`, on the batch, not on this branch): `TestAssignedEventMembersFollowPi`, 33 subtests (`tool_call`, `context`, `turn_end` in `subprocess-go`, `subprocess-node`, `subprocess-node-packed`, `subprocess-rust`, `subprocess-python`, `subprocess-lisp`, `fused-go` and the packed Go, Rust, Python and Lisp cells; no in-process Go row), of which 20 expect something Pi does not do: `context` and `turn_end` in the nine native rows (the carried list), and `tool_call` in `subprocess-rust` and `subprocess-rust-packed`; `TestAssignedContextListIsValidatedLikeAReturnedOne` (the nine native rows: a bad assigned list is answered as the same value returned); `TestAssignedEventMembersMatchPi` (Pi's own runner over the three assignments: the next handler reads the assigned input and keeps the list or entries it was handed).

Parity allowance: no scenario compares a nested write of one handler read by another handler of the same dispatch: Pi's tests (`packages/coding-agent/test/extensions-runner.test.ts`) check what a handler returns and the `entries` a boundary handler pushes (`keeps shared mutations made before a handler throws`, line 1063; D541's field), not a second handler's read of a nested payload the first wrote; the conformance row states PiG's behavior, not Pi's. A test that fails when Pi changes (a pinned-Pi run in which the second handler of an `emit` reads the first's write) is owed.

## D658 The Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs invoke handlers in request arrival order, but a descheduled thread can run its handler's first statement after a later request's

What: Pi runs a handler of an event, a command or a tool call synchronously up to its first `await` in the order the requests came: `runner.ts:1110` is `await handler(event, ctx)` with no await before the first handler, and a parallel batch invokes each call in source order (`agent-loop.ts:656-658`). The Node runtime serves frames on one thread and keeps that. The Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs serve each request on a thread of its own. Each reserves a start place for a `tool_call`, `event` or `command` request in arrival order and invokes the handler only after every earlier request's handler was invoked or its request ended without one. That fixes the order of INVOCATION. It does not fix what a handler runs first: a thread descheduled between the gate's release and its handler's first statement can be overtaken by the thread of the next request, so a handler's own first-statement log can read [c2 c1] where Pi and Node never read it. Go: measured on 2026-11-18, about 5e-5 per start under one-cpu pressure, 0 in 600 unpressured runs; the gate arrived in v0.5.2 (before it, event and command requests had no gate). Rust: by reading, not measured: `extensions/sdk-rs/src/request_start_order.rs` states the limit itself ("a thread that the operating system preempts between its hand-off and its handler's first statement can still be overtaken, and no Rust construct closes that window"). Python: the same hand-off by the code's order (`_RequestStart.begin` in `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` sets the place's event, then the request's thread calls the handler), measured on 2026-11-18 by lane `PySdkStartOrder` (base `3b5e51db29`, CPython 3.12.3, GIL on, switch interval 5 ms, xd670; pressure is `taskset -c 0` plus 8 busy loops on that cpu). The invocation order was the arrival order in all 2,400 rounds of a probe. The first-statement order is load-sensitive: the three tests of `test_tool_dispatch.py` (`tool_call` 16 x 24, event and command 18 x 24, the late request thread) held in 900 of 900 runs free and 900 of 900 under pressure; the conformance cell `TestToolBatchStartsInSourceOrderAcrossSDKs/subprocess-python` 100 of 100 free and 100 of 100 under pressure; the two first-statement rows of `TestOrderingRowsAcrossSDKs`, which do not apply to Python today, forced on for Python in a scratch build (-count=100): free 100 of 100 in both cells, under pressure `subprocess-python-packed` 100 of 100 and `subprocess-python` 99 of 100, the one red in row `tool-result-handlers-start-in-request-order`, round 0: got `[order-0-7 order-0-6 order-0-1 ...]`, want `[order-0-6 order-0-7 order-0-1 ...]` (two adjacent handlers swapped; plain handlers, no yield); a probe with a yield as the handler's first statement lost the first-statement order in 64 to 200 of 200 rounds depending on the yield and the load, and 0 of 200 without a yield. By reading, not measured: CPython switches threads at a blocking call or when the 5 ms interval expires, so a handler that does not block usually keeps the interpreter from the hand-off to its first statement, and a thread descheduled at the interval boundary can still lose it. Lisp: by reading, not run: `ticket-begin` in `dispatch.lisp` hands the place on before the handler runs, so the same window exists by the code's order.

Audience: an extension author whose handlers of two requests that arrive together (two `tool_call`s of a parallel batch, two commands, two events) record or act on their FIRST statement and depend on the order, and who runs under CPU pressure. A Node extension, and Pi, never see it.

Why: the order Pi keeps is a property of one thread running each handler to its first await; a runtime with one thread per request cannot hand the place on at the handler's first statement, because it does not know where that is. It is not made faithful now: holding the place until the handler's first host call was tried and rejected, because it stalls every later handler behind a handler that never calls the host and can deadlock when that handler waits for a later request.

Pi bug: none.

Scope: the Go, Rust, Python and Lisp SDKs, `tool_call`, `event` and `command` requests, in every load mode (isolated, packed, fused Go); the rate is measured for Go (about 5e-5 per start under one-cpu pressure) and for Python (one red in 100 pressured runs of a first-statement row forced on for it; none in 1,000 pressured runs of its tests and its strict cell), by reading for Rust and Lisp. Not: D187, which holds a batch call's first host call until the earlier calls wrote theirs (a different place; this record is about the handler's first statement); the order of invocation, which is kept (`TestEventAndCommandHandlersStartInArrivalOrder`, `TestToolCallHandlersStartInArrivalOrder`, `TestOvertakenHandlerKeepsItsInvocationPlace`); Node.

Owner decision: 2026-11-18, owner Michael Kinsy approved the coordinator's recommendation "Start-order limit: give it a numbered record, covering Go, Rust and Lisp" with the words "proceed with recommendations"; on the same day, shown that the Python SDK has the same hand-off by reading, he chose "Cover Go, Rust, Python and Lisp. Python and Lisp are marked "by reading, not measured"; Pi and Node never reorder." (the coordinator's form, option text quoted). Python was measured afterwards (What). The wording of this record awaits the owner's read.

SCRUTINIZED:approved

Remove when: an SDK can invoke the handler and hand the place on in one step the scheduler cannot split, or a start place that is held until the handler's first host call without stalling later handlers or deadlocking.

Call-site markers (`pig divergence (D658): ...`): `extensions/sdk/extension.go` (`Extension.passStart`), `extensions/sdk-rs/src/request_start_order.rs` (`RequestStart::begin`, called at the begin sites of `extensions/sdk-rs/src/extension.rs`), `extensions/sdk-py/pig_sdk/__init__.py` (`_RequestStart.begin`), `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/dispatch.lisp` (`ticket-begin`).

Evidence: `extensions/sdk` `TestOvertakenHandlerKeepsItsInvocationPlace`, `TestEventAndCommandHandlersStartInArrivalOrder` (`extensions/sdk/request_start_order_test.go`) and `TestToolCallHandlersStartInArrivalOrder` (`extensions/sdk/tool_prepare_arguments_test.go`); Python `test_tool_call_handlers_start_in_arrival_order`, `test_a_request_thread_that_runs_late_does_not_let_a_later_handler_start_first` (invocation order; none asserts first-statement order, and none failed in the 900 pressured runs of What); the conformance helpers `startOrderStrict` (`test/extension-conformance/tool_dispatch_test.go`, true for Node and Python, used by `TestToolBatchStartsInSourceOrderAcrossSDKs`, whose Python cell passed 100 of 100 under pressure) and `firstStatementOrder` (`test/extension-conformance/ordering_test.go`, Node only, used by two ordering rows), which between them leave the first-statement order of Go, Rust and Lisp unpinned; the cure that made the invocation order hold, merged on the batch as `3fe84706cc`; Rust `extensions/sdk-rs/src/request_start_order.rs`; Lisp `extensions/sdk-lisp/src/dispatch.lisp`. The Go rate: about 5e-5 per start under one-cpu pressure and 0 in 600 unpressured runs (measured 2026-11-18). The Python figures are those of What (lane `PySdkStartOrder`, batch `3b5e51db29`, 2026-11-18).

Parity allowance: no parity scenario observes it: a scenario's extension handlers are written to read in order, and the window is a scheduler race of microseconds that no scenario can pin; the conformance helpers above say which realizations are held to the strict first-statement order.
